*   The paper doesn't explore the *evolution* of skip-connection importance across differing ViT architectures and pre-training methodologies. Why is the effect more pronounced now than in earlier ViT variants? The *modern* qualifier lacks sufficient elaboration.
*   The paper mentions the importance of scaling laws, but does not explore how token graying affects the required compute for certain ViT performance levels.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a useful empirical discovery supported by adequate theoretical analysis. While the theoretical component hinges on simplification, it builds a plausible argument for skip-connection importance to attention. The proposed TG method is practically sound, but it provides only modest improvements in performance. The biggest limitation is the lack of discussion as to when skip connections are most/least valuable, leading to the question of what ViT properties make this effect more extreme. Overall, this paper gives strong initial insight, but lacks the exploration required to make it a seminal piece in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG

### Restoring Calibration for Aligned Large Language Models: A Calibration-Aware Fine-Tuning Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01997v1)
- **Authors**: Jiancong Xiao, Bojian Hou, Zhanliang Wang, Ruochen Jin, Qi Long, Weijie J. Su, Li Shen
- **Abstract**: One of the key technologies for the success of Large Language Models (LLMs) is preference alignment. However, a notable side effect of preference alignment is poor calibration: while the pre-trained models are typically well-calibrated, LLMs tend to become poorly calibrated after alignment with human preferences. In this paper, we investigate why preference alignment affects calibration and how to address this issue. For the first question, we observe that the preference collapse issue in alignment undesirably generalizes to the calibration scenario, causing LLMs to exhibit overconfidence and poor calibration. To address this, we demonstrate the importance of fine-tuning with domain-specific knowledge to alleviate the overconfidence issue. To further analyze whether this affects the model's performance, we categorize models into two regimes: calibratable and non-calibratable, defined by bounds of Expected Calibration Error (ECE). In the calibratable regime, we propose a calibration-aware fine-tuning approach to achieve proper calibration without compromising LLMs' performance. However, as models are further fine-tuned for better performance, they enter the non-calibratable regime. For this case, we develop an EM-algorithm-based ECE regularization for the fine-tuning loss to maintain low calibration error. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of poor calibration in large language models (LLMs) after they have been aligned to human preferences using techniques like Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). The authors observe that preference alignment often leads to overconfidence and inaccurate probability estimates. The paper investigates why this happens, identifying a generalization of the "preference collapse" issue to the calibration scenario. To mitigate this, the authors propose a calibration-aware fine-tuning (CFT) approach, especially emphasizing the importance of domain-specific knowledge. They categorize models into calibratable and non-calibratable regimes, developing different fine-tuning strategies for each: standard fine-tuning (SFT) in the calibratable regime and an EM-algorithm-based Expected Calibration Error (ECE) regularization for the non-calibratable regime. Experiments across multiple LLMs and datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods in restoring calibration without sacrificing performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a solid, though not groundbreaking, contribution in terms of novelty.  Here's a breakdown:

*   **Identifying the issue:** The observation that preference alignment degrades calibration is not entirely new (as the authors themselves acknowledge), but the paper provides a more systematic investigation of this phenomenon across different models and alignment methods. Linking preference collapse to calibration is a useful contribution.
*   **Calibratable/Non-Calibratable Regimes:**  The distinction between calibratable and non-calibratable regimes, while intuitive, provides a useful framework for thinking about the problem. This is a moderately novel perspective and provides a structured approach.
*   **CFT and Regularized CFT:** The proposed fine-tuning approaches are incremental improvements. CFT is largely a standard SFT approach with a focus on domain-specific data, and the Regularized CFT adds an ECE regularization term using an EM algorithm. While the EM based approach for regularization is technically sound, it's not radically different from other regularization strategies.
*    **EM Algorithm**: The use of the EM algorithm for the non-calibratable regime, while providing a principled way to deal with ECE regularization, may face challenges in scalability and convergence especially when fine-tuning large models.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper is moderate.

*   **Practical Impact:** The problem of poor calibration in LLMs is a significant one, as it can lead to unreliable decision-making. The paper's methods provide practical tools to improve calibration, which is valuable. Better calibration is beneficial for real-world applications where accurate uncertainty estimates are crucial (e.g., medical diagnosis, legal reasoning).
*   **Theoretical Contributions:** The theoretical framework with the calibratable/non-calibratable regimes offers some insights, but isn't a major theoretical breakthrough.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes thorough empirical validation on multiple LLMs and datasets, which strengthens the claims and demonstrates the generalizability of the proposed methods.

**Strengths:**

*   Systematic investigation of a practical problem.
*   Clear framework for categorizing models and applying different solutions.
*   Strong empirical results across multiple models and datasets.
*   Well-written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The novelty is incremental, with the proposed methods being largely based on existing techniques.
*    The use of EM-Algorithm for non-calibratable regime adds complexity and may face challenges in scalability.
*   The theoretical framework, while useful, is not deeply insightful.
*   The paper could explore the computational costs associated with different strategies more explicitly.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses an important practical problem and provides useful methods for improving calibration in LLMs. However, the novelty of the technical contributions is incremental rather than revolutionary. The "calibratable/non-calibratable" regime framework is valuable, and the empirical validation is thorough. However, the methods used are largely based on standard fine-tuning and regularization techniques. Considering these aspects, a score of 6 is appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Safer Pretraining: Analyzing and Filtering Harmful Content in Webscale datasets for Responsible LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02009v1)
- **Authors**: Sai Krishna Mendu, Harish Yenala, Aditi Gulati, Shanu Kumar, Parag Agrawal
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have become integral to various real-world applications, leveraging massive, web-sourced datasets like Common Crawl, C4, and FineWeb for pretraining. While these datasets provide linguistic data essential for high-quality natural language generation, they often contain harmful content, such as hate speech, misinformation, and biased narratives. Training LLMs on such unfiltered data risks perpetuating toxic behaviors, spreading misinformation, and amplifying societal biases which can undermine trust in LLM-driven applications and raise ethical concerns about their use. This paper presents a large-scale analysis of inappropriate content across these datasets, offering a comprehensive taxonomy that categorizes harmful webpages into Topical and Toxic based on their intent. We also introduce a prompt evaluation dataset, a high-accuracy Topical and Toxic Prompt (TTP), and a transformer-based model (HarmFormer) for content filtering. Additionally, we create a new multi-harm open-ended toxicity benchmark (HAVOC) and provide crucial insights into how models respond to adversarial toxic inputs. Upon publishing, we will also opensource our model signal on the entire C4 dataset. Our work offers insights into ensuring safer LLM pretraining and serves as a resource for Responsible AI (RAI) compliance.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the crucial issue of harmful content in web-scale datasets used for pretraining large language models (LLMs). It introduces a three-dimensional taxonomy (Safe, Topical, Toxic) to categorize web content more precisely than binary approaches, capturing intent and severity across five harm categories (Hate & Violence, Ideological Harm, Sexual Harm, Self-Inflicted Harm, and Illegal Activity Harm). To operationalize this, the authors develop TTP-Eval, a high-accuracy Topical and Toxic Prompt, and HarmFormer, a Transformer-based model for content filtering. They also present HAVOC, a new multi-harm toxicity benchmark, and analyze how LLMs respond to adversarial toxic inputs. The paper includes a large-scale analysis of harmful content in Common Crawl, C4, and FineWeb datasets and plans to open-source the HarmFormer model signals on the entire C4 dataset. The goal is to provide resources and insights for safer LLM pretraining and Responsible AI (RAI) compliance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers several novel contributions:

    *   **Three-Dimensional Taxonomy:** The Safe/Topical/Toxic taxonomy is a significant improvement over existing binary classification approaches. This allows for nuanced content moderation, preserving contextually important information while filtering out harmful content. This is valuable because it acknowledges that some content may contain harmful keywords but ultimately serve an educational or informational purpose, which needs to be retained for model competence.
    *   **HarmFormer and TTP-Eval:** The development of a transformer model specifically for long-form content moderation and the creation of a benchmark dataset (TTP-Eval) are valuable resources for the community.
    *   **HAVOC Benchmark:** The introduction of a multidimensional, multi-harm benchmark (HAVOC) for evaluating LLM safety goes beyond existing benchmarks like RealToxicityPrompts (RTP) by considering a wider range of harms and focusing on intent-aware evaluation. Analyzing a Model's ability to resist adversarial inputs also contributes to understanding safety measures.
    *   **Web-Scale Analysis:** Performing a systematic audit of pretraining datasets provides crucial insights into the prevalence and distribution of different types of harmful content. The analysis of Common Crawl, C4, and FineWeb reveals the limitations of current filtering practices and highlights areas for improvement.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its contribution to safer and more responsible LLM development. By providing a more nuanced framework for content moderation, the authors help mitigate the risks of perpetuating toxic behaviors, spreading misinformation, and amplifying societal biases in LLM outputs. This is particularly important for applications where LLMs have a direct impact on human well-being and decision-making. The tools, datasets, and analyses provided in this paper can be valuable resources for researchers and practitioners working on RAI compliance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper offers a holistic approach to the problem of harmful content in LLMs, addressing taxonomy, dataset analysis, content filtering, and safety evaluation.
    *   **Practical Resources:** The development of TTP-Eval, HarmFormer, and HAVOC provides tangible resources that can be used by the community.
    *   **Large-Scale Analysis:** The large-scale analysis of web datasets offers valuable insights into the distribution of harmful content.
    *   **Intent-Aware Moderation:** The emphasis on intent-aware moderation is a key strength of the proposed taxonomy and methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **English Language Focus:** The focus on English language data limits the generalizability of the findings and tools to other languages.
    *   **Subjectivity of Taxonomy:** While the taxonomy provides a more nuanced approach, there is still some inherent subjectivity in categorizing content as Safe, Topical, or Toxic, particularly across diverse cultural contexts.
    *   **Computational Limitations:** Due to computational constraints, the HAVOC benchmark is limited to a relatively small size. Expanding this benchmark in the future would improve its representativeness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting more nuanced and responsible content moderation practices for LLM pretraining. The tools, datasets, and analyses provided in this paper can be valuable resources for researchers and practitioners working on RAI compliance.

**Overall:**

The paper addresses a timely and important problem with several novel contributions. It offers a more nuanced framework for content moderation, provides practical resources for filtering and evaluation, and offers valuable insights into the prevalence of harmful content in web-scale datasets. It is a solid contribution to the community.

### CASA: CNN Autoencoder-based Score Attention for Efficient Multivariate Long-term Time-series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02011v1)
- **Authors**: Minhyuk Lee, HyeKyung Yoon, MyungJoo Kang
- **Abstract**: Multivariate long-term time series forecasting is critical for applications such as weather prediction, and traffic analysis. In addition, the implementation of Transformer variants has improved prediction accuracy. Following these variants, different input data process approaches also enhanced the field, such as tokenization techniques including point-wise, channel-wise, and patch-wise tokenization. However, previous studies still have limitations in time complexity, computational resources, and cross-dimensional interactions. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel CNN Autoencoder-based Score Attention mechanism (CASA), which can be introduced in diverse Transformers model-agnosticically by reducing memory and leading to improvement in model performance. Experiments on eight real-world datasets validate that CASA decreases computational resources by up to 77.7%, accelerates inference by 44.0%, and achieves state-of-the-art performance, ranking first in 87.5% of evaluated metrics.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces CASA (CNN Autoencoder-based Score Attention), a novel attention mechanism for multivariate long-term time series forecasting (LTSF). It aims to address the limitations of traditional Transformer-based models, such as high computational complexity, large memory footprint, and insufficient cross-dimensional interaction capture. CASA replaces the self-attention mechanism with a CNN autoencoder to approximate the QKT matrix, thereby reducing memory usage, improving computational speed, and enhancing prediction performance. The authors demonstrate that CASA can be integrated into various Transformer architectures agnostically of tokenization method, and they showcase its superior performance on eight real-world datasets, achieving state-of-the-art results.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's contribution lies in its proposed CASA module as an alternative to the standard self-attention mechanism. The core idea of using a CNN autoencoder to approximate the attention score calculation and reduce computational burden is reasonably novel. It attempts to balance computational efficiency with performance, a crucial consideration for LTSF. The results showing reduced memory and inference time are compelling. The integration of CASA into different Transformer variants, regardless of tokenization method, is another merit of the paper.

However, several aspects warrant critical consideration:

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While CASA presents a distinct approach, the fundamental building blocks (CNNs, autoencoders, attention) are well-established. The paper needs to more strongly emphasize *how* their particular combination and application to the attention problem in LTSF is unique and provides specific advantages beyond existing compression or approximation techniques for attention. It needs to differentiate itself from prior work combining CNNs and attention mechanisms.
*   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical explanation in Section 3.3 and the accompanying proofs could be stronger. The claims that the existing self-attention mechanism does not sufficiently consider cross-dimensional information are not convincingly supported.
*   **Ablation Studies and Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper could benefit from more thorough ablation studies to justify the specific architecture and hyperparameters of the CNN autoencoder. An assessment of the sensitivity of CASA's performance to these choices is important for establishing its robustness.
*   **Comparison with more recent baselines:** While the baselines used are relevant, there are many newer models in the LTSF area which address speed and accuracy tradeoffs, especially in the recent ICLR and NeurIPS conferences. A wider comparative analysis would improve the argument that it is state-of-the-art.
*   **Reproducibility concerns:** Without hyperparameter configurations for the baselines and detailed description of how CASA was combined with the baseline models, independent verification of the experiments might be problematic. The appendix D mentioned is still high level.

Despite these limitations, the empirical results are impressive and suggest that CASA is a promising approach for efficient and accurate LTSF. The reduction in computational resources is particularly valuable in scenarios where resources are constrained. The model's generalizability across different datasets is also a positive aspect.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a reasonably novel and potentially significant approach to improve the efficiency of Transformers for LTSF. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance and reduced resource consumption. However, the theoretical justification could be strengthened, additional ablation studies would be helpful, and comparison to newer baselines is needed to truly demonstrate its superiority. The incremental nature of the technical contribution compared to pre-existing techniques combining attention with CNNs and/or dimensionality reduction warrants a degree of caution in ascribing high levels of significance. The reproducibility also needs to be enhanced for broader adoption.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Testing Database Systems with Large Language Model Synthesized Fragments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02012v1)
- **Authors**: Suyang Zhong, Manuel Rigger
- **Abstract**: Various automated testing approaches have been proposed for Database Management Systems (DBMSs). Many such approaches generate pairs of equivalent queries to identify bugs that cause DBMSs to compute incorrect results, and have found hundreds of bugs in mature, widely used DBMSs. Most of these approaches are based on manually written SQL generators; however, their bug-finding capabilities remain constrained by the limited set of SQL features supported by the generators. In this work, we propose ShQveL, an approach that augments existing SQL test-case generators by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to synthesize SQL fragments. Our key idea is to systematically incorporate SQL features gained through automated interactions with LLMs into the SQL generators, increasing the features covered while efficiently generating test cases. Specifically, ShQveL uses SQL sketches -- SQL statements with incomplete code segments that LLMs fill -- to integrate LLM-generated content into the generator. We evaluated ShQveL on 5 DBMSs and discovered 55 unique and previously unknown bugs, 50 of which were promptly fixed after our reports.
- **Summary**: The paper "Testing Database Systems with Large Language Model Synthesized Fragments" introduces ShQveL, a novel approach that augments existing SQL test-case generators with Large Language Models (LLMs) to synthesize SQL fragments, aiming to increase the feature coverage and bug-finding capabilities of automated DBMS testing. ShQveL systematically incorporates SQL features gained through interactions with LLMs into SQL generators using SQL sketches – SQL statements with incomplete code segments that LLMs fill. The approach was evaluated on five DBMSs, discovering 55 unique and previously unknown bugs. The paper claims that this method achieves comparable or improved code coverage compared to existing tools while significantly reducing the manual effort required to incorporate new features into the testing process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel combination of techniques. While LLMs have been used in data management and testing before, their specific application to augment existing SQL generators through the concept of SQL sketches is a distinctive contribution. This approach addresses a crucial problem in DBMS testing: the high manual effort required to maintain and extend test case generators for diverse SQL dialects. The idea of SQL sketches offers a structured and efficient way to integrate LLM-generated content into existing generators.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to automate and improve the testing of DBMSs. Discovering 55 unique bugs, with 50 promptly fixed, clearly demonstrates the practical value of ShQveL. The ability to scale automated DBMS testing to more systems without extensive manual effort is an important step forward. The cost-effectiveness of the approach (less than $1 USD per DBMS for learning) also enhances its appeal.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined solution.
    *   Effective use of LLMs to augment existing SQL generators instead of replacing them entirely, resulting in a good balance of efficiency and coverage.
    *   The concept of SQL sketching provides a structured interface between LLM-generated fragments and the generator's code.
    *   Empirical evaluation on multiple DBMSs, demonstrating the practical applicability of the approach.
    *   Demonstrated ability to find real bugs that were acknowledged and fixed by developers.
    *   Ablation studies shed light on the contributions of different components of ShQveL.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation could have included more direct comparisons with manually-written generators, even if the stated goal wasn't to outperform them. It would provide more insight into how much feature coverage this method is gaining.
    *   The paper mentions using Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) but could benefit from a deeper discussion on the performance of the system without RAG. It's useful to know the contribution of this design choice.
    *   The authors mention "internal schema model" and SQLancer's reliance on it, but the paper would benefit from a better explanation of why LLMs cannot incorporate schema changes.
    *   The "feature learning costs" could be more precisely justified by quantifying the complexity of features learned for each USD spent.

*   **Impact and Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field of DBMS testing by providing a more scalable and automated approach. It opens the door for further research into using LLMs for test case generation and augmentation, potentially leading to more robust and reliable DBMSs. The concept of SQL sketching could be adapted to other domains where automated code generation is needed.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid and novel approach to DBMS testing, addressing a practical problem with a clear and effective solution. While some aspects of the evaluation could be strengthened, the discovery of real bugs and the demonstration of cost-effectiveness make this a valuable contribution. The innovative use of SQL sketching has the potential to influence future research in automated code generation and testing. The limitations prevent a higher score, but the paper's overall impact is significant and warrants a high rating.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### MLLM-Enhanced Face Forgery Detection: A Vision-Language Fusion Solution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02013v1)
- **Authors**: Siran Peng, Zipei Wang, Li Gao, Xiangyu Zhu, Tianshuo Zhang, Ajian Liu, Haoyuan Zhang, Zhen Lei
- **Abstract**: Reliable face forgery detection algorithms are crucial for countering the growing threat of deepfake-driven disinformation. Previous research has demonstrated the potential of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in identifying manipulated faces. However, existing methods typically depend on either the Large Language Model (LLM) alone or an external detector to generate classification results, which often leads to sub-optimal integration of visual and textual modalities. In this paper, we propose VLF-FFD, a novel Vision-Language Fusion solution for MLLM-enhanced Face Forgery Detection. Our key contributions are twofold. First, we present EFF++, a frame-level, explainability-driven extension of the widely used FaceForensics++ (FF++) dataset. In EFF++, each manipulated video frame is paired with a textual annotation that describes both the forgery artifacts and the specific manipulation technique applied, enabling more effective and informative MLLM training. Second, we design a Vision-Language Fusion Network (VLF-Net) that promotes bidirectional interaction between visual and textual features, supported by a three-stage training pipeline to fully leverage its potential. VLF-FFD achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance in both cross-dataset and intra-dataset evaluations, underscoring its exceptional effectiveness in face forgery detection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VLF-FFD, a Vision-Language Fusion framework for face forgery detection, designed to leverage Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) more effectively. The key contributions include: 1) EFF++, a new frame-level, explainability-driven extension of the FaceForensics++ (FF++) dataset, enriched with detailed textual annotations describing forgery artifacts and manipulation techniques. 2) VLF-Net, a novel architecture that incorporates an external detector, an MLLM, and a fusion module to facilitate bidirectional interaction between visual and textual features.  A three-stage training pipeline is proposed to optimize the network.  The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on both cross-dataset and intra-dataset evaluations, showcasing improved effectiveness in face forgery detection. Ablation studies are performed to validate the design choices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a few novel aspects. The EFF++ dataset, with frame-level annotations explaining both artifacts and manipulation techniques, is a valuable contribution. The VLF-Net architecture, specifically the Vision-Language Fusion Module (VLFM) and its training strategy, introduces a new approach to combining external detectors and MLLMs.  The bidirectional fusion is a significant improvement over methods relying on just LLM predictions or external detector predictions.

*   **Significance:** The problem of face forgery detection is highly relevant due to the potential for misuse of deepfake technology. The improvements in performance shown by VLF-FFD over existing methods suggest that this approach could contribute to more robust and reliable detection systems. The explainability aspect is also crucial for building trust in the system and understanding its decisions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its components.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, including both cross-dataset and intra-dataset evaluations, as well as detailed ablation studies.
    *   The ablation studies effectively isolate the impact of each component of the proposed framework (EFF++, external detector, MLLM integration, VLFM), providing strong support for the design choices.
    *   The visualization of attention maps alongside explanatory outputs is a compelling way to demonstrate the alignment between visual and textual modalities.
    * The introduction of a novel three-stage training pipeline is crucial in the overall improvement in face forgery detection using MLLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a commercial LLM (Gemini) for generating annotations in EFF++ introduces a potential dependency and reproducibility concern. Although the prompts are provided, future access to the exact model and its responses cannot be guaranteed. This dependency limits reproducibility.
    *   The computational cost and complexity of the proposed approach are not explicitly addressed. While the experiments were run on powerful GPUs, the practical applicability of VLF-FFD in resource-constrained environments is unclear.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on frame-level detection. While this is a valid starting point, many real-world deepfakes exhibit inconsistencies that can only be detected by analyzing temporal information. The lack of video-level analysis is a limitation.
    *   While providing good results, the paper does not focus on efficiency or resource-constraints of the developed pipeline.

*   **Potential Influence:**  VLF-FFD has the potential to influence future research in face forgery detection by promoting the use of more sophisticated vision-language fusion techniques and by highlighting the importance of explainability. The EFF++ dataset could serve as a valuable resource for training and evaluating future models.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of face forgery detection by introducing a novel and effective framework for vision-language fusion. The gains in performance, coupled with the explainability aspects and the creation of EFF++, warrant a positive assessment.  However, the reliance on a commercial LLM, the lack of video-level analysis, resource constraints, and lack of focus on efficiency, slightly temper the overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Wide & Deep Learning for Node Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02020v1)
- **Authors**: Yancheng Chen, Wenguo Yang, Zhipeng Jiang
- **Abstract**: Wide & Deep, a simple yet effective learning architecture for recommendation systems developed by Google, has had a significant impact in both academia and industry due to its combination of the memorization ability of generalized linear models and the generalization ability of deep models. Graph convolutional networks (GCNs) remain dominant in node classification tasks; however, recent studies have highlighted issues such as heterophily and expressiveness, which focus on graph structure while seemingly neglecting the potential role of node features. In this paper, we propose a flexible framework GCNIII, which leverages the Wide & Deep architecture and incorporates three techniques: Intersect memory, Initial residual and Identity mapping. We provide comprehensive empirical evidence showing that GCNIII can more effectively balance the trade-off between over-fitting and over-generalization on various semi- and full- supervised tasks. Additionally, we explore the use of large language models (LLMs) for node feature engineering to enhance the performance of GCNIII in cross-domain node classification tasks. Our implementation is available at https://github.com/CYCUCAS/GCNIII.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GCNIII, a graph convolutional network architecture that draws inspiration from the "Wide & Deep" learning approach popularized for recommendation systems. The core idea is to combine the memorization capabilities of linear models ("Wide" component) with the generalization power of deep GCNs ("Deep" component). GCNIII incorporates three main techniques: Intersect memory, Initial residual, and Identity mapping.  The authors present empirical results demonstrating that GCNIII can effectively balance overfitting and over-generalization in node classification tasks.  Furthermore, they explore the use of large language models (LLMs) for node feature engineering to improve performance in cross-domain node classification scenarios. They identify and provide an explanation for the over-generalization problem.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of applying Wide & Deep learning to GCNs is interesting. However, the technical contributions (Intersect memory, Initial residual, and Identity mapping) are essentially adaptations or combinations of existing techniques. Initial residual connection has been used in GCNII. Intersect memory is simply applying a graph convolution (adjacency matrix) to the output of the "wide" component, which is not substantially innovative. While the combination is potentially useful, each individual element lacks groundbreaking novelty. The paper also studies how a deep GCN can produce over-generalization effects, and proposes the importance of initial residual, which is a novel finding and exploration.

*   **Significance:** The empirical results are a strength of the paper. The authors provide comprehensive experiments showing that GCNIII achieves state-of-the-art performance on several benchmark datasets. The investigation into using LLMs for feature engineering is also relevant and shows promise for cross-domain applications. The work is likely to be of interest to researchers working on graph neural networks and node classification, specifically for building more robust and accurate models. By providing an explanation to the over-generalization effects, the paper provides guidance for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical evaluation with comparisons to competitive baselines.
    *   Addresses the practical problem of balancing overfitting and over-generalization in deep GCNs.
    *   Exploration of LLMs for feature engineering is a relevant and potentially impactful direction.
    *   Identifies and explains the over-generalization problem.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited novelty in the individual techniques used in GCNIII (they are mostly re-purposed or combined from existing methods).
    *   The justification for Intersect memory could be stronger (it seems somewhat ad-hoc).
    *   While the paper identifies GCNIII is capable of balancing the trade-off between underfitting and overfitting, the paper fails to sufficiently discuss the limitations of GCNIII.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence practical GCN model design, particularly for addressing challenges related to deep model training. The insights into balancing overfitting and over-generalization are valuable and may guide future research in this area.

*   **Rigorous Justification of Techniques:** The techniques are empirically justified but the explanations for why these methods work well in this specific context could be more thorough.

Overall, the paper presents a useful combination of existing techniques to create a competitive GCN architecture. While lacking fundamental innovation in its individual components, the system-level engineering and comprehensive empirical validation make it a valuable contribution to the field. The discovery of over-generalization in deep GCNs also add value to this paper.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Mind to Machine: The Rise of Manus AI as a Fully Autonomous Digital Agent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02024v1)
- **Authors**: Minjie Shen, Qikai Yang
- **Abstract**: Manus AI is a general-purpose AI agent introduced in early 2025, marking a significant advancement in autonomous artificial intelligence. Developed by the Chinese startup Monica.im, Manus is designed to bridge the gap between "mind" and "hand" - combining the reasoning and planning capabilities of large language models with the ability to execute complex, end-to-end tasks that produce tangible outcomes. This paper presents a comprehensive overview of Manus AI, exploring its core technical architecture, diverse applications across sectors such as healthcare, finance, manufacturing, robotics, and gaming, as well as its key strengths, current limitations, and future potential. Positioned as a preview of what lies ahead, Manus AI represents a shift toward intelligent agents that can translate high-level intentions into real-world actions, heralding a new era of human-AI collaboration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Manus AI," a general-purpose AI agent developed by Monica.im. Manus AI is presented as a breakthrough in autonomous AI, capable of not just thinking and planning like a large language model, but also executing complex tasks end-to-end. The paper provides an overview of Manus AI's architecture, applications across various industries (healthcare, finance, manufacturing, robotics, gaming), its strengths and limitations, and its potential future impact. The paper positions Manus AI as a glimpse into a future where intelligent agents revolutionize work and daily life. It emphasizes Manus AI's ability to translate intentions into actionable outcomes, heralding a new era of human-AI collaboration. The paper emphasizes a multi-agent architecture, autonomous task execution, multi-modal understanding, advanced tool use, and continuous learning as key features. Finally, the paper concludes with discussing wider deployment, future use cases, impact, and societal considerations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents Manus AI as a significant advancement in autonomous AI agents. However, several aspects require critical evaluation:

*   **Novelty:** The concept of an autonomous AI agent isn't entirely new. Research on AI agents, particularly those capable of tool use, has been ongoing. The *claimed* novelty lies in the *integrated* approach of Manus AI. The *alleged* seamless fusion of planning, execution, and verification within a multi-agent architecture, coupled with multi-modal capabilities, is presented as a key differentiator. However, it's crucial to understand the *specific* architectural and algorithmic innovations that enable this integration. The paper describes a Planner, Execution, and Verification agent framework but doesn't delve deeply into the specific algorithms and data structures used, making it difficult to fully assess the novelty of their implementation. Furthermore, while the benchmark results are mentioned, those results require outside validation from the broader AI community for a thorough independent validation of the claims.

*   **Significance:** The *potential* significance of Manus AI, if its claims are substantiated, is considerable. A truly autonomous agent capable of operating in diverse domains could transform industries, automate complex workflows, and democratize access to expertise. The wide-ranging application examples (healthcare, finance, robotics, etc.) illustrate this potential. However, the paper acknowledges limitations related to transparency, reliability, data privacy, and ethical control. Addressing these limitations is critical to realizing the promised significance. The societal impact section is well-balanced, acknowledging potential benefits and risks.

*   **Evidence and Validation:** The paper relies heavily on claims and reported benchmark results from the developers of Manus AI (Monica.im). Independent verification of these claims is essential. The limited availability of Manus AI (invite-only beta) further restricts independent assessment. The reliance on anecdotal evidence, without detailed technical specifics, limits the paper's ability to be rigorously evaluated. The paper would be substantially strengthened by concrete examples that can be used in a controlled environment that can be independently verified.

*   **Clarity and Depth:** While the paper provides a broad overview, it lacks deep technical detail. Specific algorithms, training methodologies, and implementation details of the multi-agent architecture are not fully explained. This makes it difficult to assess the actual technical contributions and to compare Manus AI with existing approaches. A more technical deep-dive would strengthen its value to the research community.

*   **Future prospects:** The discussion around the potential evolution of Manus AI is reasonable, highlighting areas such as expanded tool integrations, enhanced multi-modal perception and adaptation.

**Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper describes an interesting concept with significant *potential* impact if the claims hold true. Manus AI's supposed ability to independently think, plan and execute end-to-end tasks, crossing industries, sets it apart. A key strength is identifying its potential benefits alongside its limitations, highlighting aspects that need further exploration, such as transparency, ethics, privacy, and reliability. But due to the lack of sufficient detail regarding its technical design and the scarcity of independently verifiable results, it prevents the paper from achieving a higher score. The current score reflects the *potential* impact but also acknowledges the need for more rigorous technical details and independent validation. A score above a 7 would require demonstrable evidence beyond self-reported performance. This is reflected in the score assigned above.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Regression s all you need for medical image translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02048v1)
- **Authors**: Sebastian Rassmann, David Kügler, Christian Ewert, Martin Reuter
- **Abstract**: The acquisition of information-rich images within a limited time budget is crucial in medical imaging. Medical image translation (MIT) can help enhance and supplement existing datasets by generating synthetic images from acquired data. While Generative Adversarial Nets (GANs) and Diffusion Models (DMs) have achieved remarkable success in natural image generation, their benefits - creativity and image realism - do not necessarily transfer to medical applications where highly accurate anatomical information is required. In fact, the imitation of acquisition noise or content hallucination hinder clinical utility. Here, we introduce YODA (You Only Denoise once - or Average), a novel 2.5D diffusion-based framework for volumetric MIT. YODA unites diffusion and regression paradigms to produce realistic or noise-free outputs. Furthermore, we propose Expectation-Approximation (ExpA) DM sampling, which draws inspiration from MRI signal averaging. ExpA-sampling suppresses generated noise and, thus, eliminates noise from biasing the evaluation of image quality. Through extensive experiments on four diverse multi-modal datasets - comprising multi-contrast brain MRI and pelvic MRI-CT - we show that diffusion and regression sampling yield similar results in practice. As such, the computational overhead of diffusion sampling does not provide systematic benefits in medical information translation. Building on these insights, we demonstrate that YODA outperforms several state-of-the-art GAN and DM methods. Notably, YODA-generated images are shown to be interchangeable with, or even superior to, physical acquisitions for several downstream tasks. Our findings challenge the presumed advantages of DMs in MIT and pave the way for the practical application of MIT in medical imaging.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Regression is all you need for medical image translation":

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the prevailing notion that Diffusion Models (DMs) are inherently superior to simpler Regression Models (RMs) for Medical Image Translation (MIT). The authors introduce YODA (You Only Denoise Once or Average), a 2.5D diffusion-based framework that can perform both DM-like and RM-like sampling. They also propose Expectation-Approximation (ExpA) sampling to mitigate noise in generated images. Through experiments on multiple medical imaging datasets (brain MRI and pelvic MRI-CT), they demonstrate that DMs and RMs can achieve comparable performance in practice and that the perceived advantages of DMs, such as higher perceptual quality, often stem from replicating noise rather than faithfully translating medical information. They show that YODA outperforms several state-of-the-art GAN and DM methods and that YODA-generated images are often interchangeable with, or even superior to, physically acquired images for downstream tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core idea of systematically comparing DMs and RMs for MIT, especially emphasizing the role of noise, is valuable and provides a fresh perspective on the field.
    *   The YODA framework, while based on existing diffusion techniques, offers a practical implementation that allows for direct comparison between DM and RM paradigms within the same architecture. The 2.5D approach is a pragmatic choice for dealing with volumetric data while maintaining computational tractability.
    *   The ExpA sampling method is a useful technique for noise reduction and facilitates a fairer comparison between different generation strategies.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's findings have significant implications for the MIT community, suggesting that the computational overhead of DMs may not always be justified in medical applications where accurate anatomical information is paramount.
    *   The demonstration that simpler RMs can achieve comparable or even better performance challenges the assumed superiority of DMs and encourages a re-evaluation of model selection criteria.
    *   The emphasis on preserving medical information rather than simply generating realistic-looking images is crucial for clinical utility.
    *   The paper provides a strong argument for minimizing image distortion and facilitating downstream analyses by noise suppression, which is a shift in focus from simply maximizing perceptual quality.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across multiple datasets and downstream tasks, providing strong evidence for their claims.
    *   **Clear Articulation of the Problem:** The authors clearly identify the problem of noise replication in DMs and its impact on image quality evaluation and clinical utility.
    *   **Practical Contributions:** The YODA framework and ExpA sampling method are valuable tools for MIT research and development.
    *   **Good Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide a solid understanding of the individual contributions of key components of the methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The specific datasets used are limited. It would have strengthened the argument if other imaging modalities were used.
    *   While the argument on the adverse effects of noise replication is strong, it could have provided deeper analysis of the exact types of noise being replicated and their specific impact on clinical diagnosis.
    *   The claims about YODA's superiority are strong but may be contingent on the datasets used. Some datasets display minimal differences when compared to each other.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research in MIT by encouraging a more critical evaluation of model selection and a greater focus on preserving medical information rather than simply replicating noise.
    *   The YODA framework could serve as a valuable tool for benchmarking different MIT algorithms and exploring the trade-offs between realism, accuracy, and computational efficiency.

*   **Score Rationale:**

    The paper presents a well-argued, comprehensively evaluated, and practically relevant contribution to the field of medical image translation. While the techniques build upon existing diffusion models, the systematic comparison with regression models, the emphasis on noise, and the focus on downstream clinical tasks are novel and important.  The paper successfully challenges the uncritical application of diffusion models and provides a compelling case for simpler, more efficient regression-based approaches. While there are limitations in the scope of datasets and in the depth of noise analysis, the paper's insights and practical contributions warrant a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 8/10

### RTV-Bench: Benchmarking MLLM Continuous Perception, Understanding and Reasoning through Real-Time Video
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02064v1)
- **Authors**: Shuhang Xun, Sicheng Tao, Jungang Li, Yibo Shi, Zhixin Lin, Zhanhui Zhu, Yibo Yan, Hanqian Li, Linghao Zhang, Shikang Wang, Yixin Liu, Hanbo Zhang, Xuming Hu, Ying Ma
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) increasingly excel at perception, understanding, and reasoning. However, current benchmarks inadequately evaluate their ability to perform these tasks continuously in dynamic, real-world environments. To bridge this gap, we introduce RTV-Bench, a fine-grained benchmark for MLLM real-time video analysis. RTV-Bench uses three key principles: (1) Multi-Timestamp Question Answering (MTQA), where answers evolve with scene changes; (2) Hierarchical Question Structure, combining basic and advanced queries; and (3) Multi-dimensional Evaluation, assessing the ability of continuous perception, understanding, and reasoning. RTV-Bench contains 552 diverse videos (167.2 hours) and 4,631 high-quality QA pairs. We evaluated leading MLLMs, including proprietary (GPT-4o, Gemini 2.0), open-source offline (Qwen2.5-VL, VideoLLaMA3), and open-source real-time (VITA-1.5, InternLM-XComposer2.5-OmniLive) models. Experiment results show open-source real-time models largely outperform offline ones but still trail top proprietary models. Our analysis also reveals that larger model size or higher frame sampling rates do not significantly boost RTV-Bench performance, sometimes causing slight decreases. This underscores the need for better model architectures optimized for video stream processing and long sequences to advance real-time video analysis with MLLMs. Our benchmark toolkit is available at: https://github.com/LJungang/RTV-Bench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RTV-Bench: Benchmarking MLLM Continuous Perception, Understanding and Reasoning through Real-Time Video":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RTV-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the continuous perception, understanding, and reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in real-time video analysis.  RTV-Bench distinguishes itself through three key features: (1) Multi-Timestamp Question Answering (MTQA), where answers evolve with scene changes; (2) a Hierarchical Question Structure, combining basic and advanced queries; and (3) Multi-dimensional Evaluation, assessing continuous perception, understanding, and reasoning abilities across eight fine-grained dimensions. The benchmark comprises 552 diverse videos and 4,631 high-quality QA pairs.  The authors evaluate several leading MLLMs, including proprietary, open-source offline, and open-source real-time models. The results reveal significant bottlenecks for existing MLLMs in real-time video analysis and underscore the need for optimized model architectures for video stream processing and long sequences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects.  Existing video benchmarks often focus on offline analysis or simplified real-time evaluations. RTV-Bench's MTQA approach, revisiting the same question conceptually over time and requiring models to track evolving states, is a genuine contribution. The hierarchical question structure is a smart way to probe for deeper reasoning ability. While some previous benchmarks touch on long-form video understanding, RTV-Bench's emphasis on *continuous* understanding and reaction to dynamic events sets it apart.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in addressing a critical gap in evaluating MLLMs. The ability to analyze real-time video is crucial for applications like autonomous driving, surveillance, and human-robot interaction.  By highlighting the limitations of current MLLMs in this domain, RTV-Bench provides valuable insights for future research.  The benchmark and the detailed evaluation metrics provide a standardized way to compare and improve models specifically for continuous video understanding.  The observation that simply scaling up models or frame rates doesn't necessarily improve performance is a valuable negative result, steering research towards more efficient architectural designs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of continuous real-time video understanding and the inadequacies of existing benchmarks.
    *   **Innovative benchmark design:** The MTQA and hierarchical question structure are well-motivated and effectively test the target capabilities.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper evaluates a good range of models and provides detailed analysis across multiple dimensions.
    *   **Actionable insights:** The results point to specific bottlenecks and future research directions (e.g., specialized architectures for video processing, better temporal modeling).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Modalities:** The benchmark primarily focuses on the visual modality, which limits its scope. Including audio or other sensory information would make it more comprehensive.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** While the paper mentions data sources, more details on the specific types of scenes and activities included in the 552 videos would be beneficial. A deeper discussion of potential biases within the dataset is warranted.
    *   **Evaluation of Proprietary Models:** Although valuable to include, the lack of details regarding GPT-4o and Gemini 2.0 makes the comparison slightly less impactful for researchers working on open-source alternatives. The results cannot be independently verified and are subject to change.
    *   **Limited negative control experiments**: It would have been beneficial to have negative control experiments in addition to Qwen2.5-VL’s scale, input frame rates and input frame counts.
    *   **Limited discussion**: the discussion around current limitations is minimal and a summary of the findings should be present.

*   **Potential Influence:** The benchmark is likely to be adopted by researchers in the field, leading to further advancements in MLLM architectures and training techniques for real-time video analysis.  It could also spur the development of new metrics and evaluation methodologies for assessing continuous understanding.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7.5**.

The paper makes a solid contribution by introducing a new benchmark that addresses a critical gap in evaluating MLLMs for real-time video understanding. The innovative benchmark design and comprehensive evaluation provide valuable insights for the field. However, the limited modality, lack of dataset details, and some limitations of evaluation of the proprietary models prevent a higher score. Future work addressing these limitations would significantly enhance the paper's impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Retrieval-augmented in-context learning for multimodal large language models in disease classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02087v1)
- **Authors**: Zaifu Zhan, Shuang Zhou, Xiaoshan Zhou, Yongkang Xiao, Jun Wang, Jiawen Deng, He Zhu, Yu Hou, Rui Zhang
- **Abstract**: Objectives: We aim to dynamically retrieve informative demonstrations, enhancing in-context learning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for disease classification. Methods: We propose a Retrieval-Augmented In-Context Learning (RAICL) framework, which integrates retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and in-context learning (ICL) to adaptively select demonstrations with similar disease patterns, enabling more effective ICL in MLLMs. Specifically, RAICL examines embeddings from diverse encoders, including ResNet, BERT, BioBERT, and ClinicalBERT, to retrieve appropriate demonstrations, and constructs conversational prompts optimized for ICL. We evaluated the framework on two real-world multi-modal datasets (TCGA and IU Chest X-ray), assessing its performance across multiple MLLMs (Qwen, Llava, Gemma), embedding strategies, similarity metrics, and varying numbers of demonstrations. Results: RAICL consistently improved classification performance. Accuracy increased from 0.7854 to 0.8368 on TCGA and from 0.7924 to 0.8658 on IU Chest X-ray. Multi-modal inputs outperformed single-modal ones, with text-only inputs being stronger than images alone. The richness of information embedded in each modality will determine which embedding model can be used to get better results. Few-shot experiments showed that increasing the number of retrieved examples further enhanced performance. Across different similarity metrics, Euclidean distance achieved the highest accuracy while cosine similarity yielded better macro-F1 scores. RAICL demonstrated consistent improvements across various MLLMs, confirming its robustness and versatility. Conclusions: RAICL provides an efficient and scalable approach to enhance in-context learning in MLLMs for multimodal disease classification.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Retrieval-Augmented In-Context Learning (RAICL), a framework that enhances multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for disease classification. RAICL leverages retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to dynamically select informative demonstrations relevant to the input query and integrates these demonstrations into the in-context learning (ICL) process. The framework examines embeddings from various encoders (ResNet, BERT, BioBERT, ClinicalBERT) to retrieve appropriate examples and constructs optimized conversational prompts for ICL.  The authors evaluate RAICL on TCGA (cancer genomics) and IU Chest X-ray datasets, assessing its performance across multiple MLLMs (Qwen, Llava, Gemma), different embedding strategies, similarity metrics, and varying numbers of demonstrations. The results show consistent improvements in classification performance compared to standard ICL, particularly with increased examples, suggesting the utility of RAG in this domain. The authors conclude that RAICL provides an efficient and scalable approach for multimodal disease classification with MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitation of standard ICL in MLLMs, which is the difficulty in selecting relevant demonstrations given the constraint on context length and the variability in real-world medical data.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The integration of RAG with ICL (RAICL) in the context of MLLMs for disease classification is a reasonable and logical extension of existing techniques. The authors propose a practical framework to address a specific problem in this domain.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed and thorough.  The authors evaluate RAICL across multiple datasets, MLLMs, embedding strategies, similarity metrics, and few-shot settings. This extensive evaluation provides a robust understanding of the framework's performance.
    *   **Performance Improvement:**  The results consistently show that RAICL improves classification performance compared to the baseline ICL, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The improvements are noteworthy, particularly with larger numbers of demonstration examples.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper provides insights into the impact of different modalities (image vs. text) and embedding strategies on retrieval performance, adding to the understanding of how these components contribute to the overall system.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of RAG and ICL is logical, the novelty is somewhat incremental. RAG and ICL are well-established techniques, and the paper mainly focuses on applying them to a specific problem.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While they mention this, the paper doesn't present concrete figures for computational efficiency.
    *   **Limited Real-World Deployment Considerations:** The paper doesn't deeply address the practical challenges of deploying RAICL in a clinical setting. How would the system handle real-time data streams, handle missing or noisy data, or integrate with existing clinical workflows?

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in its demonstration of how RAG can be effectively used to enhance ICL in MLLMs for disease classification.  This is especially valuable in the medical domain, where labeled data can be scarce and the ability to generalize from few examples is crucial. The framework has the potential to improve the accuracy and efficiency of disease diagnosis and support clinical decision-making. However, wider real-world applicability considerations temper its importance.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, I assign a score of **7** to this paper.  While RAICL is a practical and effective framework with a thorough evaluation, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon well-established techniques. Its practical impact is limited by a lack of real-world deployment details and further details on implementation.  The performance improvements and the insightful analysis contribute to the paper's significance, making it a valuable contribution to the field, but without reaching the highest echelons of novelty and influence.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-OptiRA: LLM-Driven Optimization of Resource Allocation for Non-Convex Problems in Wireless Communications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02091v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyue Peng, Yanming Liu, Yihan Cang, Chaoqun Cao, Ming Chen
- **Abstract**: Solving non-convex resource allocation problems poses significant challenges in wireless communication systems, often beyond the capability of traditional optimization techniques. To address this issue, we propose LLM-OptiRA, the first framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to automatically detect and transform non-convex components into solvable forms, enabling fully automated resolution of non-convex resource allocation problems in wireless communication systems. LLM-OptiRA not only simplifies problem-solving by reducing reliance on expert knowledge, but also integrates error correction and feasibility validation mechanisms to ensure robustness. Experimental results show that LLM-OptiRA achieves an execution rate of 96% and a success rate of 80% on GPT-4, significantly outperforming baseline approaches in complex optimization tasks across diverse scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLM-OptiRA, a framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the solution of non-convex resource allocation problems in wireless communication systems.  LLM-OptiRA automatically detects and transforms non-convex components into solvable forms, reducing the need for expert knowledge. It also includes Error Correction Loops (ECL) and Feasibility Domain Correction (FDC) mechanisms to improve reliability. Experimental results demonstrate that LLM-OptiRA outperforms baseline methods in execution and success rates across various scenarios, particularly when using the GPT-4 model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in applying LLMs to *specifically non-convex* resource allocation problems in wireless communication.  Prior works (cited and acknowledged by the authors) have explored LLMs for convex optimization and some wireless communication scenarios but haven't tackled the complexities of non-convexity in a fully automated and generalizable manner. The inclusion of ECL and FDC as integral parts of the framework to refine solutions and ensure feasibility contributes further to its novelty. The framework seems well organized, incorporating LLM-driven mathematical modeling and code generation with error correction and feasibility validation.

*   **Significance:** Solving non-convex optimization problems in wireless communication is a significant challenge, often requiring specialized knowledge and manual intervention.  If LLM-OptiRA can effectively automate this process, it could have a substantial impact on how wireless networks are designed and managed. It has the potential to simplify optimization tasks, reduce the reliance on expert knowledge, and accelerate the deployment of advanced wireless technologies. Furthermore, The clear and well-documented architecture enables other researchers to explore and extend its capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem is well-motivated, addressing a real-world challenge in wireless communications.
    *   The framework is well-defined, with clear steps and functionalities.
    *   The integration of ECL and FDC is a valuable addition, enhancing the robustness and reliability of the solutions.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to baselines.
    *   The ablation study provides insights into the importance of each component of the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset construction is based on existing problems augmented by LLMs. It might be helpful to provide deeper analysis of the diversity and complexity of the problems within the dataset.
    *   The experiments could benefit from more rigorous comparisons against state-of-the-art *traditional* non-convex optimization algorithms, even if such algorithms require manual tuning.  This would provide a clearer understanding of LLM-OptiRA's competitive advantage.  The paper compares LLM-OptiRA primarily to other LLM-based approaches or naive implementations, which may not highlight its true potential relative to established techniques in the field.
    *   While the paper discusses complexity in general, a deeper analysis of the computational complexity of the LLM-OptiRA framework itself is missing. How does the runtime of the LLM, and each stage of LLM-OptiRA compare to traditional methods?

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in the intersection of LLMs and wireless communication. It sets a baseline for using LLMs in non-convex optimization and provides a practical framework that can be further improved and extended. LLM-OptiRA can contribute to the ongoing development and application of LLMs in the communication engineering domain.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

LLM-OptiRA presents a novel and significant approach to non-convex optimization in wireless communication. The design of LLM-OptiRA and integration of ECL and FDC enhances robustness, but the performance improvements of the framework are well substantiated by the experiments, even though the empirical evaluation could be further strengthened through comparison with traditional methods. The paper, however, is not without its weaknesses, in particular the lack of strong comparison against conventional methods for solving optimization problems. However, I firmly believe that this work will make the work of many communications engineers easier and push the field forward.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### GRAIL: Graph Edit Distance and Node Alignment Using LLM-Generated Code
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02124v1)
- **Authors**: Samidha Verma, Arushi Goyal, Ananya Mathur, Ankit Anand, Sayan Ranu
- **Abstract**: Graph Edit Distance (GED) is a widely used metric for measuring similarity between two graphs. Computing the optimal GED is NP-hard, leading to the development of various neural and non-neural heuristics. While neural methods have achieved improved approximation quality compared to non-neural approaches, they face significant challenges: (1) They require large amounts of ground truth data, which is itself NP-hard to compute. (2) They operate as black boxes, offering limited interpretability. (3) They lack cross-domain generalization, necessitating expensive retraining for each new dataset. We address these limitations with GRAIL, introducing a paradigm shift in this domain. Instead of training a neural model to predict GED, GRAIL employs a novel combination of large language models (LLMs) and automated prompt tuning to generate a program that is used to compute GED. This shift from predicting GED to generating programs imparts various advantages, including end-to-end interpretability and an autonomous self-evolutionary learning mechanism without ground-truth supervision. Extensive experiments on seven datasets confirm that GRAIL not only surpasses state-of-the-art GED approximation methods in prediction quality but also achieves robust cross-domain generalization across diverse graph distributions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GRAIL, a novel approach for approximating Graph Edit Distance (GED) that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) instead of training neural networks directly. Instead of predicting the GED value, GRAIL uses LLMs to generate a program that computes an approximation of GED.  A key innovation is the use of automated prompt tuning, guided by an evolutionary algorithm, to refine the LLM-generated code. This tuning process does *not* require ground truth GED data, circumventing the NP-hard computation of training data needed for traditional neural GED approximators.  The authors demonstrate, through extensive experiments on multiple datasets, that GRAIL achieves state-of-the-art GED approximation quality, exhibits robust cross-domain generalization, and provides end-to-end interpretability due to its code-based output.  The programs generated by GRAIL represent foundational code-based heuristics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits significant novelty in its approach to GED approximation. The shift from *predicting* GED with a trained model to *generating a program* that approximates GED is a paradigm shift. The elimination of the need for NP-hard ground truth GED data through clever prompt tuning and the focus on generating human-interpretable code are also notable contributions. The use of evolutionary algorithms for prompt optimization is not entirely new, but its application in this context, particularly within the constraint of minimizing an *upper bound* on GED, is creative.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to make a substantial impact on the field of graph similarity computation. The achieved approximation quality, coupled with the interpretability and cross-domain generalization capabilities, addresses critical limitations of existing neural GED methods.  The fact that it avoids the need for precomputed GED scores for training data also alleviates a bottleneck.  The approach opens up new avenues for exploring algorithmic solutions to graph similarity problems via code generation. The idea of learning foundational code-based heuristics is also appealing.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Paradigm Shift:** Moving from prediction to code generation.
    *   **Elimination of Ground Truth Data:** A major practical advantage.
    *   **Interpretability:** Making algorithmic decisions transparent.
    *   **Cross-Domain Generalization:** A valuable property that many neural networks lack.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  Consistent state-of-the-art performance across multiple datasets.
    *   **Foundational Heuristics:** Capability of discovering generic algorithms.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLMs:**  The reliance on LLMs introduces potential issues related to cost, availability, and bias.  The performance is fundamentally limited by the capabilities of the LLM used. A more detailed analysis of the sensitivity of GRAIL to different LLMs would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Inference Speed:** While the paper claims comparable or faster inference times for smaller graphs, the reported inference times are higher than neural networks (but achieved with CPU vs GPU). This might be a barrier to adoption in some applications where speed is paramount. More detail on how performance scales with graph size would be valuable. The potential for converting the generated code into a more efficient lower-level language (as discussed in the paper) should be further explored.
    *   **Complexity:** While the core idea is elegant, the implementation involves several components (LLM, evolutionary algorithm, prompt tuning), adding to the complexity of the system. A more thorough ablation study to quantify the contribution of each component would be beneficial.
    *   **Heuristic Limitations:**  The reliance on a sub-optimal selection of mappings is a theoretical limitation. While experiments show good performance, there's a possibility for better performance with a more exhaustive search over possible mappings.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could significantly influence research in graph similarity, leading to:

    *   New approaches for solving combinatorial problems using LLMs and code generation.
    *   Development of more interpretable and generalizable graph algorithms.
    *   Shift away from purely data-driven neural methods towards hybrid approaches that combine machine learning with symbolic reasoning.

* **Justification of Score:**

   GRAIL represents a substantial advancement in the field. The shift from prediction to program generation, combined with a clever unsupervised training method to circumvent the expensive ground-truth computation, addresses significant limitations of existing methods. The cross-domain generalizability, and clear empirical results on a wide range of datasets further strengthens its significance. While it relies on the availability and capability of LLMs, the benefits in terms of interpretability, training data requirements, and generalization are compelling. The weaknesses are not so severe as to diminish the core contribution, but instead indicate areas for future research and improvement.
