### Attention Mechanisms Perspective: Exploring LLM Processing of Graph-Structured Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02130v1)
- **Authors**: Zhong Guan, Likang Wu, Hongke Zhao, Ming He, Jianpin Fan
- **Abstract**: Attention mechanisms are critical to the success of large language models (LLMs), driving significant advancements in multiple fields. However, for graph-structured data, which requires emphasis on topological connections, they fall short compared to message-passing mechanisms on fixed links, such as those employed by Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). This raises a question: ``Does attention fail for graphs in natural language settings?'' Motivated by these observations, we embarked on an empirical study from the perspective of attention mechanisms to explore how LLMs process graph-structured data. The goal is to gain deeper insights into the attention behavior of LLMs over graph structures. We uncovered unique phenomena regarding how LLMs apply attention to graph-structured data and analyzed these findings to improve the modeling of such data by LLMs. The primary findings of our research are: 1) While LLMs can recognize graph data and capture text-node interactions, they struggle to model inter-node relationships within graph structures due to inherent architectural constraints. 2) The attention distribution of LLMs across graph nodes does not align with ideal structural patterns, indicating a failure to adapt to graph topology nuances. 3) Neither fully connected attention nor fixed connectivity is optimal; each has specific limitations in its application scenarios. Instead, intermediate-state attention windows improve LLM training performance and seamlessly transition to fully connected windows during inference. Source code: \href{https://github.com/millioniron/LLM_exploration}{LLM4Exploration}
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) process graph-structured data, focusing on attention mechanisms. Through empirical studies, the authors uncover several phenomena: LLMs struggle to effectively leverage graph connectivity information despite recognizing graph data and node-text interactions. LLMs attention distribution on graph nodes does not align with ideal structural patterns. Intermediate-state attention windows are more effective than either fully connected attention or fixed connectivity during training, and they can be transitioned to fully connected windows during inference. The paper introduces the Global Linkage Horizon (GLH) metric to measure the visibility range between nodes. The authors identify "Attention Sink" and "Skewed Line Sink" issues specific to graph data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of probing LLMs' attention mechanisms specifically for graph data processing is reasonably novel, as is the identification of "Skewed Line Sink" phenomena. Analyzing LLMs in this context, rather than simply treating them as black boxes or applying them directly, is a plus. The introduction of the GLH metric is also a tangible contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights limitations of applying LLMs directly to graph data, providing valuable insights for the graph machine learning community. Identifying specific issues like the mismatch between attention distribution and graph topology, and the existence of "Skewed Line Sink", is helpful in guiding future research toward more effective graph-aware LLMs. The finding regarding intermediate attention windows having advantages during training is also practically significant. The findings on attention sinks and node interactions is a welcome addition to an evolving field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Focus:** The paper emphasizes empirical analysis and experimentation, providing concrete evidence for its claims.
    *   **Specific Findings:** The authors go beyond simply saying "LLMs don't work well on graphs" and pinpoint specific reasons for the challenges.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:** The intermediate attention window observation provides a tangible way to improve LLM training on graph data.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper appears to have a clear experimental methodology.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The number of datasets is relatively small. While valuable, the findings' generalizability might be limited. A wider variety of graph structures and tasks would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper is strong on empirical observation, it lacks a deep theoretical explanation for why certain phenomena occur. A theoretical underpinning could significantly boost the paper's impact.
    *   **Incremental Improvement Only:** While the observations provide useful information for the field, the results provided only provide incremental improvements in the field. More rigorous improvements would be beneficial for the paper's impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the graph machine learning community to focus on developing graph-aware attention mechanisms or adapting existing LLM architectures to better handle graph data. The identified challenges ("Attention Sink", "Skewed Line Sink") could become focal points for future research.

**Overall, this paper provides a valuable empirical investigation into the challenges of using LLMs for graph data processing, with some practical implications for future research. Although limited, the presented observations are insightful and will encourage the field to delve deeper into the inner mechanics of how LLMs are trained on graphical data.**

### Enhancing LLM Code Generation: A Systematic Evaluation of Multi-Agent Collaboration and Runtime Debugging for Improved Accuracy, Reliability, and Latency
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02133v1)
- **Authors**: Nazmus Ashrafi, Salah Bouktif, Mohammed Mediani
- **Abstract**: The use of large language models (LLMs) for automated code generation has emerged as a significant focus within AI research. As these pretrained models continue to evolve, their ability to understand and generate complex code structures has opened new possibilities for automating intricate programming tasks for the sake of accurate code generation. Although contemporary foundational models demonstrate promoting results, researchers continue to explore optimal post-training strategies to enhance code quality. These include supervised fine-tuning, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), debugging, and many others. In this paper, we combine two widely used approaches namely multi-agent collaboration and runtime execution information-based debugging, for improving code generation functionality, reliability, and practical applicability. We perform an empirical study in order to extend the evaluation of the individual strategies as well as the proposed composition of the activities of both strategies. Our study use 19 LLMs to examines the performance of individual and the proposed strategies, offering comprehensive insights into how different programming activities compositions and training paradigms influence code generation effectiveness. In particular, we implement a chained system that combines both strategies to assess their combined impact on functional accuracy, code reliability, and generation latency using two benchmark datasets commonly used for code generation. Our findings provide valuable insights for organizations seeking robust AI-driven coding solutions by guiding them in selecting models that can better adapt to complex post-training strategies, ultimately fostering the adoption of more effective and reliable code generation technologies.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize and critically evaluate the provided research paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Enhancing LLM Code Generation: A Systematic Evaluation of Multi-Agent Collaboration and Runtime Debugging for Improved Accuracy, Reliability, and Latency" explores the impact of combining two post-training strategies to improve code generation using Large Language Models (LLMs): multi-agent collaboration (ACT) and runtime execution information-based debugging (Debugger).  The authors evaluate these strategies individually and in combination across a diverse set of 19 LLMs using the HumanEval and HumanEval+ datasets.  Their experiments analyze functional accuracy, code rigor, and generation latency. The results suggest that while Debugger alone often outperforms multi-agent approaches, a combination of a simple multi-agent workflow (Analyst-Coder) with debugging often leads to an optimal balance of accuracy, rigor, and latency.  The study highlights that the effectiveness of the combined approaches is model-dependent, with OpenAI models generally benefiting from combined strategies and the effectiveness of AСТ being model-dependent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper offers a broad and systematic evaluation across a diverse set of LLMs, which enhances the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The authors employ statistical testing (t-tests) to assess the significance of their results. They also account for trade-offs between execution time and quality.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper provides valuable insights for organizations seeking to deploy LLM-driven coding solutions. The findings help guide the selection of models that are better suited for complex post-training strategies.
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The research questions are clearly articulated, and the experiments are designed to address them effectively.
    *   **Balanced Approach:** The paper evaluates process-oriented (multi-agent) and product-oriented (debugging) approaches and their synergy.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Strategies:** While the combination of ACT and Debugger is a valuable contribution, the individual strategies themselves are not entirely novel, as acknowledged in the introduction and literature review.
    *   **Dataset limitations:** The reliance on HumanEval and HumanEval+ datasets, while standard, raises concerns about real-world applicability since these are contrived datasets.
    *   **Inconsistent model behaviors:** Adding to the argument that some model behaviors are inconsistent and need more thorough investigation.
    *   **Alpha Choice:** In the paper, the alpha level is increased to 0.15 in order to achieve significance, which is relatively high for the current field.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work provides a useful comparative analysis of different post-training strategies for code generation.
    *   The identification of model-specific responses to combined strategies is important for practical deployments.
    *   The analysis of trade-offs between accuracy, rigor, and latency is valuable for real-world applications.
    *   The investigation of the models such as openai, Mistral and etc.

**Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the *combination* of the ACT and Debugging strategies and the systematic investigation of how this combination interacts with different LLM architectures. It's a valuable synthesis of existing ideas, rather than a completely groundbreaking innovation.

**Overall:** The paper is a well-executed empirical study that provides practical insights into optimizing LLM code generation. While the individual strategies are not entirely new, the systematic evaluation and the identification of model-dependent effects make a solid contribution to the field. The statistical justification for the efficacy of the method can be questioned due to the relaxed alpha level. The findings are primarily relevant for practitioners seeking to improve existing LLM-based coding solutions.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's strong empirical contribution and practical relevance, but acknowledges the limited novelty of the individual strategies and potential limitations of its reliance on contrived testing methods, also, the relatively relaxed alpha level contributes to lowering the score. The paper's impact will likely be in informing deployment decisions and guiding further research in this area, rather than fundamentally changing the direction of the field.

### Efficient Multivariate Time Series Forecasting via Calibrated Language Models with Privileged Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02138v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxi Liu, Hao Miao, Qianxiong Xu, Shaowen Zhou, Cheng Long, Yan Zhao, Ziyue Li, Rui Zhao
- **Abstract**: Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) endeavors to predict future observations given historical data, playing a crucial role in time series data management systems. With advancements in large language models (LLMs), recent studies employ textual prompt tuning to infuse the knowledge of LLMs into MTSF. However, the deployment of LLMs often suffers from low efficiency during the inference phase. To address this problem, we introduce TimeKD, an efficient MTSF framework that leverages the calibrated language models and privileged knowledge distillation. TimeKD aims to generate high-quality future representations from the proposed cross-modality teacher model and cultivate an effective student model. The cross-modality teacher model adopts calibrated language models (CLMs) with ground truth prompts, motivated by the paradigm of Learning Under Privileged Information (LUPI). In addition, we design a subtractive cross attention (SCA) mechanism to refine these representations. To cultivate an effective student model, we propose an innovative privileged knowledge distillation (PKD) mechanism including correlation and feature distillation. PKD enables the student to replicate the teacher's behavior while minimizing their output discrepancy. Extensive experiments on real data offer insight into the effectiveness, efficiency, and scalability of the proposed TimeKD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TimeKD, a framework for efficient multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) that leverages calibrated language models (CLMs) and privileged knowledge distillation (PKD). Addressing the high computational cost of directly using large language models (LLMs) for MTSF, TimeKD trains a smaller, efficient student model by distilling knowledge from a cross-modality teacher model. The teacher model utilizes CLMs with ground truth prompts (Learning Under Privileged Information - LUPI) and a subtractive cross-attention (SCA) mechanism to generate high-quality future representations. The student model is then trained using a novel PKD mechanism, incorporating both correlation and feature distillation to mimic the teacher's behavior.  The authors demonstrate through experiments on real-world datasets that TimeKD achieves competitive accuracy with significantly improved inference efficiency compared to direct LLM-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents several novel components. The use of privileged knowledge distillation in conjunction with calibrated language models for MTSF is a relatively new approach. The specific designs of the calibrated attention mechanism within the CLMs and the subtractive cross-attention (SCA) are also novel. Furthermore, the combination of correlation and feature distillation in the PKD framework contributes to the overall novelty.

* **Significance:**  The paper tackles a crucial problem in the application of LLMs to time series forecasting: the high computational cost of inference. The approach of using knowledge distillation to train smaller, more efficient models is well-motivated and practically relevant.  The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in inference speed while maintaining accuracy, which could enable real-time MTSF applications. The demonstration of effectiveness on multiple real-world datasets strengthens the claim of practical significance. The study also includes thorough ablation studies of key components, making it easy to understand the impact of each design choice. Analysis of varying training data also show generalisability. The attention maps visualization allows interpretation.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of directly using LLMs for MTSF and motivates the need for a more efficient approach.
    * **Well-Defined Framework:**  The TimeKD framework is well-defined and explained, with clear descriptions of each component (CLMs, SCA, PKD).
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering multiple datasets, forecasting horizons, and baseline methods.  Ablation studies and visualization help understanding the components' roles.
    * **Strong Results:**  The results demonstrate significant improvements in inference efficiency compared to direct LLM-based approaches, while maintaining competitive accuracy.
    * **Zero-shot ability** The paper tests and shows that it generalizes across training sets
    * **Thorough resource evaluation** The paper performs comprehensive test showing how it performs regarding training and inference time.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The framework involves several complex components (CLMs, SCA, PKD), which might make it difficult to implement and tune in practice. It also raises questions of what the best teacher or student framework is.
    * **LLM specific design:** It should be noted that the paper does heavily rely on LLM knowledge from the teacher model. The advantage of LLMs here is they provide cross-domain knowledge.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of time series forecasting by demonstrating a practical and effective approach for leveraging LLMs while addressing the computational cost limitations. The PKD framework and the specific designs of the CLMs and SCA could serve as building blocks for future research in this area. The results suggest that knowledge distillation is a promising approach for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained MTSF applications.

**Overall Score:**

Given the novelty of the approach, the significant improvements in efficiency, and the thorough experimental evaluation, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field. While the complexity of the framework and limitations should be addressed, the potential for practical impact is high.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring the Potential of Offline RL for Reasoning in LLMs: A Preliminary Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02142v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyu Tian, Sitong Zhao, Haotian Wang, Shuaiting Chen, Yiping Peng, Yunjie Ji, Han Zhao, Xiangang Li
- **Abstract**: Despite significant advances in long-context reasoning by large language models (LLMs), primarily through Online Reinforcement Learning (RL) methods, these approaches incur substantial computational costs and complexity. In contrast, simpler and more economical Offline RL methods remain underexplored. To address this gap, we investigate the effectiveness of Offline RL methods, specifically Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and its length-desensitized variant LD-DPO, in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Extensive experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that these simpler Offline RL methods substantially improve model performance, achieving an average enhancement of 3.3\%, with a particularly notable increase of 10.1\% on the challenging Arena-Hard benchmark. Furthermore, we analyze DPO's sensitivity to output length, emphasizing that increasing reasoning length should align with semantic richness, as indiscriminate lengthening may adversely affect model performance. We provide comprehensive descriptions of our data processing and training methodologies, offering empirical evidence and practical insights for developing more cost-effective Offline RL approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the use of Offline Reinforcement Learning (RL) methods, specifically Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and its length-desensitized variant LD-DPO, to enhance the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors compare these offline RL techniques against more commonly used (and computationally expensive) Online RL approaches. Through experiments on various reasoning benchmarks, they demonstrate that these simpler Offline RL methods can substantially improve model performance, with LD-DPO showing particularly significant gains on the Arena-Hard benchmark. The paper also analyzes DPO's sensitivity to output length, concluding that increasing reasoning length only improves performance when aligned with increased semantic richness. The authors provide details on their data processing and training methodologies, offering insights for developing more cost-effective Offline RL approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the systematic evaluation of *Offline RL* specifically *LD-DPO* for *reasoning* in LLMs. While offline RL and DPO aren't new concepts, their application to reasoning in LLMs and the detailed analysis of length sensitivity is a notable contribution. The work fills a gap by exploring a more economical alternative to Online RL, which dominates current LLM research. The contribution is solid but incremental, building on existing methods. This can also be observed in the comparison of the DPO vs LDDPO models. The impact is more obvious with the LDDPO model since it can provide the same or better results with less complex architectures.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance comes from its potential to make LLM training more accessible and efficient. Online RL methods are known for their computational costs and complexity, making them difficult to replicate and scale. By demonstrating the effectiveness of Offline RL, this paper opens avenues for researchers and practitioners with limited resources to improve LLM reasoning abilities. The detailed data processing and training descriptions contribute to reproducibility. The observed 10.1% improvement on Arena-Hard is encouraging.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly addresses the question of whether Offline RL can effectively enhance reasoning in LLMs.
    *   **Rigorous Experiments:**  The experiments cover multiple reasoning benchmarks, providing a comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:** The paper provides enough details about data processing and training, enhancing reproducibility.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of length sensitivity offers a valuable lesson about the importance of meaningful reasoning steps.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  While significant, the performance gains aren't groundbreaking compared to the gains achieved with much more expansive (and expensive) Online RL methods.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on one model (DeepDistill-32B) and a few Offline RL techniques. Exploring a wider range of models and methods would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Explanation:** The paper provides an empirical analysis but lacks a deeper theoretical explanation of why LD-DPO works.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence researchers to explore Offline RL approaches for LLM training and to pay more attention to the quality of reasoning steps. It also could guide the development of more cost-effective training methodologies.

*   **Rigorous Justification:**

The paper tackles a relevant problem (cost and complexity of LLM training) with a promising, albeit incremental, solution (Offline RL). The empirical results are solid, and the length sensitivity analysis is valuable. The work is not groundbreaking, but it provides a clear and well-executed demonstration of the potential of Offline RL for reasoning in LLMs. Therefore, the contribution is of moderate significance.

### QiMeng-Xpiler: Transcompiling Tensor Programs for Deep Learning Systems with a Neural-Symbolic Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02146v1)
- **Authors**: Shouyang Dong, Yuanbo Wen, Jun Bi, Di Huang, Jiaming Guo, Jianxing Xu, Ruibai Xu, Xinkai Song, Yifan Hao, Xuehai Zhou, Tianshi Chen, Qi Guo, Yunji Chen
- **Abstract**: Heterogeneous deep learning systems (DLS) such as GPUs and ASICs have been widely deployed in industrial data centers, which requires to develop multiple low-level tensor programs for different platforms. An attractive solution to relieve the programming burden is to transcompile the legacy code of one platform to others. However, current transcompilation techniques struggle with either tremendous manual efforts or functional incorrectness, rendering "Write Once, Run Anywhere" of tensor programs an open question. We propose a novel transcompiler, i.e., QiMeng-Xpiler, for automatically translating tensor programs across DLS via both large language models (LLMs) and symbolic program synthesis, i.e., neural-symbolic synthesis. The key insight is leveraging the powerful code generation ability of LLM to make costly search-based symbolic synthesis computationally tractable. Concretely, we propose multiple LLM-assisted compilation passes via pre-defined meta-prompts for program transformation. During each program transformation, efficient symbolic program synthesis is employed to repair incorrect code snippets with a limited scale. To attain high performance, we propose a hierarchical auto-tuning approach to systematically explore both the parameters and sequences of transformation passes. Experiments on 4 DLS with distinct programming interfaces, i.e., Intel DL Boost with VNNI, NVIDIA GPU with CUDA, AMD MI with HIP, and Cambricon MLU with BANG, demonstrate that QiMeng-Xpiler correctly translates different tensor programs at the accuracy of 95% on average, and the performance of translated programs achieves up to 2.0x over vendor-provided manually-optimized libraries. As a result, the programming productivity of DLS is improved by up to 96.0x via transcompiling legacy tensor programs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "QiMeng-Xpiler: Transcompiling Tensor Programs for Deep Learning Systems with a Neural-Symbolic Approach":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces QiMeng-Xpiler, a novel transcompiler designed to automatically translate tensor programs across heterogeneous deep learning systems (DLS). It addresses the challenge of writing and optimizing low-level tensor programs for diverse platforms (GPUs, ASICs, etc.). QiMeng-Xpiler leverages a neural-symbolic approach, combining the code generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs) with the correctness guarantees of symbolic program synthesis. It employs LLM-assisted transformation passes followed by symbolic repair of incorrect code snippets.  A hierarchical auto-tuning strategy further optimizes performance by exploring parameters and pass sequences. The authors demonstrate QiMeng-Xpiler's effectiveness on four DLS (Intel DL Boost, NVIDIA GPU, AMD MI, and Cambricon MLU), achieving high accuracy and performance improvements over vendor-provided libraries, while also significantly boosting programming productivity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the **integrated neural-symbolic approach** for transcompilation of tensor programs for DLS. While LLMs and symbolic synthesis have been explored individually, their combination in a systematic and automated transcompiler for this specific domain is significant. The decomposition of the transcompilation process into multiple LLM-assisted passes, followed by symbolic repair, helps to mitigate the limitations of relying solely on LLMs or symbolic methods. The hierarchical auto-tuning is also a novel element that enhances the overall performance.
*   **Significance:** This work addresses a crucial practical problem: the increasing complexity and burden of programming heterogeneous DLS. The "Write Once, Run Anywhere" paradigm is highly desirable in data centers.  By automating the transcompilation process with guarantees of correctness and competitive performance, QiMeng-Xpiler can significantly reduce development costs and improve resource utilization in deep learning deployments. The reported performance gains of up to 2.0x over manually optimized libraries are impressive. The productivity gains of up to 96x are highly significant, showing considerable improvements.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenges of programming heterogeneous DLS.
    *   **Innovative approach:** The neural-symbolic synthesis effectively combines the strengths of LLMs and symbolic methods.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The experiments cover a diverse range of DLS, operators, and shapes, demonstrating the generality of the approach.
    *   **Quantifiable results:** The performance and accuracy improvements are clearly presented with concrete numbers.
    *   **Productivity gains**: The paper presents clear data on productivity improvement in programming productivity.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4:** The strong performance depends on the capabilities of GPT-4, and there is limited analysis of how the approach would perform with weaker LLMs. The specific prompts and meta-prompts were not fully disclosed due to confidentiality concerns. This somewhat limits the reproducibility of the research.
    *   **Limited disclosure**: There is little information disclosed about the architecture of the LLM. This significantly limits the replicability of this code by others, as one must have access to GPT-4 or comparable alternatives.
    *   **Potential scalability limitations**: While the paper demonstrates results on a variety of operators and network architectures, the long compilation times (hours) might pose a limitation for very large and complex models. An analysis of compilation time scaling would be valuable. The discussion of the 'failure case' is helpful but highlights that there are some cases it does not yet resolve.
    *   **Implementation detail secrecy**: It seems that implementation secrecy in key areas of the code affects replicability by others. This limits follow-on works.
*   **Potential Influence:** QiMeng-Xpiler could potentially shift the paradigm of DLS programming towards more automated transcompilation.  It could influence the development of future compilers and programming tools for heterogeneous systems.  The neural-symbolic synthesis approach could be adopted in other domains beyond deep learning.

**Justification for Score:**

QiMeng-Xpiler represents a significant step forward in automating and simplifying DLS programming. The well-designed neural-symbolic approach, along with the thorough evaluation, provides strong evidence of its effectiveness. While the reliance on GPT-4 and limited scaling analysis somewhat diminish its impact, the practical significance and innovative nature of the work warrant a high score.

**Score: 8.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Large Language Models are overconfident and amplify human bias
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02151v1)
- **Authors**: Fengfei Sun, Ningke Li, Kailong Wang, Lorenz Goette
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are revolutionizing every aspect of society. They are increasingly used in problem-solving tasks to substitute human assessment and reasoning. LLMs are trained on what humans write and thus prone to learn human biases. One of the most widespread human biases is overconfidence. We examine whether LLMs inherit this bias. We automatically construct reasoning problems with known ground truths, and prompt LLMs to assess the confidence in their answers, closely following similar protocols in human experiments. We find that all five LLMs we study are overconfident: they overestimate the probability that their answer is correct between 20% and 60%. Humans have accuracy similar to the more advanced LLMs, but far lower overconfidence. Although humans and LLMs are similarly biased in questions which they are certain they answered correctly, a key difference emerges between them: LLM bias increases sharply relative to humans if they become less sure that their answers are correct. We also show that LLM input has ambiguous effects on human decision making: LLM input leads to an increase in the accuracy, but it more than doubles the extent of overconfidence in the answers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates overconfidence in large language models (LLMs) and its potential impact on human decision-making. The authors construct reasoning problems with known ground truths and prompt various LLMs to assess their confidence in their answers, using protocols similar to human experiments. They find that LLMs are consistently overconfident, significantly overestimating the probability of their answers being correct compared to human accuracy. The paper further explores how LLM input affects human decision-making, revealing that exposure to LLM responses, even with confidence levels, increases both accuracy and overconfidence in human answers. The study highlights a divergence in how LLMs and humans handle uncertainty: LLMs exhibit increased bias in less certain situations (akin to a Dunning-Kruger effect), whereas human bias tends to decrease.  The authors also analyze how the influence of LLMs varies based on users' initial confidence levels and demonstrate some differences across genders.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While previous research has explored calibration and uncertainty in LLMs, this paper distinguishes itself through its focus on algorithmically generated reasoning problems, minimizing training data contamination. This design allows a more direct examination of LLMs' intrinsic overconfidence in tasks requiring reasoning rather than mere recall.  Additionally, examining the influence of LLMs on *human* overconfidence is a meaningful contribution.

*   **Significance:** The findings carry considerable significance for understanding the limitations of LLMs in decision-making contexts. The identified overconfidence bias can lead to flawed reasoning and potentially amplified errors when LLMs are used as decision aids. The paper's demonstration that LLM input can increase *human* overconfidence despite improving accuracy is particularly concerning. It raises questions about user trust in AI systems and the potential for unintended consequences in real-world applications (e.g., healthcare, finance).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of algorithmically generated questions is a major strength, minimizing data contamination and ensuring that LLMs must engage in reasoning.
    *   **Human-LLM Comparison:**  Directly comparing LLM performance to human benchmarks, and also analyzing how LLMs can influence human confidence, provides valuable context and real-world relevance.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper goes beyond a simple demonstration of overconfidence, exploring the relationship between accuracy and confidence at varying levels, examining interaction effects and demographic considerations.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The results are presented clearly and concisely, supported by well-designed tables and figures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Question Complexity:** While the algorithmic generation method is valuable, some questions could be considered to be "trick" questions, which are designed to be difficult for LLMs to answer correctly; these types of questions may not reflect real-world scenarios.
    *   **Limited LLM Scope:** The paper focuses on only a few specific LLMs (GPT and Llama families). While these are representative models, the findings might not generalize to other architectures or training paradigms.
    *   **Simplifying Model of Overconfidence Effects:** The welfare model, while providing valuable insight, offers a simplification of a complex issue. It relies on specific assumptions about cost and payoff functions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM calibration, human-AI interaction, and the ethical considerations of deploying LLMs in decision-support systems. It highlights the need for more nuanced evaluation metrics that go beyond simple accuracy and account for confidence biases.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper demonstrates solid methodological rigor and explores a highly relevant topic. The findings regarding *amplification* of human overconfidence are important and concerning. While there may be limitations due to the simplified welfare model and LLMs used, the algorithmic question generation and clear human comparison strengthen the study. Therefore, I believe the paper warrants a high score.

### Think on your Feet: Adaptive Thinking via Reinforcement Learning for Social Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02156v1)
- **Authors**: Minzheng Wang, Yongbin Li, Haobo Wang, Xinghua Zhang, Nan Xu, Bingli Wu, Fei Huang, Haiyang Yu, Wenji Mao
- **Abstract**: Effective social intelligence simulation requires language agents to dynamically adjust reasoning depth, a capability notably absent in current approaches. While existing methods either lack this kind of reasoning capability or enforce uniform long chain-of-thought reasoning across all scenarios, resulting in excessive token usage and inappropriate social simulation. In this paper, we propose $\textbf{A}$daptive $\textbf{M}$ode $\textbf{L}$earning ($\textbf{AML}$) that strategically selects from four thinking modes (intuitive reaction $\rightarrow$ deep contemplation) based on real-time context. Our framework's core innovation, the $\textbf{A}$daptive $\textbf{M}$ode $\textbf{P}$olicy $\textbf{O}$ptimization ($\textbf{AMPO}$) algorithm, introduces three key advancements over existing methods: (1) Multi-granular thinking mode design, (2) Context-aware mode switching across social interaction, and (3) Token-efficient reasoning via depth-adaptive processing. Extensive experiments on social intelligence tasks confirm that AML achieves 15.6% higher task performance than state-of-the-art methods. Notably, our method outperforms GRPO by 7.0% with 32.8% shorter reasoning chains. These results demonstrate that context-sensitive thinking mode selection, as implemented in AMPO, enables more human-like adaptive reasoning than GRPO's fixed-depth approach
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary of the paper along with a rigorous and critical evaluation of its novelty and significance within the field.

**Summary:**
The paper "Think on Your Feet: Adaptive Thinking via Reinforcement Learning for Social Agents" addresses the lack of dynamic reasoning depth adaptation in current language agent approaches for social intelligence simulation. The authors propose Adaptive Mode Learning (AML) which strategically selects from four thinking modes (intuitive reaction to deep contemplation) based on real-time context. The framework's core is the Adaptive Mode Policy Optimization (AMPO) algorithm, which introduces (1) multi-granular thinking mode design, (2) context-aware mode switching across social interaction, and (3) token-efficient reasoning via depth-adaptive processing. Experiments on social intelligence tasks show AML achieving higher performance and shorter reasoning chains compared to state-of-the-art methods, suggesting a more human-like adaptive reasoning approach.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The concept of adaptive reasoning depth in social agents is a compelling and novel contribution. Existing approaches often enforce uniform reasoning, leading to inefficiencies and unrealistic social behaviors. AML offers a practical way to address this limitation.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The AMPO algorithm provides a structured approach to implementing adaptive thinking, with clear explanations of the multi-granular thinking modes and the reinforcement learning methodology.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The comprehensive experiments across different social intelligence tasks and comparisons to strong baselines (including GRPO and closed-source LLMs) convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of AML. The reduction in token usage is a significant practical benefit.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a critical gap in existing social agent research.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The AML framework and AMPO algorithm are clearly defined, facilitating reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity of Design:** The four thinking modes, while inspired by cognitive control theory, may seem somewhat arbitrary. A more detailed justification of these specific modes would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Evaluation:** While acknowledged as a common practice, the use of GPT-4 for evaluation introduces potential biases. Human evaluation, although conducted, is limited in sample size. A more extensive human evaluation would further bolster the claims.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** The paper focuses primarily on the successes of AML. A discussion of the scenarios where AML struggles or fails to select the appropriate thinking mode would offer a more balanced perspective.
    *   **Generalizability beyond SOTOPIA:** It's important to consider if the adaptive mode selection truly enhances social intelligence or is specifically tailored for SOTOPIA environment.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper significantly advances the state-of-the-art in social agent design by introducing a mechanism for adaptive reasoning depth. This is a crucial step toward creating more realistic and efficient social agents.
    *   The AMPO algorithm provides a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners interested in developing adaptive AI systems.
    *   The findings have implications for various applications, including virtual assistants, negotiation agents, and simulated social environments.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper is likely to stimulate further research into adaptive reasoning in social agents and other AI domains.
    *   The AML framework and AMPO algorithm can serve as a foundation for future innovations in this area.
    *   The work may influence the design of more sophisticated and human-like AI systems.

*   **Overall:**
    The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of social intelligence by addressing the limitations of static reasoning approaches. The proposed AML framework and AMPO algorithm offer a practical and effective solution for adaptive reasoning depth. While there are some minor weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh them, making it a valuable and influential work. The rigorous evaluation with strong baselines also supports the significance and robustness of the findings.

### Focus What Matters: Matchability-Based Reweighting for Local Feature Matching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02161v1)
- **Authors**: Dongyue Li
- **Abstract**: Since the rise of Transformers, many semi-dense matching methods have adopted attention mechanisms to extract feature descriptors. However, the attention weights, which capture dependencies between pixels or keypoints, are often learned from scratch. This approach can introduce redundancy and noisy interactions from irrelevant regions, as it treats all pixels or keypoints equally. Drawing inspiration from keypoint selection processes, we propose to first classify all pixels into two categories: matchable and non-matchable. Matchable pixels are expected to receive higher attention weights, while non-matchable ones are down-weighted. In this work, we propose a novel attention reweighting mechanism that simultaneously incorporates a learnable bias term into the attention logits and applies a matchability-informed rescaling to the input value features. The bias term, injected prior to the softmax operation, selectively adjusts attention scores based on the confidence of query-key interactions. Concurrently, the feature rescaling acts post-attention by modulating the influence of each value vector in the final output. This dual design allows the attention mechanism to dynamically adjust both its internal weighting scheme and the magnitude of its output representations. Extensive experiments conducted on three benchmark datasets validate the effectiveness of our method, consistently outperforming existing state-of-the-art approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of uniformly weighting all pixels in Transformer-based local feature matching, arguing that this leads to redundancy and noisy interactions from irrelevant regions.  The authors propose a "matchability-based reweighting" mechanism. This involves: first classifying pixels as either "matchable" or "non-matchable" via a binary classification task.  Then, they modify the attention mechanism in two ways: (1) injecting a bias term into the attention logits to favor likely query-key pairs, and (2) rescaling the input value features based on the matchability scores. This dual-stage reweighting aims to focus attention on semantically significant regions, improving robustness and accuracy. The method is evaluated on image matching and relative pose estimation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating a matchability prior into the attention mechanism is a reasonable and potentially effective approach. Drawing inspiration from keypoint detectors in traditional pipelines to guide attention is intuitive. The dual-stage attention reweighting (bias in logits + value rescaling) offers a refinement over simply weighting the attention scores directly. This two-pronged approach might allow finer-grained control and adaptation. The implementation details, such as the specific CNN backbone used for matchability estimation and the RoPE encoding, may not be fundamentally novel in themselves, but the combination and application to attention reweighting are where the novelty lies.
* **Significance:** Local feature matching is a fundamental problem in computer vision, impacting many downstream tasks. Improving the efficiency and accuracy of these methods is therefore important. If the proposed method consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art approaches, it constitutes a significant step forward. The method is detector-free which is beneficial. The visualisations provided show the method is better at focusing on the regions of interest. The experiments performed showcase the method's effectiveness across different datasets and tasks, increasing its significance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Motivation:** The problem of uniformly weighting all pixels is well-articulated, and the motivation for introducing a matchability prior is clear and compelling.
    * **Intuitive Approach:**  The idea of focusing attention on "matchable" regions aligns with intuition about how humans solve correspondence problems.
    * **Dual-Stage Design:** The combination of bias injection and value rescaling adds complexity and potentially greater flexibility to the attention mechanism.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover multiple datasets and tasks, providing evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    * **Strong Results:** The paper claims to outperform existing state-of-the-art methods, which would be a major strength if validated.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Matchability Prediction:** The entire method hinges on the accuracy of the matchability prediction. If the matchability classification is poor, the reweighting could actually *hurt* performance.  The sensitivity of the results to this module's accuracy needs to be explored.
    * **Complexity:** Introducing an additional binary classification task increases the complexity of the overall pipeline. The benefit in performance must outweigh this additional complexity.
    * **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the experiments demonstrate performance gains, there may be a limited amount of analysis on why the specific design choices are effective. Further analysis or ablation study to isolate the contribution from each components would be helpful.
    * **Incremental Improvement?:** While claiming to outperform the state of the art, the actual improvement margin needs to be looked at. Is the performance difference significant enough to justify the added complexity?

**Overall:**

The paper proposes a reasonable and potentially effective approach to improve Transformer-based local feature matching. It successfully utilizes a dual-stage design and showcases strong experimental results. However, the reliance on the matchability prediction module and the increased complexity of the pipeline bring up certain concerns.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The proposed method is a solid contribution to the field of local feature matching. However, given the novelty is somewhat incremental (building on top of existing attention mechanisms and using established techniques for matchability prediction) and given the concerns related to complexity and reliance on accurate matchability prediction, a score higher than 7 would be an overstatement. It's a valuable step forward, but not a revolutionary breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Incorporating Legal Structure in Retrieval-Augmented Generation: A Case Study on Copyright Fair Use
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02164v1)
- **Authors**: Justin Ho, Alexandra Colby, William Fisher
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a domain-specific implementation of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) tailored to the Fair Use Doctrine in U.S. copyright law. Motivated by the increasing prevalence of DMCA takedowns and the lack of accessible legal support for content creators, we propose a structured approach that combines semantic search with legal knowledge graphs and court citation networks to improve retrieval quality and reasoning reliability. Our prototype models legal precedents at the statutory factor level (e.g., purpose, nature, amount, market effect) and incorporates citation-weighted graph representations to prioritize doctrinally authoritative sources. We use Chain-of-Thought reasoning and interleaved retrieval steps to better emulate legal reasoning. Preliminary testing suggests this method improves doctrinal relevance in the retrieval process, laying groundwork for future evaluation and deployment of LLM-based legal assistance tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) techniques specifically tailored for legal reasoning within the Fair Use Doctrine of US copyright law. Recognizing the limitations of generic RAG systems in the legal domain, particularly concerning hallucination and irrelevant retrievals, the authors propose a structured RAG approach that incorporates legal knowledge graphs and court citation networks. This system models legal precedents at the statutory factor level (purpose, nature, amount, market effect), utilizes citation-weighted graph representations to prioritize authoritative sources, and employs Chain-of-Thought reasoning to emulate legal reasoning processes. Preliminary results suggest improved doctrinal relevance in retrieval.  The authors also constructed a prototype of the system, which is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The core idea of applying RAG to the legal domain is not entirely novel; however, the specific implementation focusing on the Fair Use Doctrine and leveraging knowledge graphs with court hierarchy and citation information significantly increases its novelty. The integration of statutory factor-level modeling and citation-weighted PageRank further differentiates it from general-purpose legal RAG systems. The one-step Interleaving Retrieval CoT also contribute to originality.

*Significance:* The problem the paper addresses—the lack of accessible legal support for content creators facing DMCA takedowns and the unreliability of LLMs in providing accurate legal advice—is significant. A reliable LLM-based tool could empower creators and reduce self-censorship. The potential impact on access to justice and the democratization of legal knowledge is considerable. The paper offers a specific approach towards addressing these issues, and the resulting prototype is publicly available.

*Strengths:*

*   **Domain Specificity:** By focusing on the Fair Use Doctrine, the authors can create a more specialized and accurate RAG system than a generic legal LLM.
*   **Structured Knowledge Representation:** The knowledge graph captures essential legal relationships (court hierarchy, citation flow) that are crucial for effective reasoning.
*   **Granular Retrieval:** Retrieving information at the statutory factor level allows for more context-sensitive and precise analysis.
*   **Citation Weighting:** Using PageRank to prioritize doctrinally authoritative sources is a smart way to improve retrieval relevance.
*   **Transparent Architecture:** The design of the system is clearly described, allowing for reproducibility and future improvements.
*   **Publicly Available Prototype and Code:** This promotes transparency and enables further research and development by others.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Preliminary Results:** The results presented are preliminary, lacking rigorous quantitative evaluations and user studies. The benefits shown are primarily qualitative and need to be validated with larger datasets.  Additionally, the existing prototype needs further refinement and testing with real users.
*   **Reliance on LLMs for Extraction:** The extraction of legal paragraphs related to each of the four factors relies on LLMs, which can introduce errors and biases. The prompts are not detailed, which means we have to trust the LLMs are doing as they should.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** The approach is highly tailored to the Fair Use Doctrine. While the principles could be adapted to other legal areas, significant engineering effort would be required for each new domain.
*   **Incomplete Mitigation of LLM Weaknesses:** Despite the structural enhancements, the prototype still exhibits the inherent weaknesses of LLMs, such as hallucination and sycophancy. Also, metrics like PageRank can also not be comprehensive estimates.
*   **Subjectivity in Legal Reasoning:** Legal reasoning often involves subjectivity and contextual nuance, which may be challenging for an automated system to capture fully.

*Potential Influence:*  The paper provides a solid framework for building more reliable and trustworthy legal AI systems. It has the potential to influence future research on RAG in legal domains, particularly in the design of knowledge graphs and the incorporation of legal structure. The availability of the prototype and source code could spur further development and experimentation. It also presents a concrete approach which could be adapted to other complex, multi-factor legal tests.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of legal AI. Its strengths lie in the innovative integration of knowledge graphs and RAG, tailored specifically for the Fair Use Doctrine. The structured approach, granular retrieval, and citation weighting offer promising improvements over general-purpose legal LLMs. The prototype and the open-source code contribute to community development. The novelty of the system is well developed, especially when taking into consideration the one-step Interleaving Retrieval CoT as well as the statutory factor modeling.

However, the preliminary nature of the results and the limitations related to LLM reliance, generalizability, and the handling of legal subjectivity temper the impact. A rigorous quantitative evaluation and user studies are needed to fully validate the claims.

### A New HOPE: Domain-agnostic Automatic Evaluation of Text Chunking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02171v1)
- **Authors**: Henrik Brådland, Morten Goodwin, Per-Arne Andersen, Alexander S. Nossum, Aditya Gupta
- **Abstract**: Document chunking fundamentally impacts Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) by determining how source materials are segmented before indexing. Despite evidence that Large Language Models (LLMs) are sensitive to the layout and structure of retrieved data, there is currently no framework to analyze the impact of different chunking methods. In this paper, we introduce a novel methodology that defines essential characteristics of the chunking process at three levels: intrinsic passage properties, extrinsic passage properties, and passages-document coherence. We propose HOPE (Holistic Passage Evaluation), a domain-agnostic, automatic evaluation metric that quantifies and aggregates these characteristics. Our empirical evaluations across seven domains demonstrate that the HOPE metric correlates significantly (p > 0.13) with various RAG performance indicators, revealing contrasts between the importance of extrinsic and intrinsic properties of passages. Semantic independence between passages proves essential for system performance with a performance gain of up to 56.2% in factual correctness and 21.1% in answer correctness. On the contrary, traditional assumptions about maintaining concept unity within passages show minimal impact. These findings provide actionable insights for optimizing chunking strategies, thus improving RAG system design to produce more factually correct responses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HOPE (Holistic Passage Evaluation), a novel, domain-agnostic, and automatic evaluation metric for text chunking in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems.  HOPE assesses chunking quality at three levels: intrinsic passage properties (concept unity), extrinsic passage properties (semantic independence), and passage-document coherence (information preservation). The authors formalize principles for effective chunking and empirically demonstrate that HOPE correlates with RAG performance across several domains. Their findings suggest that semantic independence between passages is crucial for factual correctness in generated responses, while the traditional assumption of maintaining concept unity within passages may be less important.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in proposing a comprehensive and automatic evaluation metric for chunking, independent of downstream task performance.  While existing work has explored the impact of chunking on RAG, this paper attempts to directly quantify the *quality* of chunking itself. The separation of chunking evaluation into intrinsic, extrinsic, and coherence properties is also a novel contribution. This holistic view provides a more structured way to understand the complex trade-offs involved in chunking. However, some aspects, like using LLMs for semantic comparison and question answering, are not entirely new individually but rather in combination within the HOPE framework. Also, the high correlation metrics between HOPE and different RAG settings does not translate to direct evidence of direct causal improvements if using HOPE.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing a crucial gap in RAG research: the lack of a direct and automated way to evaluate chunking strategies.  By providing HOPE, the authors offer a tool that can help researchers and practitioners develop more effective chunking methods and understand their impact on RAG performance. The finding that semantic independence is important, while concept unity is not, challenges existing assumptions and provides actionable insights for improving RAG systems.
The paper would be greatly improved with a more thorough exploration of the limitations, particularly those relating to reliance on LLMs for evaluation (which inherently injects model-specific biases and potential for hallucination) and a clearer demonstration of direct causal improvements when HOPE is implemented.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper clearly defines the problem and motivates the need for a dedicated chunking evaluation metric.
    * The proposed HOPE metric is well-structured and incorporates several relevant factors.
    * The empirical evaluation is extensive, covering a diverse set of domains and chunking methods.
    * The findings challenge existing assumptions about chunking and provide actionable insights.
    * The paper formalizes principles for chunking, providing a theoretical foundation for the metric.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * *LLM Reliance:*  The metric heavily relies on LLMs for various aspects of evaluation (concept unity, information preservation, semantic independence), introducing potential biases, and uncertainty. The paper acknowledges this but could benefit from a deeper discussion of mitigation strategies (e.g., using multiple LLMs, prompting techniques to minimize biases, or sensitivity analysis to assess the impact of LLM variations).
    * *Computational Cost:* The extensive use of LLMs makes HOPE computationally expensive, limiting its scalability and practical applicability. The authors could explore alternative methods for approximating HOPE's components that are less resource-intensive.
    * *Limited Causal Evidence:* While the paper demonstrates a correlation between HOPE and RAG performance, it does not provide strong evidence that optimizing HOPE *directly* leads to improved RAG results. A more rigorous experimental setup would involve using HOPE to guide chunking strategy selection and demonstrating the resulting improvement in RAG performance. This direct causal link is missing.
    * *Simplified Binary Scoring:* The information preservation metric uses a binary scoring mechanism which simplifies the evaluation.
    * *Limited Diversity:* The Concept Unity implementations generates limited diversity.
    * *External dependencies:* The heavy reliance on various libraries hinders reproducibility and scalability.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing a new framework for evaluating chunking in RAG systems. The conceptualization of chunking quality into intrinsic, extrinsic, and coherence properties is well-reasoned and provides a useful structure for analysis. The empirical results, particularly the emphasis on semantic independence, offer important insights. However, the heavy reliance on LLMs introduces significant limitations in terms of bias, computational cost, and generalizability. Furthermore, the lack of direct causal evidence linking HOPE optimization to improved RAG performance weakens the overall impact of the paper. A higher score could be justified if the authors addressed these weaknesses, particularly by exploring alternative evaluation methods, providing stronger causal evidence, and reducing HOPE's computational complexity.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Identifying Legal Holdings with LLMs: A Systematic Study of Performance, Scale, and Memorization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02172v1)
- **Authors**: Chuck Arvin
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) continue to advance in capabilities, it is essential to assess how they perform on established benchmarks. In this study, we present a suite of experiments to assess the performance of modern LLMs (ranging from 3B to 90B+ parameters) on CaseHOLD, a legal benchmark dataset for identifying case holdings. Our experiments demonstrate ``scaling effects'' - performance on this task improves with model size, with more capable models like GPT4o and AmazonNovaPro achieving macro F1 scores of 0.744 and 0.720 respectively. These scores are competitive with the best published results on this dataset, and do not require any technically sophisticated model training, fine-tuning or few-shot prompting. To ensure that these strong results are not due to memorization of judicial opinions contained in the training data, we develop and utilize a novel citation anonymization test that preserves semantic meaning while ensuring case names and citations are fictitious. Models maintain strong performance under these conditions (macro F1 of 0.728), suggesting the performance is not due to rote memorization. These findings demonstrate both the promise and current limitations of LLMs for legal tasks with important implications for the development and measurement of automated legal analytics and legal benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of large language models (LLMs) on the CaseHOLD dataset, a legal benchmark for identifying case holdings. It examines scaling effects (how performance improves with model size), compares performance to custom-built legal models, and addresses the critical question of whether LLMs are simply memorizing training data. The authors introduce a novel citation anonymization technique to assess memorization, finding that models maintain strong performance even when case names and citations are replaced with fictitious ones, suggesting that performance is not solely based on rote memorization. The study concludes that LLMs show promise for legal NLP tasks but highlights the need for careful measurement and scrutiny to ensure accurate and reliable results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents two primary novel contributions:

    1.  **Systematic LLM evaluation on CaseHOLD:** While LLMs have been evaluated on various tasks, this paper provides a specific and thorough examination of their performance on legal reasoning, focusing on the task of identifying case holdings. The breadth of models (from 3B to 90B+ parameters) tested is also a strength.
    2.  **Citation anonymization technique:** The paper introduces a novel approach to address the crucial question of memorization in LLMs. This method allows the authors to evaluate whether models are truly "reasoning" or simply recalling information from their training data. While anonymization itself isn't new, applying it systematically to citations in the legal domain and measuring its impact on performance is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper has several important implications:

    *   **Benchmarks for Legal NLP:** It provides valuable insights into the capabilities of LLMs for legal tasks, contributing to the development and refinement of legal benchmarks.
    *   **Memorization Debate:** By addressing the memorization issue, the paper contributes to the broader discussion about the trustworthiness and reliability of LLMs, particularly in high-stakes domains like law.
    *   **Scaling Effects:** The demonstration of scaling effects reinforces the importance of continued development and refinement of general-purpose LLMs, as they may have significant impacts on specialized domains like legal analytics.
    *   **Practical implications:** It showcases that general-purpose LLMs without fine-tuning can achieve results comparable to specially trained legal models on a certain legal task.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper presents a systematic and well-designed set of experiments, covering a range of LLMs and performance metrics.
    *   **Addressing Memorization:** The citation anonymization technique is a valuable contribution and provides a concrete method for addressing the memorization issue.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its research questions, methodology, and findings.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The availability of code and datasets is a significant strength.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Domain:** While the focus on CaseHOLD is understandable, the generalizability of the findings to other legal tasks may be limited. It would be beneficial to see how these models perform on other legal datasets or tasks.
    *   **Memorization Detection Imperfect:** While the anonymization is a good step, it doesn't completely eliminate the possibility of the model memorizing patterns or broader reasoning structures, not just case names. It would be beneficial to conduct further testing to explore this area.
    *   **Scope of Anonymization:** While the paper mentions that it ensured that citation numbers and other identifiers were changed in the anonymization process, it does not mention if it conducted fact-checking to ensure that it was not relying on other facts within the text.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings are likely to influence future research in legal NLP, particularly in the development of more robust benchmarks and techniques for assessing the trustworthiness of LLMs. The citation anonymization technique could be adopted and refined by other researchers working on similar problems. The demonstration that LLMs can, without task-specific training, equal or exceed specialized legal models on some tasks encourages further exploration of foundation models in the legal domain.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable and well-executed study of LLMs on a legal task, addresses a significant concern (memorization) through a novel approach, and presents clear and potentially impactful findings. While the scope is somewhat limited by its focus on a single dataset, the quality of the research and the significance of its contributions warrant a score of 7. The citation anonymization is a notable contribution, and the systematic scaling analysis is valuable. The memorization question has high stakes and has only been addressed in very limited ways so far. Further exploration and validation of this technique is necessary. A score of 7 reflects both the strengths of the work and the need for further research to address its limitations and generalize its findings.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Measuring Hong Kong Massive Multi-Task Language Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02177v1)
- **Authors**: Chuxue Cao, Zhenghao Zhu, Junqi Zhu, Guoying Lu, Siyu Peng, Juntao Dai, Weijie Shi, Sirui Han, Yike Guo
- **Abstract**: Multilingual understanding is crucial for the cross-cultural applicability of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, evaluation benchmarks designed for Hong Kong's unique linguistic landscape, which combines Traditional Chinese script with Cantonese as the spoken form and its cultural context, remain underdeveloped. To address this gap, we introduce HKMMLU, a multi-task language understanding benchmark that evaluates Hong Kong's linguistic competence and socio-cultural knowledge. The HKMMLU includes 26,698 multi-choice questions across 66 subjects, organized into four categories: Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM), Social Sciences, Humanities, and Other. To evaluate the multilingual understanding ability of LLMs, 90,550 Mandarin-Cantonese translation tasks were additionally included. We conduct comprehensive experiments on GPT-4o, Claude 3.7 Sonnet, and 18 open-source LLMs of varying sizes on HKMMLU. The results show that the best-performing model, DeepSeek-V3, struggles to achieve an accuracy of 75\%, significantly lower than that of MMLU and CMMLU. This performance gap highlights the need to improve LLMs' capabilities in Hong Kong-specific language and knowledge domains. Furthermore, we investigate how question language, model size, prompting strategies, and question and reasoning token lengths affect model performance. We anticipate that HKMMLU will significantly advance the development of LLMs in multilingual and cross-cultural contexts, thereby enabling broader and more impactful applications.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces HKMMLU, a new multi-task language understanding benchmark specifically designed to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of Hong Kong's unique linguistic and socio-cultural environment. The benchmark comprises over 26,000 multi-choice questions across 66 subjects, organized into STEM, Social Sciences, Humanities, and Other categories.  Additionally, the benchmark includes over 90,000 Mandarin-Cantonese translation tasks to assess LLMs' ability to handle Cantonese, the spoken language of Hong Kong. The paper evaluates several closed-source and open-source LLMs on HKMMLU and analyzes the impact of factors such as question language, model size, prompting strategies, and token lengths on model performance. The results reveal that even the best-performing models struggle to achieve high accuracy on HKMMLU, highlighting the need for improvement in LLMs' understanding of Hong Kong-specific language and knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a clear gap in existing benchmarks by focusing on Hong Kong's specific linguistic and cultural landscape, which is often overlooked in general Chinese language understanding evaluations. Existing benchmarks like CMMLU and C-Eval, while valuable, do not adequately capture the nuances of Hong Kong's legal system, historical narratives, socio-linguistic practices, and its unique blend of Eastern and Western cultures. The creation of a dedicated benchmark that incorporates Cantonese translation tasks, and questions tailored to Hong Kong's knowledge domain is a solid contribution. The paper differentiates itself from HKCanto-Eval as HKMMLU mostly sourced from Hong Kong-specific material and covered a wider range of subjects.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in recognizing the importance of culturally sensitive and linguistically appropriate LLMs for cross-cultural applicability. The poor performance of current LLMs on HKMMLU underscores the need for models to be trained and evaluated on region-specific data to ensure their effectiveness in diverse cultural contexts. This has implications for the responsible development and deployment of LLMs globally. It highlights that achieving high accuracy in a general language or even a broader cultural context (like Chinese) does not guarantee understanding of more specific regional or cultural niches.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined benchmark with a good variety of subjects.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of multiple LLMs.
    *   Analysis of various factors influencing model performance.
    *   Human evaluation to compare LLM performance to that of local residents.
    *   The paper is clearly written and well-organized.
    *   The inclusion of a Cantonese translation task is a major strength.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper analyzes the impact of question language, it could delve deeper into specific linguistic features of Cantonese that pose challenges for LLMs. Perhaps comparing error types on Cantonese questions vs. Mandarin questions would reveal such findings.
    *   The human evaluation sample size (100 questions) could be expanded to provide more statistically robust results.
    *   The paper could further explore the biases potentially encoded in current pre-training datasets toward certain regional Chinese variations.
    *   The dataset, while novel, has an unequal number of questions per category which could skew the overall averaging.
    *   Some of the described methodologies are under development.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the development of more culturally and linguistically sensitive LLMs. It could encourage researchers to create more targeted benchmarks for other underrepresented languages and cultures. The analysis of factors influencing model performance provides valuable insights for improving LLMs' regional knowledge. Also, the dataset could be useful for future research.

*   **Justification of Score:** I assign a score of 7. The paper's novelty in creating a Hong Kong-specific benchmark is undeniable and important for the responsible development of LLMs. The evaluation of existing models and analysis of influencing factors are also valuable contributions. However, the relatively small scale of the human evaluation and potential biases in existing pre-training datasets limit the paper's overall impact. Furthermore, some of the dataset creation methodologies are under development.
