### Sharpness-Aware Minimization with Z-Score Gradient Filtering for Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02369v1)
- **Authors**: Juyoung Yun
- **Abstract**: Generalizing well in deep neural networks remains a core challenge, particularly due to their tendency to converge to sharp minima that degrade robustness. Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) mitigates this by seeking flatter minima but perturbs parameters using the full gradient, which can include statistically insignificant directions. We propose ZSharp, a simple yet effective extension to SAM that applies layer-wise Z-score normalization followed by percentile-based filtering to retain only statistically significant gradient components. This selective perturbation aligns updates with curvature-sensitive directions, enhancing generalization without requiring architectural changes. ZSharp introduces only one additional hyperparameter, the percentile threshold, and remains fully compatible with existing SAM variants. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet using ResNet, VGG, and Vision Transformers show that ZSharp consistently outperforms SAM and its variants in test accuracy, particularly on deeper and transformer-based models. These results demonstrate that ZSharp is a principled and lightweight improvement for sharpness-aware optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZSharp, a novel extension to Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) for training neural networks. ZSharp aims to improve generalization by incorporating statistical filtering into the SAM process. It applies layer-wise Z-score normalization to the gradients, followed by a percentile-based filtering to retain only the statistically significant gradient components. The idea is that by focusing on these significant components, the perturbation used in SAM becomes more aligned with curvature-sensitive directions, leading to flatter minima and improved generalization. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of ZSharp through experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet datasets using ResNet, VGG, and Vision Transformer architectures, showing consistent improvements over SAM and its variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of ZSharp – integrating Z-score based gradient filtering with SAM – is moderately novel. While both SAM and Z-score normalization have been used before, their combination within the SAM framework, particularly to refine the perturbation direction, represents an incremental innovation. The paper builds upon existing techniques (SAM and ZNorm), and the contribution can be seen as a principled refinement rather than a radical departure. It differentiates itself from methods like ASAM and Friendly-SAM by acting directly on the perturbation *direction* rather than scaling the magnitude or approximating the sharpness objective.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to enhance the generalization capabilities of neural networks with a relatively simple and computationally lightweight modification to SAM. The experimental results consistently demonstrate improved performance compared to SAM and its variants across various datasets and architectures, including Vision Transformers. This is particularly important, as Transformers are often more susceptible to noise in gradients. The approach requires minimal hyperparameter tuning (only the percentile threshold) and is easily integrated with existing SAM implementations. The gains in performance and robustness are valuable, which suggest this technique could be important.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on widely used benchmark datasets, providing convincing evidence for the effectiveness of ZSharp.
*   **Simplicity and Efficiency:** ZSharp introduces only one additional hyperparameter and is computationally efficient, making it practical for real-world applications.
*   **Compatibility:**  ZSharp is compatible with existing SAM implementations and optimizers, making it easy to integrate into existing training pipelines.
*   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of SAM and provides a well-reasoned motivation for the proposed approach.
*   **Good comparisons:** Compared with a good set of baselines, including SAM itself and SAM variants.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Innovation:** The novelty is incremental, building upon existing techniques. While the combination is effective, the individual components are well-established.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides a justification for ZSharp, a more rigorous theoretical analysis of its convergence properties and generalization guarantees would strengthen the work. The discussion of convergence is brief and lacks depth.
*   **Limited Exploration of the Percentile Threshold:** The paper identifies 0.95 as the optimal percentile threshold, but a more thorough exploration of its sensitivity and impact on different datasets and architectures would be valuable. Are the results that are shown robust to different threshold choices?
*   **Generalization Claims:** The paper's analysis focuses on image classification datasets. It could benefit from testing its method on a broader range of application areas, such as NLP or reinforcement learning, to solidify the generalization claims.
*   **Hessian Approximation Discussion:** Discussion of Hessian approximation is light. A little more detail would be valuable.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a simple and effective technique for improving the generalization of neural networks. The improved performance on Transformers is particularly valuable, given their growing importance in various applications. Other researchers and practitioners may adopt the ZSharp modification to enhance the performance of their models.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.  The paper presents a valuable and well-validated incremental improvement to a widely used optimization technique (SAM). The experimental results are convincing, and the approach is both simple and efficient. The score reflects the paper's practical contribution and its potential to be adopted by the community. The main limitations are its incremental novelty, the lack of deeper theoretical analysis and the limited scope of evaluated datasets. However, its relative simplicity and broad effectiveness still make it a noteworthy contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Score**: 7/10

### LAMeD: LLM-generated Annotations for Memory Leak Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02376v1)
- **Authors**: Ekaterina Shemetova, Ilya Shenbin, Ivan Smirnov, Anton Alekseev, Alexey Rukhovich, Sergey Nikolenko, Vadim Lomshakov, Irina Piontkovskaya
- **Abstract**: Static analysis tools are widely used to detect software bugs and vulnerabilities but often struggle with scalability and efficiency in complex codebases. Traditional approaches rely on manually crafted annotations -- labeling functions as sources or sinks -- to track data flows, e.g., ensuring that allocated memory is eventually freed, and code analysis tools such as CodeQL, Infer, or Cooddy can use function specifications, but manual annotation is laborious and error-prone, especially for large or third-party libraries. We present LAMeD (LLM-generated Annotations for Memory leak Detection), a novel approach that leverages large language models (LLMs) to automatically generate function-specific annotations. When integrated with analyzers such as Cooddy, LAMeD significantly improves memory leak detection and reduces path explosion. We also suggest directions for extending LAMeD to broader code analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LAMED: LLM-generated Annotations for Memory Leak Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LAMED (LLM-generated Annotations for Memory leak Detection), a novel approach that leverages large language models (LLMs) to automatically generate function-specific annotations for memory leak detection in C/C++ code. The core idea is to use LLMs to identify functions that allocate or deallocate memory, addressing the limitations of manually crafted annotations and rule-based heuristics in static analysis tools.  LAMED extracts the call graph and source code, prompts the LLM to identify memory allocation/deallocation points, converts the LLM output to an analyzer-specific annotation format, and then executes the static analysis with these annotations. The authors evaluate LAMED with CodeQL and Cooddy on a mix of synthetic and real-world C projects, demonstrating improved memory leak detection compared to baseline configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for static analysis annotations is a promising direction, building on the increased abilities of code-aware LLMs to understand code semantics. While several papers have investigated LLMs for code analysis, repair, or generating documentation, the specific focus on *automated annotation for memory leak detection*, particularly with the detailed pipeline involving call graph extraction and context enrichment, adds a valuable contribution.  Existing annotation strategies often relied on heuristics or manual labor. LAMED aims to automate and potentially improve this process, which is a valuable step.

* **Significance:** Memory leak detection is a persistent problem in software development, especially in languages like C/C++ without automatic garbage collection. Existing static analysis tools often struggle with scalability and accuracy, especially in large and complex codebases. The significance lies in the potential to improve the accuracy of these tools, reducing false positives, and enabling them to detect memory leaks that might otherwise be missed, especially when dealing with custom memory management routines. By automatically generating annotations, LAMED can significantly reduce the burden on developers and security experts and democratize the static analysis usage. The results on real-world bugs support the claim that LLM-generated annotations improve real-life leak detection with the tools.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing memory leak detection techniques and positions LAMED as a solution.
    * **Well-Defined Approach:** The LAMED pipeline is well-explained, outlining the steps involved in extracting code context, prompting the LLM, and integrating the annotations with static analysis tools.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation, testing LAMED with different static analysis tools (CodeQL and Cooddy) and datasets (synthetic and real-world projects). The use of a manually annotated cJSON library for annotation quality analysis is a strong point. The study uses carefully selected memory leak cases in various projects.
    * **Promising Results:** The experimental results demonstrate that LAMED can improve the accuracy of memory leak detection.
    * **Implementation details:** The prompt examples illustrate the prompting strategies, highlighting the questions used to elicit memory-allocation knowledge from the LLM.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **LLM Cost and Efficiency:** The paper acknowledges the cost of querying LLMs but doesn't fully address the efficiency implications. While annotation generation may be less frequent, the query time can still be substantial for large codebases. Future work should focus on caching mechanisms, efficient prompting strategies, and smaller models or model fine-tuning.
    * **Warning Explosion:**  The significant increase in analyzer warnings with annotations is a major concern. While more bugs are found, the flood of warnings might overwhelm developers, making it difficult to triage the actual issues.  Strategies for filtering and prioritizing warnings are crucial. Although this weakness is acknowledged, a more in-depth analysis of which annotations lead to false alarms, and strategies to avoid them, would have been beneficial.  This is a key area for improvement.
    * **Generalization and LLM Reliance:** The performance of LAMED is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLM.  Different LLMs might yield different results. Furthermore, the reliance on LLMs introduces a potential dependency on external services or specific model architectures. More experiments and analysis would have been performed with different LLMs.
    * **Lack of Comparisons to Existing Techniques:** While the paper compares LAMED to the baseline configurations of existing static analysis tools, it lacks comparisons to alternative automated annotation techniques (if any exist).

* **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to a significant problem, with promising empirical results. The clear methodology and evaluation strengthen the contribution. However, the limitations related to LLM cost, warning explosion, and LLM reliance are significant. A lot of engineering will be required for scaling this technique.

### EntroLLM: Entropy Encoded Weight Compression for Efficient Large Language Model Inference on Edge Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02380v1)
- **Authors**: Arnab Sanyal, Prithwish Mukherjee, Gourav Datta, Sandeep P. Chinchali
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate exceptional performance across various tasks, but their large storage and computational requirements constrain their deployment on edge devices. To address this, we propose EntroLLM, a novel compression framework that integrates mixed quantization with entropy coding to reduce storage overhead while maintaining model accuracy. Our method applies a layer-wise mixed quantization scheme - choosing between symmetric and asymmetric quantization based on individual layer weight distributions - to optimize compressibility. We then employ Huffman encoding for lossless compression of the quantized weights, significantly reducing memory bandwidth requirements. Furthermore, we introduce parallel Huffman decoding, which enables efficient retrieval of encoded weights during inference, ensuring minimal latency impact. Our experiments on edge-compatible LLMs, including smolLM-1.7B-Instruct, phi3-mini-4k-Instruct, and mistral-7B-Instruct, demonstrate that EntroLLM achieves up to $30%$ storage reduction compared to uint8 models and up to $65%$ storage reduction compared to uint4 models, while preserving perplexity and accuracy, on language benchmark tasks. We further show that our method enables $31.9%$ - $146.6%$ faster inference throughput on memory-bandwidth-limited edge devices, such as NVIDIA Jetson P3450, by reducing the required data movement. The proposed approach requires no additional re-training and is fully compatible with existing post-training quantization methods, making it a practical solution for edge LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "EntroLLM: Entropy Encoded Weight Compression for Efficient Large Language Model Inference on Edge Devices" introduces a novel compression framework called EntroLLM designed to reduce the storage and memory bandwidth requirements of large language models (LLMs) when deployed on edge devices. EntroLLM combines mixed quantization (layer-wise selection of symmetric or asymmetric quantization) with Huffman encoding to achieve high compression rates. A parallel Huffman decoding scheme is also proposed to minimize inference latency. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on several edge-compatible LLMs, showing significant storage reduction and improved inference throughput on a memory-bandwidth-limited device (NVIDIA Jetson P3450). The method is post-training and requires no retraining, making it practical for deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of mixed quantization with Huffman encoding for LLM weight compression is somewhat novel. While both techniques have been explored separately, the specific synergy achieved through layer-wise quantization adaptation and subsequent entropy coding, along with the parallel decoding scheme, contributes to a unique approach. However, the use of Huffman coding itself for compression is a relatively standard technique, reducing the overall degree of innovation.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: the deployment of large LLMs on resource-constrained edge devices. Reducing storage and memory bandwidth requirements is essential for enabling real-time inference in applications like robotics and IoT. The reported improvements in storage reduction and inference throughput are significant, especially considering the lack of retraining requirement.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-structured method with a detailed description of each component (mixed quantization, Huffman encoding, parallel decoding).
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, including multiple LLMs and a relevant edge device (Jetson P3450).
    *   The results demonstrate substantial improvements in storage reduction and inference throughput.
    *   The method is practical as it is post-training quantization and does not require any retraining.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the overhead associated with Huffman decoding, especially in terms of compute resources. While the authors demonstrate improvements in throughput, a deeper dive into computational complexity would strengthen the argument.
    *   The evaluation is primarily focused on one edge device (Jetson P3450). Extending the evaluation to other platforms with different architectures would enhance the generalizability of the results.
    *   While the paper mentions CUDA kernels for managing non-standard bit formats, further details about the implementation and optimization of these kernels would be valuable.
    *   The novelty is somewhat incremental; although the integration is innovative, the individual components are well-established techniques.
*   **Impact:** The proposed method has the potential to enable wider adoption of LLMs on edge devices, making AI more accessible in resource-constrained environments. The practical nature of the approach (no retraining) increases its potential for real-world deployment.
*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper provides a practical solution with solid empirical results, its novelty is somewhat limited by the reliance on established techniques like Huffman coding. The implementation of the parallel decoding and CUDA kernels contributes to the innovations. The impact on the field, while promising, relies on the adaptability of the proposed methods on more widely available and diverse edge-computing platforms and is not directly observable.

### RM-R1: Reward Modeling as Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02387v1)
- **Authors**: Xiusi Chen, Gaotang Li, Ziqi Wang, Bowen Jin, Cheng Qian, Yu Wang, Hongru Wang, Yu Zhang, Denghui Zhang, Tong Zhang, Hanghang Tong, Heng Ji
- **Abstract**: Reward modeling is essential for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences, especially through reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF). To provide accurate reward signals, a reward model (RM) should stimulate deep thinking and conduct interpretable reasoning before assigning a score or a judgment. However, existing RMs either produce opaque scalar scores or directly generate the prediction of a preferred answer, making them struggle to integrate natural language critiques, thus lacking interpretability. Inspired by recent advances of long chain-of-thought (CoT) on reasoning-intensive tasks, we hypothesize and validate that integrating reasoning capabilities into reward modeling significantly enhances RM's interpretability and performance. In this work, we introduce a new class of generative reward models -- Reasoning Reward Models (ReasRMs) -- which formulate reward modeling as a reasoning task. We propose a reasoning-oriented training pipeline and train a family of ReasRMs, RM-R1. The training consists of two key stages: (1) distillation of high-quality reasoning chains and (2) reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. RM-R1 improves LLM rollouts by self-generating reasoning traces or chat-specific rubrics and evaluating candidate responses against them. Empirically, our models achieve state-of-the-art or near state-of-the-art performance of generative RMs across multiple comprehensive reward model benchmarks, outperforming much larger open-weight models (e.g., Llama3.1-405B) and proprietary ones (e.g., GPT-4o) by up to 13.8%. Beyond final performance, we perform thorough empirical analysis to understand the key ingredients of successful ReasRM training. To facilitate future research, we release six ReasRM models along with code and data at https://github.com/RM-R1-UIUC/RM-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "RM-R1: Reward Modeling as Reasoning" paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a new approach to reward modeling (RM) for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences, called Reasoning Reward Models (REASRMS).  The core idea is to formulate reward modeling as a reasoning task, rather than just a scalar score prediction or direct answer generation.  The authors propose a two-stage training pipeline for their REASRM model, RM-R1: 1) Distillation of high-quality reasoning chains from strong teacher models (like Claude and OpenAI) and 2) Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). This pipeline is designed to enable the model to generate interpretable reasoning traces (chain-of-thought) and/or chat-specific rubrics, which are then used to evaluate candidate responses. Empirical results demonstrate that RM-R1 achieves state-of-the-art or near state-of-the-art performance across several RM benchmarks, surpassing larger open-weight and proprietary models. The paper also includes extensive ablation studies and analyses to understand the critical ingredients of the training process. The authors also release code, models and data to foster future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific approach to integrating reasoning into the reward modeling process. Formulating reward modeling as a reasoning task and training a reward model to output rationales and rubrics, followed by comparing candidate outputs to the model's own reasoned output or rubrics, is a conceptually novel idea. The training pipeline, especially the two-stage distillation and RLVR, is also a distinctive contribution, as are the chain-of-rubrics formulation.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is tied to the growing importance of reward models in LLM alignment. Improving the interpretability and accuracy of RMs is crucial for building reliable and trustworthy LLMs. RM-R1 addresses two key limitations of existing RMs: the opacity of scalar-based models and the superficial reasoning of generative models. The empirical results showing RM-R1 outperforming larger models on established benchmarks demonstrate the practical value of the proposed approach. The detailed ablation studies offer valuable insights into the factors that contribute to successful REASRM training. The release of models and data further enhances the paper's potential impact by enabling other researchers to build upon their work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current reward modeling approaches and motivates the need for a more reasoning-driven framework.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The proposed REASRM and training pipeline are conceptually novel and well-executed.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of RM-R1 across multiple benchmarks, outperforming strong baselines.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The ablation studies and analyses provide valuable insights into the training process and the key factors that contribute to performance.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of code, models, and data promotes reproducibility and enables future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Teacher Models:** The distillation stage relies on strong teacher models (Claude and OpenAI), which introduces a dependency on closed-source APIs. This makes it harder to fully replicate the model for someone without such access.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Training REASRMS might be more computationally expensive than training simpler scalar-based or purely supervised RM, due to the two-stage training approach and reliance on chain of thought generation. However, the paper has shown superior data efficiency to counter this weakness to a large extent.
    *   **Generalizability:** While tested on multiple benchmarks, a more diverse range of tasks and scenarios would further strengthen the claim of generalizability.
*   **Justification of Score:**
I'm assigning a score of **8**.

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to reward modeling that addresses critical limitations of existing methods. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating significant performance gains over strong baselines. The thorough analysis provides valuable insights into the training process. The limitations, such as the dependence on strong teacher models and potential computational cost, are acknowledged and don't overshadow the significant contributions of the paper. The release of models and code will likely foster further research in this area. While the core ideas are interesting, I consider an 'exceptional' score (9 or 10) to need a higher level of breakthrough for the field. This paper provides an important step forward.

### Optimizing Chain-of-Thought Reasoners via Gradient Variance Minimization in Rejection Sampling and RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02391v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarui Yao, Yifan Hao, Hanning Zhang, Hanze Dong, Wei Xiong, Nan Jiang, Tong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in large language models (LLMs) can be formalized as a latent variable problem, where the model needs to generate intermediate reasoning steps. While prior approaches such as iterative reward-ranked fine-tuning (RAFT) have relied on such formulations, they typically apply uniform inference budgets across prompts, which fails to account for variability in difficulty and convergence behavior. This work identifies the main bottleneck in CoT training as inefficient stochastic gradient estimation due to static sampling strategies. We propose GVM-RAFT, a prompt-specific Dynamic Sample Allocation Strategy designed to minimize stochastic gradient variance under a computational budget constraint. The method dynamically allocates computational resources by monitoring prompt acceptance rates and stochastic gradient norms, ensuring that the resulting gradient variance is minimized. Our theoretical analysis shows that the proposed dynamic sampling strategy leads to accelerated convergence guarantees under suitable conditions. Experiments on mathematical reasoning show that GVM-RAFT achieves a 2-4x speedup and considerable accuracy improvements over vanilla RAFT. The proposed dynamic sampling strategy is general and can be incorporated into other reinforcement learning algorithms, such as GRPO, leading to similar improvements in convergence and test accuracy. Our code is available at https://github.com/RLHFlow/GVM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the inefficiency of uniform sampling strategies in Chain-of-Thought (CoT) training of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that standard Reward-Ranked Fine-Tuning (RAFT) and related methods apply inference budgets equally across prompts, failing to account for varying prompt difficulty and convergence behavior. The authors propose GVM-RAFT, a prompt-specific Dynamic Sample Allocation Strategy.  This strategy minimizes stochastic gradient variance under a computational budget constraint by dynamically allocating resources based on prompt acceptance rates and stochastic gradient norms.  Theoretical analysis suggests accelerated convergence under suitable conditions. Experiments on mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate 2-4x speedup and accuracy improvements over vanilla RAFT. The approach is generalized to other Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms like GRPO, yielding similar benefits.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the dynamic sampling strategy tailored to individual prompts based on both acceptance rates *and* gradient norms. While the idea of adaptive sampling is not entirely new, its application to the RAFT framework, with a focus on *gradient variance minimization* for CoT training, presents a worthwhile contribution. Prior RAFT-style works are primarily motivated by imitation learning via best-of-n sampling, lacking a principled variance reduction perspective. The approach is also somewhat novel in its theoretical underpinnings which are used to guide and justify the proposed budget allocation.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact CoT training and LLM fine-tuning. The identified bottleneck of inefficient stochastic gradient estimation is well-argued, and the proposed solution is practical and yields substantial performance gains. The ability to adapt to prompt difficulty and convergence behavior is a significant advantage over static sampling strategies, potentially leading to more efficient use of computational resources. The generalization to GRPO further enhances the significance. The reported 2-4x speedup in convergence and accuracy improvements can substantially reduce the time and cost associated with LLM fine-tuning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of current CoT training approaches.
    *   **Principled Approach:** GVM-RAFT is grounded in theoretical analysis (minimizing gradient variance) providing a strong justification for the algorithm.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The algorithm is readily implementable and demonstrates significant performance improvements in empirical evaluations.
    *   **Generalizability:** The applicability of the dynamic sampling strategy to different learning frameworks (RAFT and GRPO) strengthens the work.
    *   **Rigorous Experiments:** The experimental setup is well described, using established benchmarks and models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Theoretical Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on certain assumptions about smoothness and convexity of the loss function which may not always hold in practice. It is worthwhile to assess the sensitivity of the proposed methods under these assumptions.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of GVM-RAFT depends on the hyperparameters α and β, controlling the regularization strength. More discussion on choosing these hyperparameters, or methods to automatically tune them, would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** While the experiments cover mathematical reasoning tasks, it would be beneficial to evaluate the algorithm on other types of CoT reasoning (e.g., commonsense reasoning, question answering).
    *   **Ablation Studies could be improved** The paper presents a series of ablation experiments that explores different aspects of the proposed algorithms. More careful designs on the ablation experiments and clearer analysis on the results will help improve the persuasiveness of the claims.
    *   **Clarity of Writing** The presentation of results for baseline could be improved with more discussions and clearer descriptions.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work has the potential to become a standard technique in CoT training, particularly in scenarios where computational resources are limited.
    *   The dynamic sampling strategy could inspire new research directions in adaptive learning and resource allocation for LLMs.
    *   The theoretical analysis provides a valuable framework for understanding the limitations of current CoT training methods and for developing more efficient algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

While the idea of adaptive sampling has been explored in different contexts, the specific adaptation of this concept based on both acceptance rate and gradient norm within the RAFT framework for CoT reasoning is well-motivated and delivers tangible performance improvements. The theoretical justification and generalizability further enhance the paper's contribution. However, the theoretical assumptions, hyperparameter sensitivity, and experimental scope represent areas for improvement. Overall the paper proposes practical improvements to the RAFT-type methods with insightful justifications.

### Estimating Commonsense Scene Composition on Belief Scene Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02405v1)
- **Authors**: Mario A. V. Saucedo, Vignesh Kottayam Viswanathan, Christoforos Kanellakis, George Nikolakopoulos
- **Abstract**: This work establishes the concept of commonsense scene composition, with a focus on extending Belief Scene Graphs by estimating the spatial distribution of unseen objects. Specifically, the commonsense scene composition capability refers to the understanding of the spatial relationships among related objects in the scene, which in this article is modeled as a joint probability distribution for all possible locations of the semantic object class. The proposed framework includes two variants of a Correlation Information (CECI) model for learning probability distributions: (i) a baseline approach based on a Graph Convolutional Network, and (ii) a neuro-symbolic extension that integrates a spatial ontology based on Large Language Models (LLMs). Furthermore, this article provides a detailed description of the dataset generation process for such tasks. Finally, the framework has been validated through multiple runs on simulated data, as well as in a real-world indoor environment, demonstrating its ability to spatially interpret scenes across different room types.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "commonsense scene composition," aiming to enable robots to understand and predict the spatial arrangement of objects within a scene, even with partial information. This is modeled as a joint probability distribution over possible locations for semantic object classes within a Belief Scene Graph (BSG).  The core contribution is a novel Graph Convolutional Network (GCN) based model called CECI (Computation of Expectation based on Correlation Information) which comes in two flavors: a baseline approach (BASE) and a neuro-symbolic extension (BASE+ONT) that incorporates a spatial ontology derived from Large Language Models (LLMs). The paper details the creation of a dataset using Matterport3D, data augmentation techniques, and methods for encoding location information. The approach is validated through simulations and real-world experiments using a legged robot in an indoor environment, demonstrating the system's ability to spatially interpret scenes across different room types. The aim is to complete scene graphs using the probability distribution of unseen objects, allowing for more robust scene understanding and planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces the concept of commonsense scene composition estimation within Belief Scene Graphs.  The use of a GCN to predict the spatial distribution of unseen objects is a novel approach, especially when combined with a spatial ontology derived from LLMs. Datasets relating commonsense object positions within scenes for robots are lacking and hence the data generation methodology along with the generation of a spatial ontology based on LLM models contributes to the field.
*   **Significance:** This work has the potential to significantly improve robotic scene understanding and planning. By enabling robots to infer the location of unseen objects, it can enhance exploration, object search, and task completion in dynamic environments. The neuro-symbolic approach, combining data-driven learning with explicit knowledge from LLMs, offers a promising direction for robust and generalizable scene understanding.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of commonsense scene composition and its relevance to robotics.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The proposed CECI model, particularly the neuro-symbolic variant, is a novel and potentially effective solution.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:** The paper provides a comprehensive description of the dataset generation, data augmentation, and model architecture.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The evaluation includes both simulated and real-world experiments, demonstrating the feasibility of the approach.
    *   **Use of LLMs for Spatial Ontology:** Integrating a spatial ontology derived from LLMs is a valuable contribution, addressing the limitations of purely data-driven approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The reliance on the Matterport3D dataset might limit the generalizability of the model to different types of environments. The data augmentation helps but does not solve the problem completely.
    *   **Limited Real-World Evaluation:** While the real-world experiments are promising, the evaluation is limited to a single indoor environment. Further validation in more diverse and complex settings is needed.
    *   **Grid Resolution Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of using a fixed-size grid for scene representation, which affects the localization accuracy, especially in larger rooms. The authors provide suggestions for alternative implementations that can solve this limitation.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Evaluation:** The paper lacks a direct comparative evaluation against other existing methods for object localization or scene completion. This makes it difficult to assess the relative performance of the proposed approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in robotic scene understanding, particularly in areas such as:
    *   Scene completion and object search.
    *   Integration of LLMs with robotic perception.
    *   Development of more robust and generalizable scene representations.
    *   Neuro-symbolic approaches to robotics.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper introduces a novel concept and a promising approach to commonsense scene composition estimation. The use of a GCN and the integration of a spatial ontology derived from LLMs are significant contributions. The detailed methodology and experimental validation add to the paper's strengths. However, the limitations of the dataset dependency, limited real-world evaluation, lack of comparative experiments, and the grid resolution limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the paper presents a valuable contribution, further research and validation are needed to fully realize its potential impact. The score reflects the novelty and potential significance of the work while acknowledging its limitations and the need for further validation.

### Token Coordinated Prompt Attention is Needed for Visual Prompting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02406v1)
- **Authors**: Zichen Liu, Xu Zou, Gang Hua, Jiahuan Zhou
- **Abstract**: Visual prompting techniques are widely used to efficiently fine-tune pretrained Vision Transformers (ViT) by learning a small set of shared prompts for all tokens. However, existing methods overlook the unique roles of different tokens in conveying discriminative information and interact with all tokens using the same prompts, thereby limiting the representational capacity of ViT. This often leads to indistinguishable and biased prompt-extracted features, hindering performance. To address this issue, we propose a plug-and-play Token Coordinated Prompt Attention (TCPA) module, which assigns specific coordinated prompts to different tokens for attention-based interactions. Firstly, recognizing the distinct functions of CLS and image tokens-global information aggregation and local feature extraction, we disentangle the prompts into CLS Prompts and Image Prompts, which interact exclusively with CLS tokens and image tokens through attention mechanisms. This enhances their respective discriminative abilities. Furthermore, as different image tokens correspond to distinct image patches and contain diverse information, we employ a matching function to automatically assign coordinated prompts to individual tokens. This enables more precise attention interactions, improving the diversity and representational capacity of the extracted features. Extensive experiments across various benchmarks demonstrate that TCPA significantly enhances the diversity and discriminative power of the extracted features. The code is available at https://github.com/zhoujiahuan1991/ICML2025-TCPA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses limitations in existing visual prompting techniques for Vision Transformers (ViTs).  Current methods often apply the same learned prompts to all tokens, overlooking the different roles and information conveyed by CLS and image tokens. This results in biased feature extraction and limits representational capacity. The authors propose Token Coordinated Prompt Attention (TCPA), a plug-and-play module that assigns specific coordinated prompts to different tokens based on their roles.  TCPA disentangles prompts into CLS and Image prompts, interacts with corresponding tokens through attention mechanisms, and uses a matching function to assign coordinated prompts to individual image tokens, enhancing diversity and representational capacity.  Experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements in diversity and discriminative power of extracted features.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of assigning specific prompts to different tokens based on their function (CLS vs. image tokens) and individual image patch content is novel. The TCPA module's design, specifically the disentanglement of prompts and the matching function, offers a distinct contribution compared to existing methods like VPT, which use the same prompts for all tokens. The paper successfully bridges the gap in understanding the distinct role of tokens and using prompts intelligently.
*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a pertinent challenge in visual prompting. Existing methods lack diversity and comprehensive feature extraction due to the same prompts being used for all tokens. TCPA's ability to coordinate prompt attention can lead to improved performance in downstream tasks and more efficient utilization of ViTs. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning is an active area, and this work provides a meaningful advancement. The gains on multiple datasets bolster the significance of the contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The TCPA module is well-motivated with clear explanations of its design choices and objectives.
    *   The paper provides detailed mathematical formulations of the module and its components.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results are presented, demonstrating the effectiveness of TCPA across various benchmarks and in combination with existing methods.
    *   Ablation studies clearly showcase the contribution of individual components (R-TCPA and T-TCPA).
    *   The analysis of computational cost and hyper-parameter sensitivity is thorough.
    *   t-SNE visualization provides qualitative evidence for the improved feature diversity and separability.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experiments are comprehensive, further analysis of the learned prompts would strengthen the paper. Examining the characteristics of the prompts assigned to specific image regions could provide deeper insights into how TCPA is working.
    *   The paper could benefit from a discussion of limitations, such as the potential for increased complexity in tasks requiring fine-grained relationships between tokens (though the masking mechanism aims to mitigate such increases).
    *   Clarity in differentiating between the learnable indicator of prompts, and the prompts used at inference could improve understanding.

*   **Impact:** The paper's impact will depend on the adoption of TCPA by the community. The plug-and-play nature of the module and its ability to improve existing visual prompting techniques makes it promising. The paper could stimulate further research into token-specific prompt design and attention mechanisms in ViTs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to visual prompting. The TCPA module addresses a clear limitation in existing methods with a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated solution. While the analysis of learned prompts could be deeper, the comprehensive experiments, ablation studies, and visualization results provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of TCPA. Given its ability to improve existing techniques and its potential for broader adoption, I believe it will have a good impact on future research in this area.

### Task-Oriented Semantic Communication in Large Multimodal Models-based Vehicle Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02413v1)
- **Authors**: Baoxia Du, Hongyang Du, Dusit Niyato, Ruidong Li
- **Abstract**: Task-oriented semantic communication has emerged as a fundamental approach for enhancing performance in various communication scenarios. While recent advances in Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI), such as Large Language Models (LLMs), have been applied to semantic communication designs, the potential of Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we investigate an LMM-based vehicle AI assistant using a Large Language and Vision Assistant (LLaVA) and propose a task-oriented semantic communication framework to facilitate efficient interaction between users and cloud servers. To reduce computational demands and shorten response time, we optimize LLaVA's image slicing to selectively focus on areas of utmost interest to users. Additionally, we assess the importance of image patches by combining objective and subjective user attention, adjusting energy usage for transmitting semantic information. This strategy optimizes resource utilization, ensuring precise transmission of critical information. We construct a Visual Question Answering (VQA) dataset for traffic scenarios to evaluate effectiveness. Experimental results show that our semantic communication framework significantly increases accuracy in answering questions under the same channel conditions, performing particularly well in environments with poor Signal-to-Noise Ratios (SNR). Accuracy can be improved by 13.4% at an SNR of 12dB and 33.1% at 10dB, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a task-oriented semantic communication framework for large multimodal models (LMMs) in vehicle networks, specifically using LLaVA as the LMM. The aim is to improve the efficiency of communication and resource allocation while maintaining accuracy in answering user queries about traffic scenarios. The key contributions are: 1) a distributed architecture with the image encoder on the vehicle and the LLM on a cloud server, 2) an improved image feature extraction method using task-oriented slicing to reduce the number of visual tokens, and 3) a method for integrating objective and subjective attention to prioritize semantically important image features for transmission, dynamically allocating transmission power based on importance. The method is evaluated using a newly constructed Visual Question Answering (VQA) dataset for traffic scenarios. Experimental results show that the proposed framework improves accuracy, especially under low Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing techniques (semantic communication, LMMs, attention mechanisms) in a novel way specifically targeted for vehicle networks. The SM-based image slicing tailored to user queries and the fusion of objective and subjective attention for resource allocation seem reasonably novel. The development and use of a custom VQA dataset also adds some value. The paper goes a step beyond just using LLaVA and looks to improve its efficiency.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is significant. Deploying LMMs in resource-constrained environments like vehicles is challenging, and efficient communication strategies are crucial. The proposed solution has the potential to improve the performance and reliability of vehicle AI assistants in real-world scenarios, especially when network conditions are poor. The application of semantic communication specifically to LMMs for V2X is also a worthy contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the proposed framework.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach, especially under low SNR conditions. The improvement in accuracy compared to baseline methods is significant.
    *   The use of both objective and subjective attention helps to address potential biases in the models.
    *   A custom VQA dataset tailored to the task is useful for evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components of the framework (SM, saliency prediction, power allocation) are not particularly innovative in isolation, however the integration could be viewed as innovative.
    *   The improvement comes at the cost of increased model complexity due to the YOLO model. The computational requirements of SM Model needs to be compared to vanilla LLaVA to demonstrate that SM-based image slicing can improve efficiency.
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited to a single VQA dataset.
    *   The dataset construction and size are adequate but could be improved by increasing the number of questions and traffic scenarios.
    *   The description of the channel model is brief and lacks detail.  The impact of different fading and shadowing parameters on the results could be explored further.
    *   The study does not compare against other baselines.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in the area of efficient LMM deployment for vehicular applications. The attention fusion and resource allocation strategies could be adapted for other modalities and tasks. The VQA dataset could serve as a benchmark for evaluating similar approaches.

*   **Clarity and Writing Quality:** The paper is well-written and relatively easy to understand. The figures and tables are clear and informative.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper does provide a reasonably novel framework for LMM deployment in V2X networks, the overall contribution is not as substantial.  The use of well-established techniques, limited experimental dataset, and some missing analyses restrain the score from being higher.  The potential for improving accuracy with low SNR and the framework structure merit a score above average.

### T2S: High-resolution Time Series Generation with Text-to-Series Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02417v1)
- **Authors**: Yunfeng Ge, Jiawei Li, Yiji Zhao, Haomin Wen, Zhao Li, Meikang Qiu, Hongyan Li, Ming Jin, Shirui Pan
- **Abstract**: Text-to-Time Series generation holds significant potential to address challenges such as data sparsity, imbalance, and limited availability of multimodal time series datasets across domains. While diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in Text-to-X (e.g., vision and audio data) generation, their use in time series generation remains in its nascent stages. Existing approaches face two critical limitations: (1) the lack of systematic exploration of general-proposed time series captions, which are often domain-specific and struggle with generalization; and (2) the inability to generate time series of arbitrary lengths, limiting their applicability to real-world scenarios. In this work, we first categorize time series captions into three levels: point-level, fragment-level, and instance-level. Additionally, we introduce a new fragment-level dataset containing over 600,000 high-resolution time series-text pairs. Second, we propose Text-to-Series (T2S), a diffusion-based framework that bridges the gap between natural language and time series in a domain-agnostic manner. T2S employs a length-adaptive variational autoencoder to encode time series of varying lengths into consistent latent embeddings. On top of that, T2S effectively aligns textual representations with latent embeddings by utilizing Flow Matching and employing Diffusion Transformer as the denoiser. We train T2S in an interleaved paradigm across multiple lengths, allowing it to generate sequences of any desired length. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that T2S achieves state-of-the-art performance across 13 datasets spanning 12 domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces "T2S: High-resolution Time Series Generation with Text-to-Series Diffusion Models." It addresses the limitations of existing time series generation (TSG) approaches, specifically the lack of general-purpose captions and the inability to generate time series of arbitrary lengths. The authors categorize time series captions into point-level, fragment-level, and instance-level and introduce a new fragment-level dataset, TSFragment-600K. They propose a diffusion-based framework, T2S, which uses a length-adaptive variational autoencoder (LA-VAE) and Flow Matching to align textual representations with latent embeddings. T2S is trained in an interleaved paradigm, allowing it to generate sequences of any desired length. The authors claim state-of-the-art performance across 13 datasets.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in time series generation: the lack of high-quality, general-purpose text-time series datasets and models capable of handling arbitrary lengths.
    *   **Novel Dataset:** The introduction of TSFragment-600K is a valuable contribution. The dataset fills a void in fragment-level descriptions, enabling more nuanced and context-aware time series generation.
    *   **Technical Innovation:** The proposed T2S framework integrates several techniques (LA-VAE, Flow Matching, Diffusion Transformer) in a novel way for the specific task of T2S generation. The length-adaptive component is a clear improvement over existing fixed-length methods. The interleaved training approach appears to be effective in handling datasets of varying lengths.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across a wide range of datasets and comparison with strong baselines, including diffusion-based models and large language models.
    *   **The combination of LA-VAE, Flow Matching, and the Diffusion Transformer is new, which could open possibilities for other downstream tasks.**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Generation Concerns:** While TSFragment-600K is a key contribution, the use of GPT-40-mini for caption generation raises some concerns. The potential biases and limitations of the language model could be reflected in the generated captions, potentially affecting the quality and diversity of the dataset. A detailed analysis of the dataset's characteristics, including potential biases and limitations, would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The individual components of T2S (LA-VAE, Flow Matching, Diffusion Transformer) are not entirely novel. The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific combination and adaptation of these techniques for text-to-time series generation. The extent of the adaptation and optimization for this specific task should be highlighted.
    *   **Lack of In-depth Analysis of Ablation Studies**: The ablation study mentions a general performance drop but doesn't go into detail. It should contain an in-depth analysis of the differences in WAPE when altering the different model settings.
    *   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis**: The paper relies on quantitative evaluations. This is a substantial oversight, given the subjective nature of captioning and time-series generation. The inclusion of qualitative examples of time series generated by T2S, along with their corresponding captions, would significantly strengthen the paper. This can give the reader a much better overview of the T2S's performance, and highlight its strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Limited Scope of Application**: The paper mainly focuses on generating univariate time series. While this is a valid starting point, many real-world applications involve multivariate time series. Addressing the generation of multivariate time series would increase the practical relevance of the research.
    *   **Zero-Shot Performance Concerns**: The paper uses Llama-3 and GPT-4 as zero-shot baselines. The zero-shot LLM methods might be inconsistent when generating time series. Furthermore, this has to be manually prompted, which means that this is not an automated process, and could introduce human errors.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Potential Impact:** The ability to generate high-quality time series data from textual descriptions has numerous potential applications, including data augmentation, anomaly detection, simulation, and creative content generation.
    *   **Community Resource:** The TSFragment-600K dataset will likely become a valuable resource for the time series generation community, fostering further research in this area.
    *   **Advancing the Field:** The T2S framework provides a strong foundation for future research in text-to-time series generation. The integration of different techniques and the length-adaptive component represent a step forward in the field.

**Overall Score and Justification**

I assign a score of **7/10**. The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing a relevant problem and providing a novel dataset and framework. The strengths of the paper lie in its comprehensive evaluation and the integration of several techniques into a cohesive system. However, the concerns regarding dataset generation, incremental novelty, and limited qualitative analysis, multivariate application, and zero-shot performance somewhat temper its impact. The paper presents a solid piece of work with the potential to influence future research, but it is not without its limitations. With further refinement, particularly in qualitative validation, analysis of potential dataset biases, and extensions to handle multivariate time series, the paper could achieve even greater significance.

### Can LLM-Simulated Practice and Feedback Upskill Human Counselors? A Randomized Study with 90+ Novice Counselors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02428v1)
- **Authors**: Ryan Louie, Ifdita Hasan Orney, Juan Pablo Pacheco, Raj Sanjay Shah, Emma Brunskill, Diyi Yang
- **Abstract**: Training more counselors, from clinical students to peer supporters, can help meet the demand for accessible mental health support; however, current training approaches remain resource-intensive and difficult to scale effectively. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promising solutions for growing counseling skills training through simulated practice and automated feedback. Despite successes in aligning LLMs with expert-counselor annotations, we do not know whether LLM-based counseling training tools -- such as AI patients that simulate real-world challenges and generative AI feedback with suggested alternatives and rationales -- actually lead to improvements in novice counselor skill development. We develop CARE, an LLM-simulated practice and feedback system, and randomize 94 novice counselors to practice using an AI patient, either alone or with AI feedback, measuring changes in their behavioral performance, self-assessments, and qualitative learning takeaways. Our results show the practice-and-feedback group improved in their use of reflections and questions (d=0.32-0.39, p$<$0.05). In contrast, the group that practiced with an AI patient alone did not show improvements, and in the case of empathy, actually had worse uses across time (d=$-$0.52, p=0.001) and when compared against the practice-and-feedback group (d=0.72, p=0.001). Participants' qualitative self-reflections revealed key differences: the practice-and-feedback group adopted a client-centered approach involving listening to and validating feelings, while the practice-alone group remained solution-oriented but delayed offering suggestions until gathering more information. Overall, these results suggest that LLM-based training systems can promote effective skill development, but that combining both simulated practice and structured feedback is critical.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Can LLM-Simulated Practice and Feedback Upskill Human Counselors? A Randomized Study with 90+ Novice Counselors."

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the effectiveness of using Large Language Models (LLMs) to train novice counselors. The study randomly assigned 94 participants to either practice counseling with an LLM-simulated patient alone or with an LLM-simulated patient and AI-generated feedback. The researchers then measured changes in the participants' behavioral performance (via LLM-based transcript analysis), self-efficacy, and qualitative self-reflections.  The results show that practice with LLM-generated feedback led to significant improvements in the use of reflections and questions compared to practice alone.  Furthermore, practice alone may have even *hindered* the development of empathy. Qualitative data suggested that the feedback group adopted a more client-centered approach while the practice-alone group remained solution-oriented. The study concludes that LLM-based training systems can be effective, but structured feedback is critical for promoting the development of core counseling skills.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous methodology:** The paper utilizes a randomized controlled trial (RCT) design, which is strong for establishing causal relationships. The relatively large sample size (N=94) increases the statistical power.
    * **Multi-faceted evaluation:** The study employs a comprehensive evaluation approach, incorporating behavioral assessments (LLM transcript analysis), self-reported measures (self-efficacy), and qualitative insights (self-reflections). This provides a holistic view of the impact of the intervention.
    * **Clear results:** The findings are relatively clear and compelling, showing a distinct benefit of incorporating AI feedback in the LLM-based training setting.  The negative impact on empathy from practice alone is a notable finding.
    * **Practical significance:** Addressing the shortage of mental health professionals is an important problem. The paper offers a potentially scalable training solution.
    * **Nuance:** The authors acknowledge the importance of how AI systems are built, acknowledging how the algorithms themselves could also hinder progress. They also acknowledge that the AI is not intended as a replacement for, but as a supplement to.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Novice population:** The study focuses on novice counselors with limited prior training.  It's unclear whether the findings generalize to more experienced therapists. Further, their method of recruiting users on Prolific might not be representative of a counseling student group.
    * **Short-term intervention:** The training intervention was relatively short (20 minutes). The long-term effectiveness and skill retention remain unknown.
    * **Limited generalizability of LLM feedback:** The LLM was fine-tuned on expert-annotated data for counseling, this may be limited to this scenario.
    * **Limited scope of skills assessed:** The study primarily assesses a subset of core counseling skills (empathy, reflections, questions). Other important skills, such as advanced empathy, therapeutic alliance, and intervention-specific techniques, were not evaluated.
    * **Potential for biases in LLM-based assessments:** While the authors report high accuracy for the LLM classifiers, there is always a risk of systematic biases in these automated assessments that could affect the interpretation of results.
    * **Measurement of true empathy:** It is not certain that this accurately measure true empathy, as it could incentivize formulaic responses without truly understanding a patient's problem.
    * **Limited exploration of AI feedback design:** The study only evaluated one specific type of LLM feedback. Other feedback mechanisms (e.g., real-time guidance, counterfactual simulations) could potentially be more effective.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its rigorous evaluation of an *end-to-end* LLM-based training system for novice counselors, including both realistic patient simulations and structured AI feedback, and showing its effectiveness on promoting skill development. While previous studies have explored AI in counselor training, this paper is among the first to demonstrate that a generative LLM with feedback, not merely practice, can be beneficial for upskilling human counselors in core listening skills. The finding that AI-based practice without feedback may *hinder* the development of empathy is a significant and cautionary result.

**Score Rationale:**

The study provides a significant contribution to the growing field of AI-assisted mental health training, particularly through its demonstration of the value of AI-based feedback and recognition of potential pitfalls of unguided LLM-based practice. While several limitations warrant caution, the study’s strong methodology and practical implications justify a relatively high score.
