- **Score**: 7/10

### Predicting the Dynamics of Complex System via Multiscale Diffusion Autoencoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02450v1)
- **Authors**: Ruikun Li, Jingwen Cheng, Huandong Wang, Qingmin Liao, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Predicting the dynamics of complex systems is crucial for various scientific and engineering applications. The accuracy of predictions depends on the model's ability to capture the intrinsic dynamics. While existing methods capture key dynamics by encoding a low-dimensional latent space, they overlook the inherent multiscale structure of complex systems, making it difficult to accurately predict complex spatiotemporal evolution. Therefore, we propose a Multiscale Diffusion Prediction Network (MDPNet) that leverages the multiscale structure of complex systems to discover the latent space of intrinsic dynamics. First, we encode multiscale features through a multiscale diffusion autoencoder to guide the diffusion model for reliable reconstruction. Then, we introduce an attention-based graph neural ordinary differential equation to model the co-evolution across different scales. Extensive evaluations on representative systems demonstrate that the proposed method achieves an average prediction error reduction of 53.23% compared to baselines, while also exhibiting superior robustness and generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MDPNet, a Multiscale Diffusion Prediction Network, for predicting the dynamics of complex systems.  MDPNet addresses the challenge of capturing the inherent multiscale structure of complex systems, which existing methods often overlook. The approach uses a multiscale diffusion autoencoder to encode and guide reconstruction, capturing features at different scales.  An attention-based graph neural ordinary differential equation (GNODE) then models the co-evolution of these features across scales. Experimental results on several benchmark systems demonstrate improved prediction accuracy compared to baseline methods, along with enhanced robustness and generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques to address a well-known problem.  Using a diffusion autoencoder to explicitly learn multiscale representations is a solid idea, and combining it with a graph neural ODE for modeling cross-scale interactions seems logical.  The multiscale residual encoder is a good adaptation of ideas from image representation. However, the individual components (diffusion models, graph neural ODEs, autoencoders) are not novel in themselves; the contribution is in their synergistic integration within the MDPNet framework. Multiscale modelling has been explored in other works (e.g., those cited in the introduction), and the explicit combination of diffusion models and latent dynamics learning is also gaining traction, though MDPNet proposes a more structured approach. Thus, the novelty is moderate rather than revolutionary.

*Significance:*

Predicting the dynamics of complex systems is a fundamental problem with broad applicability. Improving prediction accuracy and robustness, as demonstrated by the paper, has practical value. The paper's emphasis on capturing multiscale structure aligns with the understanding that many real-world systems exhibit dynamics at different scales, thereby enhancing practical relevance. The experiments show a clear performance gain over existing methods across several datasets, which increases the paper's significance. However, the specific datasets (Lambda-Omega, Brusselator, Gray-Scott, Navier-Stokes) are somewhat canonical and might not fully represent the breadth of complex systems where multiscale phenomena are prominent. Additionally, while the results are compelling, a more thorough analysis of the method's computational cost relative to its performance gains would strengthen the significance argument. Given recent advancements in diffusion-based modelling, the reported performance improvements may become less significant in the near future as the baseline methods improve.

*Strengths:*

*   The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivation.
*   The method is well-described with sufficient details.
*   The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering several datasets and baselines.
*   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components of MDPNet.
*   The robustness analysis demonstrates that the model is relatively robust to noisy data and data scarcity.

*Weaknesses:*

*   The novelty of the individual components is limited.
*   While the datasets are standard, they are relatively small and might not fully reflect real-world complexity.
*   The computational cost analysis, while presented, could be more rigorous. Specifically, benchmarking against the other proposed approaches would strengthen the paper.
*   The lack of discussion concerning the computational scalability of the proposed approach makes it difficult to assess its real-world applicability.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence research on dynamics prediction by highlighting the importance of multiscale modeling and by providing a concrete framework for integrating diffusion models with latent dynamics learning. However, the impact will depend on the community's adoption of MDPNet and its adaptation to more complex and realistic systems. The paper's influence will also depend on whether subsequent work can address the computational cost issues and demonstrate scalability.

Score: 7

*Rationale:*

The paper provides a solid contribution to the field by proposing and demonstrating an effective method for predicting complex system dynamics using a multiscale approach. The method's integration of existing techniques is well-executed, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the relatively limited novelty of the individual components and the lack of thorough consideration for computational cost prevent a higher score. The paper has good potential for influence, but this potential remains to be demonstrated in future research and applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Colombian Waitresses y Jueces canadienses: Gender and Country Biases in Occupation Recommendations from LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02456v1)
- **Authors**: Elisa Forcada Rodríguez, Olatz Perez-de-Viñaspre, Jon Ander Campos, Dietrich Klakow, Vagrant Gautam
- **Abstract**: One of the goals of fairness research in NLP is to measure and mitigate stereotypical biases that are propagated by NLP systems. However, such work tends to focus on single axes of bias (most often gender) and the English language. Addressing these limitations, we contribute the first study of multilingual intersecting country and gender biases, with a focus on occupation recommendations generated by large language models. We construct a benchmark of prompts in English, Spanish and German, where we systematically vary country and gender, using 25 countries and four pronoun sets. Then, we evaluate a suite of 5 Llama-based models on this benchmark, finding that LLMs encode significant gender and country biases. Notably, we find that even when models show parity for gender or country individually, intersectional occupational biases based on both country and gender persist. We also show that the prompting language significantly affects bias, and instruction-tuned models consistently demonstrate the lowest and most stable levels of bias. Our findings highlight the need for fairness researchers to use intersectional and multilingual lenses in their work.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates gender and country biases in occupation recommendations generated by large language models (LLMs). It constructs a multilingual benchmark (English, Spanish, German) systematically varying the country of origin, gender (using pronoun sets), and ground country (current location). Five Llama-based models are evaluated, revealing significant gender and country biases, including intersectional biases that persist even when individual axes seem balanced. The study finds that the prompting language significantly affects bias, with instruction-tuned models demonstrating the lowest levels. The authors argue for the necessity of intersectional and multilingual approaches in fairness research within NLP.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is the multilingual investigation of *intersectional* country and gender biases in occupation recommendations. While prior work has examined gender biases and some work has considered individual country biases or multilingual settings, the combination is relatively novel. This moves beyond the typical focus on single axes (gender) and English, addressing a real gap in the fairness literature.
*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications. The demonstration that intersectional biases can persist even when individual biases seem mitigated is a crucial insight. The influence of prompting language is also a significant factor that needs more attention in fairness evaluations. The study's findings underscore the limitations of narrow bias assessments and the importance of considering complex interactions between sociodemographic factors across languages.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The systematic construction of the multilingual benchmark with controlled variation of countries, genders, and ground countries demonstrates methodological rigor. The use of multiple models (Llama family) and prompts strengthens the robustness of the findings.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, with clear explanations of the methodology, results, and limitations. The examples in Figure 1 effectively illustrate the intersectional biases.
    *   **Relevance:** The topic is highly relevant given the increasing deployment of LLMs in real-world applications, particularly those related to employment and career advice. The potential for these systems to perpetuate harmful stereotypes is a significant concern.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of code and prompts enhances reproducibility and encourages further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Family:** The focus on Llama-based models is a limitation. While Llama is a popular family, the findings may not generalize to other architectures (e.g., GPT, PaLM). Evaluating a wider range of models would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Limited Ground Countries:** While 25 origin countries were assessed, testing only 5 ground countries reduces the generalization scope of the analysis. The choice of ground countries, mainly Western, could also impact the observed biases.
    *   **Clustering Method:** While supervised clustering was preferred, its reliability depends on the few-shot examples. Errors in this clustering could affect the quantitative metrics. The discussion surrounding the limitations of only using the clustering technique is not detailed enough, for example the potential for clustering to combine several job titles that are distinct which may lead to an under- or overestimation of certain biases.
    *   **Bias Definition:** The definition of 'ideal' or 'unbiased' behavior as equally distributed classes is a simplification. A more nuanced discussion of alternative fairness definitions and their implications is needed. The authors acknowledge this, but could have offered alternative fairness perspectives.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence future research in fairness and bias within NLP. It provides a strong argument for intersectional, multilingual evaluations and highlights the importance of instruction tuning and prompt engineering for mitigating biases in LLMs. The reusable framework and code release facilitate further exploration in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the limitations, the paper presents a valuable and timely contribution. The multilingual, intersectional approach is novel and the findings regarding the persistence of biases, the effect of prompting language, and the benefits of instruction-tuning are significant. The paper fills a gap in the fairness literature and provides a solid foundation for future research. The main limitation, which hinders a score in the higher range, is limiting model architectures to the Llama family.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Incentivizing Inclusive Contributions in Model Sharing Markets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02462v1)
- **Authors**: Enpei Zhang, Jingyi Chai, Rui Ye, Yanfeng Wang, Siheng Chen
- **Abstract**: While data plays a crucial role in training contemporary AI models, it is acknowledged that valuable public data will be exhausted in a few years, directing the world's attention towards the massive decentralized private data. However, the privacy-sensitive nature of raw data and lack of incentive mechanism prevent these valuable data from being fully exploited. Addressing these challenges, this paper proposes inclusive and incentivized personalized federated learning (iPFL), which incentivizes data holders with diverse purposes to collaboratively train personalized models without revealing raw data. iPFL constructs a model-sharing market by solving a graph-based training optimization and incorporates an incentive mechanism based on game theory principles. Theoretical analysis shows that iPFL adheres to two key incentive properties: individual rationality and truthfulness. Empirical studies on eleven AI tasks (e.g., large language models' instruction-following tasks) demonstrate that iPFL consistently achieves the highest economic utility, and better or comparable model performance compared to baseline methods. We anticipate that our iPFL can serve as a valuable technique for boosting future AI models on decentralized private data while making everyone satisfied.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper and provide a critical evaluation, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "inclusive and incentivized Personalized Federated Learning (iPFL)," a framework designed to motivate participation and facilitate collaboration in model sharing markets using decentralized private data. iPFL addresses the challenge of incentivizing institutions to share their privacy-sensitive data by creating a model-sharing market. The key innovations include a graph-based training optimization to construct the market and a game theory-based incentive mechanism ensuring individual rationality and truthfulness.  The authors demonstrate through theoretical analysis and empirical evaluations on diverse AI tasks (including LLM instruction-following) that iPFL achieves high economic utility and comparable or superior model performance compared to existing methods.  The authors argue that iPFL provides a viable approach for leveraging decentralized private data for training future AI models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in combining personalized federated learning with a game-theoretic incentive mechanism tailored for a model-sharing market.  While PFL and game-theoretic incentives have been explored separately, their integration in this specific context, emphasizing inclusivity and accommodating diverse participant preferences, is a valuable contribution. The use of a graph-based approach for training optimization further enhances the novelty. However, the individual components are well-established, so the novelty is primarily in the specific combination and application.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of iPFL is significant. The paper directly addresses a critical bottleneck in the advancement of AI: access to diverse and valuable private data in a privacy-preserving and incentivized manner. As public datasets are exhausted, solutions like iPFL become increasingly important for sustaining AI progress. The paper is strengthened by its empirical results, demonstrating the practical benefits of the framework on a range of AI tasks, including those related to large language models. The promise of higher economic utility and better performance compared to baselines will resonate with potential users of the system.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of limited access to private data and the need for incentive mechanisms.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** iPFL provides a well-defined framework with a novel combination of techniques, including graph-based optimization and game-theoretic incentives.
    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** The paper offers theoretical analysis of key incentive properties like individual rationality and truthfulness, lending credibility to the framework.
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:**  The extensive experiments across diverse datasets and tasks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of iPFL. The inclusion of LLM instruction-following tasks is particularly relevant.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The framework addresses a real-world challenge and provides a pathway for organizations to participate in collaborative AI development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is fairly complex, involving several components and parameters.  Implementation and deployment could be challenging for practitioners without expertise in federated learning, game theory, and graph algorithms.  Scalability may also be an issue, although not explicitly addressed in the paper.
    *   **Assumptions:** The assumptions regarding static data, economic needs, and participant willingness throughout the training process are limiting. The paper acknowledges these limitations, but they still impact the real-world applicability of the system.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Attacker Models:** While the paper discusses robustness against model poisoning attacks, a more detailed analysis of various attack strategies and defenses would strengthen the paper. The simulation of attackers is rudimentary.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of PFL and game theory is novel, the individual techniques used are not groundbreaking.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in federated learning, privacy-preserving AI, and incentive mechanism design. It could spur further exploration of game-theoretic approaches for PFL and the development of more robust and scalable model-sharing markets. The empirical results can also serve as a benchmark for future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**.

The score reflects the paper's valuable contribution to a critical area of AI research by effectively integrating personalized federated learning with game-theoretic incentives in a novel way. The comprehensive empirical evaluation and theoretical guarantees are particularly commendable. However, the limitations regarding complexity, assumptions, and the incremental nature of the technical contributions prevent a higher score. While the novelty is not revolutionary, the successful combination and application of existing techniques in this context are significant enough to merit a high rating. The paper's potential influence on future research is also a key factor in the assigned score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tevatron 2.0: Unified Document Retrieval Toolkit across Scale, Language, and Modality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02466v1)
- **Authors**: Xueguang Ma, Luyu Gao, Shengyao Zhuang, Jiaqi Samantha Zhan, Jamie Callan, Jimmy Lin
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have driven interest in billion-scale retrieval models with strong generalization across retrieval tasks and languages. Additionally, progress in large vision-language models has created new opportunities for multimodal retrieval. In response, we have updated the Tevatron toolkit, introducing a unified pipeline that enables researchers to explore retriever models at different scales, across multiple languages, and with various modalities. This demo paper highlights the toolkit's key features, bridging academia and industry by supporting efficient training, inference, and evaluation of neural retrievers. We showcase a unified dense retriever achieving strong multilingual and multimodal effectiveness, and conduct a cross-modality zero-shot study to demonstrate its research potential. Alongside, we release OmniEmbed, to the best of our knowledge, the first embedding model that unifies text, image document, video, and audio retrieval, serving as a baseline for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Tevatron 2.0 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tevatron 2.0, an updated toolkit designed for unified document retrieval across scale, language, and modality.  It addresses challenges associated with training large-scale retrieval models, handling multimodal data, and optimizing inference efficiency. Key contributions include a new unified data format that decouples queries from the corpus, facilitating multimodal training; integration of techniques like LoRA, ZeRO, and FlashAttention for GPU memory efficiency; integration of VLLM for faster encoding speeds; and a demonstrated capability to train a unified dense retriever effective across multilingual and multimodal retrieval tasks. The authors release the OmniEmbed model and converted multiple open-source datasets into a unified format for ease of use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is incremental but valuable. While individual components (LoRA, VLLM, ZeRO) are well-established, their combination within a single, extensible retrieval toolkit targeting multimodality and multilingualism represents a useful engineering contribution. The unified data format and the release of OmniEmbed also adds some new functionality, but it's building on existing foundations. The cross-modality zero-shot experiments demonstrate the practical utility, highlighting interesting observations regarding textual vs multimodal adaptation.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in making state-of-the-art techniques more accessible to researchers with varying levels of computational resources. By addressing GPU memory constraints and inference efficiency, Tevatron 2.0 can lower the barrier to entry for experimenting with large retrieval models. The unified data format and provided datasets should also facilitate research in cross-modal retrieval, a promising but often complex area.  The unified pipeline for training, inference, and evaluation is a significant plus.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practicality:**  The paper focuses on practical solutions to real-world challenges in retrieval research.
*   **Comprehensive Integration:**  Tevatron 2.0 integrates several best practices (LoRA, ZeRO, FlashAttention, VLLM) in a unified framework.
*   **Extensibility:**  The modular design and emphasis on flexibility make it easy to incorporate new techniques.
*   **Open Source and Reproducibility:**  The toolkit is open-sourced, and the unified datasets contribute to reproducibility.
*   **Demonstrated Capabilities:** The experiments clearly showcase the effectiveness of Tevatron 2.0 across different dimensions (scale, language, modality).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The individual techniques integrated into the toolkit are not novel on their own.
*   **Limited Analysis:**  While the results are presented, the discussion of the model's limitations and potential failure cases is relatively limited.  A deeper error analysis would strengthen the paper.
*   **Dependency on existing LLMs:** The Toolkit depends on having an existing LLM. Training or adaptation of this LLM is not a primary focus.

**Potential Influence:**

Tevatron 2.0 has the potential to become a popular tool within the information retrieval community, particularly for researchers working on multimodal and multilingual retrieval. Its accessibility and ease of use could facilitate further advancements in these areas.

**Justification for Score:**

While Tevatron 2.0 doesn't introduce groundbreaking theoretical advancements, its practical utility, comprehensive integration of existing techniques, and focus on accessibility make it a valuable contribution to the field. The toolkit lowers the barrier to entry for research on large-scale retrieval models and promotes reproducibility. The focus on multilingual and multimodal aspects aligns with important trends in the field. The novelty is incremental, but the overall package is significant.

### Ming-Lite-Uni: Advancements in Unified Architecture for Natural Multimodal Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02471v1)
- **Authors**: Biao Gong, Cheng Zou, Dandan Zheng, Hu Yu, Jingdong Chen, Jianxin Sun, Junbo Zhao, Jun Zhou, Kaixiang Ji, Lixiang Ru, Libin Wang, Qingpei Guo, Rui Liu, Weilong Chai, Xinyu Xiao, Ziyuan Huang
- **Abstract**: We introduce Ming-Lite-Uni, an open-source multimodal framework featuring a newly designed unified visual generator and a native multimodal autoregressive model tailored for unifying vision and language. Specifically, this project provides an open-source implementation of the integrated MetaQueries and M2-omni framework, while introducing the novel multi-scale learnable tokens and multi-scale representation alignment strategy. By leveraging a fixed MLLM and a learnable diffusion model, Ming-Lite-Uni enables native multimodal AR models to perform both text-to-image generation and instruction based image editing tasks, expanding their capabilities beyond pure visual understanding. Our experimental results demonstrate the strong performance of Ming-Lite-Uni and illustrate the impressive fluid nature of its interactive process. All code and model weights are open-sourced to foster further exploration within the community. Notably, this work aligns with concurrent multimodal AI milestones - such as ChatGPT-4o with native image generation updated in March 25, 2025 - underscoring the broader significance of unified models like Ming-Lite-Uni on the path toward AGI. Ming-Lite-Uni is in alpha stage and will soon be further refined.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Ming-Lite-Uni, an open-source multimodal framework designed to unify vision and language processing. It features a unified visual generator and a native multimodal autoregressive model. The key contributions include:

1.  An open-source implementation of integrated MetaQueries and M2-omni frameworks.
2.  Novel multi-scale learnable tokens.
3.  Multi-scale representation alignment strategy.

Ming-Lite-Uni utilizes a fixed MLLM and a learnable diffusion model, enabling it to perform text-to-image generation and instruction-based image editing. The authors open-source the code and model weights and claim strong performance and interactive capabilities. They contextualize their work within the broader landscape of unified multimodal AI models, such as ChatGPT-4o.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is mixed.  While integrating MetaQueries and M2-omni and introducing multi-scale learnable tokens and representation alignment are contributions, the incremental nature is a concern. The use of existing MLLMs and diffusion models reduces the core innovation.  The paper claims alignment with ChatGPT-4o, implying they are similarly tackling native image generation. This is not particularly novel; many are working on this and the key metric would be the model's performance in generating images.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies primarily in open-sourcing the framework, which can foster community exploration and improvement. The framework's ability to perform text-to-image and image editing tasks using a unified model is a positive. It claims to outperform other similar unified models. The lack of a thorough experimental evaluation in this version limits an independent assessment of its actual performance and significance. A critical weakness is the absence of ablation studies that isolates the effect of the different design choices (e.g., multi-scale learnable tokens or the multi-scale alignment), and no discussion about the architecture's computational efficiency.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Open-source implementation.
    *   Unified architecture for multiple tasks.
    *   Good preliminary experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental novelty; relies heavily on existing components (MLLM, diffusion models).
    *   Limited experimental evaluation in this version; claims are not thoroughly validated, with a future experimental evaluation.
    *   Lack of ablation studies and efficiency analysis.
    *   Overclaims of being similar to ChatGPT-4o.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the claims hold true and the model does perform well, it can contribute to the community by improving the development of new multimodal applications due to it being open-source. The multi-scale representation alignment strategy could become a commonly used way to ensure consistency, if it works as claimed.

**Justification for Score:**

The score reflects the paper's incremental novelty and lack of rigorous experimental validation. While the open-source aspect is a significant strength, the paper needs a more robust experimental section and deeper technical innovations to warrant a higher score. The claim of good performance needs to be backed up more rigorously.

### Automated Hybrid Reward Scheduling via Large Language Models for Robotic Skill Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02483v1)
- **Authors**: Changxin Huang, Junyang Liang, Yanbin Chang, Jingzhao Xu, Jianqiang Li
- **Abstract**: Enabling a high-degree-of-freedom robot to learn specific skills is a challenging task due to the complexity of robotic dynamics. Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising solution; however, addressing such problems requires the design of multiple reward functions to account for various constraints in robotic motion. Existing approaches typically sum all reward components indiscriminately to optimize the RL value function and policy. We argue that this uniform inclusion of all reward components in policy optimization is inefficient and limits the robot's learning performance. To address this, we propose an Automated Hybrid Reward Scheduling (AHRS) framework based on Large Language Models (LLMs). This paradigm dynamically adjusts the learning intensity of each reward component throughout the policy optimization process, enabling robots to acquire skills in a gradual and structured manner. Specifically, we design a multi-branch value network, where each branch corresponds to a distinct reward component. During policy optimization, each branch is assigned a weight that reflects its importance, and these weights are automatically computed based on rules designed by LLMs. The LLM generates a rule set in advance, derived from the task description, and during training, it selects a weight calculation rule from the library based on language prompts that evaluate the performance of each branch. Experimental results demonstrate that the AHRS method achieves an average 6.48% performance improvement across multiple high-degree-of-freedom robotic tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes an Automated Hybrid Reward Scheduling (AHRS) framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to dynamically adjust reward component weights during reinforcement learning for robotic skill acquisition.  AHRS decomposes the reward function into components, uses a multi-branch value network where each branch learns the value of one reward component, and utilizes LLMs to generate and select appropriate weight calculation rules for each branch. These rules are chosen based on the current performance of the agent, as evaluated by language prompts to the LLM. The method also includes a LLM-generated auxiliary reward function to enhance skill acquisition. Experiments across various robotic tasks show that AHRS improves performance compared to PPO and a manually designed dynamic policy gradient method (HDPG).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the automated generation and scheduling of reward weights using LLMs.  While reward shaping and hybrid reward architectures exist, the use of LLMs to dynamically adapt reward component importance based on policy performance during training is a relatively novel approach. The idea of constructing a weight rule repository is also interesting. However, similar concepts of automating the RL pipeline are emerging in other works that incorporate LLMs for the RL task. Therefore, the incrementality of novelty decreases.

**Significance:**  If validated by follow-up work, the AHRS could be significant as automating reward engineering is a major bottleneck in RL.  By automating the often tedious and expert-dependent process of designing dynamic reward weighting schemes, the approach could make RL more accessible to a broader audience and improve its applicability to complex robotic tasks. The experimental results are convincing within the simulated environment used.

**Strengths:**
*   **Automated Reward Shaping:** AHRS automates a complex and time-consuming aspect of RL, potentially reducing the need for human expertise in reward design.
*   **Dynamic Adaptation:** The use of LLMs allows for adaptation of the reward structure based on real-time policy performance, which may be more effective than static reward shaping strategies.
*   **Clear Experimental Results:** The paper presents clear experimental results that demonstrate performance gains compared to baselines.
*   **Incorporation of Auxiliary Rewards**: Augmenting the original reward function with additional LLM-generated rewards is a valuable strategy to improve skill learning.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Dependency on LLMs:** The performance of AHRS is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the LLM used.  LLMs are known to have limitations, including sensitivity to prompts and potential for generating nonsensical or biased outputs. The prompts should be designed appropriately, which requires experience in both LLM knowledge and RL.
*   **Limited Real-World Validation:** The experiments are conducted in simulation. Sim-to-real transfer remains a major challenge for RL, and the performance of AHRS in real-world robotic tasks is uncertain. It should be also noted that the results seem to be conducted in the Isaac Gym environment, which is not the actual physical environment.
*   **Computational Overhead:** Querying LLMs during training can be computationally expensive. The paper does not discuss the computational overhead associated with AHRS, which may limit its scalability. The paper should analyze the time consumption in terms of LLM API call, which is important for practical usage.
*   **Lack of Comparative LLM Analysis**: The paper did not report the usage of various LLMs in the framework.
*   **Limited ablation study of auxiliary rewards**:  The experiments on auxiliary rewards are limited, and it's not clear how much the auxiliary reward alone contributes to the overall performance improvement.
*   **Baseline limitations**: The paper has a limited performance in terms of baselines. There should be another baseline, such as other reward scheduling algorithms, to strengthen the results.
*   **Generalizability and Task Specificity**: The LLM-generated rules may overfit to the specific tasks and environments used in the experiments. The generalizability of AHRS to new and unseen robotic tasks requires further investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of using LLMs for dynamic reward scheduling, the clear experimental results in simulation, and the potential for automating reward engineering, the paper makes a significant contribution. However, the limitations related to LLM dependency, limited real-world validation, potential computational overhead, and generalizability concerns prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the approach is promising, further research is needed to address these limitations and fully realize its potential.

### SEFE: Superficial and Essential Forgetting Eliminator for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02486v1)
- **Authors**: Jinpeng Chen, Runmin Cong, Yuzhi Zhao, Hongzheng Yang, Guangneng Hu, Horace Ho Shing Ip, Sam Kwong
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) aims to enable Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to incrementally learn new tasks without catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we explore forgetting in this context, categorizing it into superficial forgetting and essential forgetting. Superficial forgetting refers to cases where the model's knowledge may not be genuinely lost, but its responses to previous tasks deviate from expected formats due to the influence of subsequent tasks' answer styles, making the results unusable. By contrast, essential forgetting refers to situations where the model provides correctly formatted but factually inaccurate answers, indicating a true loss of knowledge. Assessing essential forgetting necessitates addressing superficial forgetting first, as severe superficial forgetting can obscure the model's knowledge state. Hence, we first introduce the Answer Style Diversification (ASD) paradigm, which defines a standardized process for transforming data styles across different tasks, unifying their training sets into similarly diversified styles to prevent superficial forgetting caused by style shifts. Building on this, we propose RegLoRA to mitigate essential forgetting. RegLoRA stabilizes key parameters where prior knowledge is primarily stored by applying regularization, enabling the model to retain existing competencies. Experimental results demonstrate that our overall method, SEFE, achieves state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SEFE: Superficial and Essential Forgetting Eliminator for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of catastrophic forgetting in Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT). It categorizes forgetting into two distinct types: superficial forgetting (where the model loses the *format* of its responses but retains underlying knowledge) and essential forgetting (where the model genuinely loses knowledge). The paper then proposes a novel MCIT method called SEFE (Superficial and Essential Forgetting Eliminator) to mitigate both types of forgetting. SEFE consists of two main components:

1.  **Answer Style Diversification (ASD):** This technique aims to prevent superficial forgetting by diversifying the answer styles during training. The original data is augmented by reformatting a portion of it into different question types (yes/no, multiple-choice, etc.), thereby reducing the model's bias towards specific response styles.

2.  **RegLoRA:** This technique builds upon LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) and minimizes essential forgetting. It identifies key elements in the weight update matrices of prior LoRAs and applies regularization to these elements when training new LoRAs, stabilizing parameters associated with prior knowledge.

The paper presents experimental results on the COIN benchmark, demonstrating that SEFE achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to existing MCIT methods. The experiments also validate the effectiveness of ASD and RegLoRA in mitigating superficial and essential forgetting, respectively.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit distinction between superficial and essential forgetting in the context of MCIT. While the phenomenon of format drift has been observed previously in continual learning, formally defining and addressing it separately from knowledge loss is a valuable contribution. The proposed ASD and RegLoRA methods also exhibit novelty in their specific application to MCIT. ASD is a simple but effective method. It uses the same dataset from the original task. The RegLoRA is based on parameter regularization to ensure that the model remembers prior tasks.
*   **Significance:** Catastrophic forgetting is a major obstacle in enabling the practical deployment of MLLMs in dynamic environments where they need to learn new tasks without forgetting old ones. By providing a more nuanced understanding of forgetting in MCIT and developing effective techniques to address it, this paper makes a significant contribution to the field.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of forgetting in MCIT and provides a compelling motivation for distinguishing between superficial and essential forgetting.
    *   **Well-Designed Methods:** The proposed ASD and RegLoRA methods are well-designed and conceptually sound. They address the specific challenges of superficial and essential forgetting in a targeted manner.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of SEFE on the COIN benchmark, including comparisons to state-of-the-art methods and ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of the individual components.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the experimental results, including case studies to illustrate the benefits of ASD and RegLoRA.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The ASD method relies on a specific set of question types. While these types may be broadly applicable, their effectiveness could vary depending on the specific tasks and datasets being used. While the paper claims that these forms were identified after observation, this aspect requires further analysis.
    *   **Dependency on MLLMs for Data Augmentation:** The ASD method relies on MLLMs for generating alternative question formats. The quality and diversity of the augmented data could be affected by the capabilities of the MLLM used for generation.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily conducted on the COIN benchmark. It would be beneficial to evaluate SEFE on other MCIT benchmarks or real-world datasets to assess its generalizability.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The effectiveness of RegLoRA depends on the appropriate tuning of hyperparameters such as the regularization weight (λ) and the proportion of regularized elements (M).

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in MCIT by:
    *   Encouraging researchers to consider the distinction between superficial and essential forgetting when developing new methods.
    *   Providing a practical and effective approach for mitigating both types of forgetting.
    *   Establishing a new benchmark (COIN-ASD) for evaluating essential forgetting in MCIT.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of MCIT by providing a more nuanced understanding of the forgetting problem and developing effective techniques to address it. While the proposed methods have some limitations, their strengths outweigh their weaknesses. The research is well-conducted, thoroughly evaluated, and clearly presented. The paper has the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Score: 8**
