    *   **End-to-End Architecture:** The end-to-end design significantly reduces latency compared to pipeline systems.
    *   **Full-Duplex Capability:** Enables more natural and dynamic voice interactions.
    *   **Voice Customization:** Efficient voice creation and persona-aware generation increases usability.
    *   **Unified Model:** Supports multiple audio tasks without task-specific specialization.
    *   **Open Source:** Promotes research and development.
    *   **Benchmark:** Establishes a necessary suite to better understand language capabilities of voice models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** Reliance on LLMs for reasoning may inherit their limitations (e.g., bias, factual inaccuracies).
    *   **Data Requirements:** Training likely requires extensive audio-text data.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** While the Voila benchmark is a great addition, the evaluation focuses primarily on traditional metrics like WER, which may not fully capture the nuances of natural interaction. A more comprehensive evaluation of the full-duplex capabilities and the quality of generated personas would be valuable.
    *   **Comparison to State-of-the-Art Full Duplex:** The paper seems to present it as a large departure from previous approaches while citing a strong contender, Moshi, in its space.

*   **Justification for Score:**

Voila presents a notable advancement in voice-language models by tackling the challenges of latency and naturalness in human-machine interaction. However, the approach relies heavily on existing techniques and would benefit from more rigorous evaluation (not just quantitative metrics, but also qualitative assessments of naturalness). Given the innovation in its architecture and the value of its integrated approach, combined with the potential for significant impact, the potential of the voice customization, the open sourcing, and the addition of the benchmark, it's a valuable step in the field. The weaknesses, particularly the limited comparative novelty and evaluation, temper the score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing LLMs' Clinical Reasoning with Real-World Data from a Nationwide Sepsis Registry
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02722v1)
- **Authors**: Junu Kim, Chaeeun Shim, Sungjin Park, Su Yeon Lee, Gee Young Suh, Chae-Man Lim, Seong Jin Choi, Song Mi Moon, Kyoung-Ho Song, Eu Suk Kim, Hong Bin Kim, Sejoong Kim, Chami Im, Dong-Wan Kang, Yong Soo Kim, Hee-Joon Bae, Sung Yoon Lim, Han-Gil Jeong, Edward Choi
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive reasoning capabilities across general domains, their effectiveness in real-world clinical practice remains limited. This is likely due to their insufficient exposure to real-world clinical data during training, as such data is typically not included due to privacy concerns. To address this, we propose enhancing the clinical reasoning capabilities of LLMs by leveraging real-world clinical data. We constructed reasoning-intensive questions from a nationwide sepsis registry and fine-tuned Phi-4 on these questions using reinforcement learning, resulting in C-Reason. C-Reason exhibited strong clinical reasoning capabilities on the in-domain test set, as evidenced by both quantitative metrics and expert evaluations. Furthermore, its enhanced reasoning capabilities generalized to a sepsis dataset involving different tasks and patient cohorts, an open-ended consultations on antibiotics use task, and other diseases. Future research should focus on training LLMs with large-scale, multi-disease clinical datasets to develop more powerful, general-purpose clinical reasoning models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces C-Reason, a large language model (LLM) fine-tuned for clinical reasoning in sepsis management. The model is trained on a nationwide sepsis registry data using a reinforcement learning approach on reasoning-intensive questions generated through feature masking. The results indicate that C-Reason significantly improves clinical reasoning compared to its base model (Phi-4) and performs competitively with general-purpose LLMs. The enhanced reasoning capability generalizes across different sepsis datasets, tasks (including open-ended consultations), and even extends to other diseases like acute kidney injury and stroke. The paper highlights the potential of leveraging real-world clinical data to enhance LLM performance in healthcare while addressing data privacy concerns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its approach to training LLMs solely on real-world clinical data without relying on external reasoning models like GPT-4, which improves scalability. While there have been attempts to use real-world clinical data, this paper's strategy of generating reasoning-intensive questions via masking coupled with reinforcement learning, all within the confines of the target clinical domain, demonstrates an important approach. Furthermore, the generalizability of the model's reasoning capabilities to tasks and conditions outside the training domain is a compelling finding.
*   **Significance:** Enhancing LLMs for clinical reasoning is significant for improving decision support systems, aiding in diagnosis, treatment planning, and guideline adherence. The paper's demonstration that these models can generalize to multiple diseases suggests the potential to build more versatile and powerful clinical decision support tools. Addressing privacy concerns in healthcare data is also crucial, and this work contributes to the development of methods that can utilize this valuable resource.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous evaluation: The paper uses quantitative metrics, expert evaluations, and tests on multiple datasets to thoroughly assess the model's performance.
    *   Generalizability: The model demonstrates strong performance across various sepsis datasets, open-ended tasks (e.g., antibiotic usage consultations), and other clinical domains (AKI and stroke).
    *   Scalability: The methodology is rule-based, avoiding labor-intensive steps, format standardization, or reliance on external resources, which makes it easier to apply to larger and more diverse clinical datasets.
    *   Addresses a critical gap: The paper specifically tackles the limitations of LLMs in real-world clinical practice by focusing on real-world data and reasoning.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Data: The experiments primarily focus on a sepsis registry, which may limit the model's ability to generalize in a more diverse clinical environment with complex and ambiguous cases.
    *   Generalizability Across Diseases: The performance on AKI and Stroke is promising, but more extensive experiments are needed. The paper notes that decreased F1 score on the 1-year MACE prediction task for the stroke registry provides some weakness to overall generalizability.
    *   While the approach addresses the reliance on external resources, fine-tuning C-Reason still requires significant computational resources.
    *   While the experts generally preferred C-Reason's responses, some of the performance gains were not statistically significant, indicating that further improvements may be necessary for the model to be consistently preferred.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in applying LLMs to clinical reasoning by addressing the critical issue of limited access to real-world data. The proposed method is novel, scalable, and demonstrates substantial improvements in performance compared to existing approaches. While further validation on more diverse datasets and detailed exploration of the generalizability is warranted, the paper offers a strong foundation for future research in this area. In my opinion, a rating above 8 would indicate a much stronger novelty and/or significance.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### FormalMATH: Benchmarking Formal Mathematical Reasoning of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02735v1)
- **Authors**: Zhouliang Yu, Ruotian Peng, Keyi Ding, Yizhe Li, Zhongyuan Peng, Minghao Liu, Yifan Zhang, Zheng Yuan, Huajian Xin, Wenhao Huang, Yandong Wen, Ge Zhang, Weiyang Liu
- **Abstract**: Formal mathematical reasoning remains a critical challenge for artificial intelligence, hindered by limitations of existing benchmarks in scope and scale. To address this, we present FormalMATH, a large-scale Lean4 benchmark comprising 5,560 formally verified problems spanning from high-school Olympiad challenges to undergraduate-level theorems across diverse domains (e.g., algebra, applied mathematics, calculus, number theory, and discrete mathematics). To mitigate the inefficiency of manual formalization, we introduce a novel human-in-the-loop autoformalization pipeline that integrates: (1) specialized large language models (LLMs) for statement autoformalization, (2) multi-LLM semantic verification, and (3) negation-based disproof filtering strategies using off-the-shelf LLM-based provers. This approach reduces expert annotation costs by retaining 72.09% of statements before manual verification while ensuring fidelity to the original natural-language problems. Our evaluation of state-of-the-art LLM-based theorem provers reveals significant limitations: even the strongest models achieve only 16.46% success rate under practical sampling budgets, exhibiting pronounced domain bias (e.g., excelling in algebra but failing in calculus) and over-reliance on simplified automation tactics. Notably, we identify a counterintuitive inverse relationship between natural-language solution guidance and proof success in chain-of-thought reasoning scenarios, suggesting that human-written informal reasoning introduces noise rather than clarity in the formal reasoning settings. We believe that FormalMATH provides a robust benchmark for benchmarking formal mathematical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the "FormalMATH: Benchmarking Formal Mathematical Reasoning of Large Language Models" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FormalMATH, a large-scale Lean4 benchmark designed for evaluating the formal mathematical reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  FormalMATH comprises 5,560 formally verified problems spanning various mathematical domains and difficulty levels.  To create this benchmark efficiently, the authors developed a human-in-the-loop autoformalization pipeline involving specialized LLMs for statement translation, semantic verification using multiple LLMs, and negation-based disproof filtering.  The paper also presents an evaluation of state-of-the-art LLM-based theorem provers on FormalMATH, revealing their limitations in terms of success rates, domain biases, and effective reasoning strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a large-scale, diverse, and rigorously formalized benchmark for mathematical reasoning. While smaller benchmarks exist, FormalMATH significantly expands the scope and addresses limitations of existing datasets. The autoformalization pipeline, combining different LLM functionalities, offers a practical approach to handling the challenges of creating formal datasets. The counter-intuitive finding that informal reasoning via natural language can *decrease* performance is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is high. FormalMATH provides a crucial tool for the FMR research community, allowing for more robust and comprehensive evaluations of LLM-based theorem provers. The benchmark can drive progress in improving LLMs' ability to perform complex mathematical reasoning, which has implications for automated verification, AI-assisted education, and other domains. The pipeline for automatic formalization is also a key enabler for more work in this field, since creating formal datasets is expensive and requires a high level of formal reasoning expertise.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scale and Diversity:** FormalMATH offers a substantial increase in scale and domain coverage compared to existing benchmarks.
    *   **Rigorous Formalization:** The benchmark is based on formally verified Lean4 statements, ensuring accuracy and enabling automated validation.
    *   **Efficient Autoformalization:** The human-in-the-loop pipeline reduces the cost of creating a formal dataset.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a detailed evaluation of LLM-based theorem provers, highlighting their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The discovery of domain bias and the inverse relationship between natural language guidance and proof success reveals important insights about LLM reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Focus on Proof Search:** While valuable, the evaluation primarily focuses on "proof search" and doesn't deeply delve into alternative FMR paradigms.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Modes:** Although error patterns were analyzed, a more fine-grained categorization or case studies exploring specific challenging theorems could have been valuable.
    *   **Reliance on Specific Tools:** The benchmark and pipeline are tied to Lean4, which might limit its accessibility to researchers unfamiliar with this formal system.

*   **Potential Influence:** FormalMATH is likely to become a widely used benchmark in the FMR research community. It will facilitate the development of more robust and generalizable LLM-based theorem provers. The autoformalization pipeline can inspire similar approaches in other formal domains. The paper will likely accelerate progress in formal mathematical reasoning and its application to real-world problems.

*Score: 8*

*Rationale:*

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field by creating a valuable resource (FormalMATH) and providing insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in formal mathematical reasoning. The meticulous approach to dataset creation, detailed evaluation, and interesting findings justify a high score. While there are some minor weaknesses (primarily related to scope and analysis depth), the strengths of the paper outweigh these concerns. The availability of FormalMATH will undoubtedly accelerate progress in this area. A score above 9 would imply a true paradigm shift, which I do not believe this paper represents. The paper is very strong in terms of the quality of the work, and provides a very valuable resource, but it's more of a very significant evolution than a revolution.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Knowledge Graphs for Enhancing Large Language Models in Entity Disambiguation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02737v1)
- **Authors**: Pons Gerard, Bilalli Besim, Queralt Anna
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have positioned them as a prominent solution for Natural Language Processing tasks. Notably, they can approach these problems in a zero or few-shot manner, thereby eliminating the need for training or fine-tuning task-specific models. However, LLMs face some challenges, including hallucination and the presence of outdated knowledge or missing information from specific domains in the training data. These problems cannot be easily solved by retraining the models with new data as it is a time-consuming and expensive process. To mitigate these issues, Knowledge Graphs (KGs) have been proposed as a structured external source of information to enrich LLMs. With this idea, in this work we use KGs to enhance LLMs for zero-shot Entity Disambiguation (ED). For that purpose, we leverage the hierarchical representation of the entities' classes in a KG to gradually prune the candidate space as well as the entities' descriptions to enrich the input prompt with additional factual knowledge. Our evaluation on popular ED datasets shows that the proposed method outperforms non-enhanced and description-only enhanced LLMs, and has a higher degree of adaptability than task-specific models. Furthermore, we conduct an error analysis and discuss the impact of the leveraged KG's semantic expressivity on the ED performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores enhancing Large Language Models (LLMs) for zero-shot Entity Disambiguation (ED) by incorporating Knowledge Graphs (KGs). The proposed method utilizes the hierarchical class structure within a KG to prune the candidate entity space and leverages entity descriptions to enrich the input prompt. The approach involves generating a subgraph from the KG, applying a pruning algorithm based on the Lowest Common Ancestor (LCA) of candidate entities, and prompting the LLM to choose between classes or entities based on the document context.  Experiments are conducted on popular ED datasets, comparing the KG-enhanced LLM against baseline LLMs, description-only enhanced LLMs, and a task-specific model. The evaluation focuses on inKB micro-F1 score and achieved gold percentage. The paper also investigates the impact of semantic expressivity in KGs (YAGO vs. DBpedia) and performs error analysis to identify limitations and potential improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using KGs to improve LLM performance is not entirely novel.  Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) has been used before. However, this paper's specific approach of using the *hierarchical class structure within the KG to guide a pruning algorithm* is a distinctive contribution. This differs from simply appending entity descriptions. While using class information has been done in task-specific models, adapting this approach in a *zero-shot* LLM setting, guiding the reasoning process rather than encoding it, provides added value.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the limitations of LLMs in zero-shot scenarios, particularly hallucination and lack of domain-specific knowledge. Improving ED performance in a zero-shot manner is vital for practical applications where training data is scarce or adapting to new domains is crucial. Furthermore, the paper provides insights into the impact of KG expressivity on LLM performance, which is valuable for KG selection or design for KG-enhanced LLMs. The demonstration that KG-enhanced LLMs can achieve comparable or even better performance than some task-specific models, especially in out-of-domain scenarios, strengthens the argument for this approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured explanation of the proposed method.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on a variety of ED datasets.
    *   Detailed analysis of results, including comparisons to existing methods and analysis of error cases.
    *   Discussion of the impact of KG expressivity, which provides valuable insights.
    *   The method is zero-shot and does not require fine-tuning of LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relies on external knowledge (KG), which might not always be up-to-date or comprehensive.
    *   Performance is dependent on the quality and structure of the KG.  The paper does address this with the KG expressivity analysis, but a more in-depth exploration of other KG types (e.g., domain-specific KGs) would strengthen the analysis.
    *   The experiments are performed using GPT-3.5. While justified, demonstrating the approach's effectiveness with other LLM architectures (e.g., open-source models) would increase the impact and generalizability of the findings.
    *   The error analysis, while valuable, is limited to two datasets due to the manual effort involved. Expanding this analysis would provide a more complete understanding of the method's limitations.
    *   The gain in performance in some datasets is marginal. The percentage of gold F1 score results are very similar in the baseline and proposed approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in KG-enhanced LLMs, particularly for tasks where zero-shot performance is critical. The pruning algorithm guided by the KG hierarchy offers a promising direction for improving LLM reasoning. The error analysis highlights the limitations, guiding future research toward more robust and adaptable KG-enhanced LLMs.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the core idea of using KGs to enhance LLMs isn't brand new, the paper demonstrates a clever approach with the hierarchical pruning strategy. The comprehensive experimental evaluation across several datasets and a well-conducted KG expressivity study are strong points. The analysis of error cases also shows a good degree of rigor.  However, the improvements over the baseline are not dramatic across all datasets, and the dependence on external KGs and limited use of more advanced LLMs limits its overall impact. The improvements might not be enough to justify the added complexity of incorporating KGs in certain situations. Therefore, the paper merits a score that acknowledges its solid contribution but also reflects the limitations in novelty and the somewhat incremental improvements.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Advancing Generalizable Tumor Segmentation with Anomaly-Aware Open-Vocabulary Attention Maps and Frozen Foundation Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02753v1)
- **Authors**: Yankai Jiang, Peng Zhang, Donglin Yang, Yuan Tian, Hai Lin, Xiaosong Wang
- **Abstract**: We explore Generalizable Tumor Segmentation, aiming to train a single model for zero-shot tumor segmentation across diverse anatomical regions. Existing methods face limitations related to segmentation quality, scalability, and the range of applicable imaging modalities. In this paper, we uncover the potential of the internal representations within frozen medical foundation diffusion models as highly efficient zero-shot learners for tumor segmentation by introducing a novel framework named DiffuGTS. DiffuGTS creates anomaly-aware open-vocabulary attention maps based on text prompts to enable generalizable anomaly segmentation without being restricted by a predefined training category list. To further improve and refine anomaly segmentation masks, DiffuGTS leverages the diffusion model, transforming pathological regions into high-quality pseudo-healthy counterparts through latent space inpainting, and applies a novel pixel-level and feature-level residual learning approach, resulting in segmentation masks with significantly enhanced quality and generalization. Comprehensive experiments on four datasets and seven tumor categories demonstrate the superior performance of our method, surpassing current state-of-the-art models across multiple zero-shot settings. Codes are available at https://github.com/Yankai96/DiffuGTS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Advancing Generalizable Tumor Segmentation with Anomaly-Aware Open-Vocabulary Attention Maps and Frozen Foundation Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffuGTS, a novel framework for generalizable zero-shot tumor segmentation. DiffuGTS leverages the internal representations of frozen medical foundation diffusion models (MFDMs) to overcome the limitations of existing methods in terms of segmentation quality, scalability, and applicability across various imaging modalities. It constructs anomaly-aware, open-vocabulary attention maps (AOVA) based on text prompts for anomaly detection. To refine the segmentation masks, it synthesizes pseudo-healthy counterparts of pathological regions via latent space inpainting, combined with pixel-level and feature-level residual learning. Experiments on four datasets and seven tumor categories demonstrate that DiffuGTS outperforms state-of-the-art models in various zero-shot settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its effective repurposing of frozen MFDMs, specifically MAISI, for zero-shot tumor segmentation. The framework doesn't synthesize training data directly or train new models from scratch; instead, it efficiently extracts and adapts the inherent anatomical knowledge within the pre-trained MFDMs. The combination of AOVA maps generated using cross-modal interactions and the mask refinement process via latent space inpainting is also a novel design. The pixel- and feature-level residual learning for refining segmentation masks further enhances the novelty.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in medical image analysis: the need for models that can generalize to unseen tumor types across diverse anatomical regions and imaging modalities.  By demonstrating a zero-shot approach using pre-trained models, the paper reduces the dependence on extensive labeled training data, which is a major bottleneck in medical AI. The gains over existing state-of-the-art methods are substantial and well-documented with DSC and NSD metrics across multiple datasets.
* **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results: The experiments showcase significant performance improvements over existing methods, providing compelling evidence for the effectiveness of DiffuGTS. The use of multiple datasets across various tumor categories and imaging modalities strengthens the robustness of the findings.
    *   Innovative design: The AOVA maps and mask refinement strategies are well-reasoned and effectively leverage the capabilities of MFDMs. The method successfully addresses the limitations of existing approaches, such as those based on vision-language alignment and tumor synthesis.
    *   Comprehensive analysis:  The paper includes thorough ablation studies that break down the contributions of different components of DiffuGTS, such as the adapter, AOVA maps, and mask refinement.  The exploration of how the method deals with misleading prompts and analysis of potential data leakage add further depth.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational cost: The method relies on the MAISI model for feature extraction and generation, resulting in relatively high computational costs (FLOPs) compared to other methods, albeit with fewer trainable parameters. While the authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest efficient knowledge distillation in the future, it currently poses a barrier to wider adoption.
    *   Dependency on MFDMs: The framework's performance is inherently limited by the capabilities of the underlying pre-trained MAISI model. While it effectively leverages this model, the results are contingent on its architecture and training data. The generalizability of the framework to other MFDMs isn't explicitly addressed, although is likely transferrable with minor adaptation.
    * Limited Modalities. The paper is limited to MRI and CT scan. While extensive results are shown in both imaging types, but not extended to other modalities such as 2D x-ray.

* **Potential Influence:** DiffuGTS has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It demonstrates the power of repurposing pre-trained models for zero-shot medical image segmentation, potentially accelerating the development of generalizable models.
    *   The AOVA map and mask refinement techniques can be adopted and adapted in other medical image analysis tasks.
    *   It encourages further exploration of foundation models and latent space manipulation for anomaly detection and segmentation in medical imaging.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a significant advance in generalizable tumor segmentation, offering a novel and effective approach to zero-shot learning.  The substantial performance improvements demonstrated by DiffuGTS, combined with its innovative design and comprehensive analysis, make it a valuable contribution to the field. While the computational cost and dependency on MAISI are limitations, the overall impact and potential influence of the work justify a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Bye-bye, Bluebook? Automating Legal Procedure with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02763v1)
- **Authors**: Matthew Dahl
- **Abstract**: Legal practice requires careful adherence to procedural rules. In the United States, few are more complex than those found in The Bluebook: A Uniform System of Citation. Compliance with this system's 500+ pages of byzantine formatting instructions is the raison d'etre of thousands of student law review editors and the bete noire of lawyers everywhere. To evaluate whether large language models (LLMs) are able to adhere to the procedures of such a complicated system, we construct an original dataset of 866 Bluebook tasks and test flagship LLMs from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta, and DeepSeek. We show (1) that these models produce fully compliant Bluebook citations only 69%-74% of the time and (2) that in-context learning on the Bluebook's underlying system of rules raises accuracy only to 77%. These results caution against using off-the-shelf LLMs to automate aspects of the law where fidelity to procedure is paramount.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Bye-bye, Bluebook? Automating Legal Procedure with Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to automate legal procedure, specifically focusing on compliance with *The Bluebook: A Uniform System of Citation*. The authors construct a dataset of Bluebook formatting tasks and evaluate the performance of several flagship LLMs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta, and DeepSeek) in zero-shot and in-context learning settings. They find that off-the-shelf LLMs achieve only modest accuracy in producing fully compliant Bluebook citations, and that in-context learning provides only marginal improvements. The paper concludes that lawyers should be cautious about entrusting even routine procedural aspects of legal practice to current LLMs due to their inability to faithfully execute complex rule-based processes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper addresses an important and under-explored area: the application of LLMs to procedural aspects of law, as opposed to substantive legal tasks that are more commonly studied. This focus on procedural compliance is highly relevant, as adherence to rules and regulations is critical in legal practice. The construction of a dedicated Bluebook task dataset is a significant contribution. Previous studies have mostly evaluated LLMs on legal reasoning or knowledge, but not on the intricacies of legal procedure. However, the core idea – assessing LLMs on a defined task – is not new. The novelty primarily lies in *the specific application* to the Bluebook and legal citation.

*   **Significance:**  The findings have significant implications for the legal profession.  The paper clearly demonstrates that current LLMs are not yet reliable enough to automate Bluebook citation tasks, which are both time-consuming and require high levels of precision. This challenges some of the more optimistic narratives about the transformative potential of AI in law and highlights the need for caution and careful evaluation. This contributes to a more nuanced discussion of AI's role in legal practice. The work serves as a cautionary tale, suggesting that AI implementation in domains with complex and inflexible rules requires more sophisticated solutions than simply applying general-purpose LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined research question:**  The paper clearly articulates its objective and scope.
    *   **Original dataset:**  The creation of the Bluebook task dataset is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Rigorous methodology:**  The authors employ a sound experimental design, testing several LLMs in both zero-shot and in-context learning settings.
    *   **Clear presentation of results:**  The results are presented in a clear and accessible manner, with informative tables and figures.
    *   **Thoughtful discussion of limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their study, such as the focus on a specific citation style and the point-in-time nature of their evaluation.
    *   **Qualitative Error Analysis**: Going beyond mere accuracy metrics, they provide examples of the *types* of errors LLMs make which offers additional insight

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study primarily focuses on a single, albeit complex, aspect of legal procedure (Bluebook citation). While this allows for in-depth analysis, the findings may not be generalizable to all forms of legal procedure.
    *   **Relatively simple evaluation metric:** The exact string matching approach to evaluate correctness can be too strict. Minor deviations from the Bluebook standard that do not affect the understanding or locatability of the cited source may be penalized. The use of string comparison has known weaknesses when evaluating text.
    *   **Lack of comparison to human performance**: While the focus is on replacing human tasks, it would be beneficial to compare the LLM performance directly to humans attempting Bluebook tasks under the same conditions.
    *   **Limited exploration of fine-tuning:** While they touch on fine tuning in the related work, focusing largely on general-purpose LLMs limits the contribution; the paper would have been strengthened by including an LLM that was specifically trained on Bluebook tasks to evaluate its performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways. It motivates further investigation into the application of AI to procedural aspects of law and highlights the need for more sophisticated techniques for rule-based automation. It also provides a valuable resource (the Bluebook task dataset) for future evaluations of LLMs in the legal domain. By cautioning against over-reliance on general-purpose LLMs, the paper could encourage the development of more specialized and robust AI systems for legal practice.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a notable contribution by focusing on a critical, yet often overlooked, area of legal practice: procedural compliance. The generation of an original Bluebook task dataset, the sound methodology, and the clear presentation of results all add to the paper's value. However, the somewhat limited scope and simplistic evaluation prevent a higher score. The key limitation remains that legal procedures can be more easily formalized compared to substantive analysis, and even this falls short.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Giving Simulated Cells a Voice: Evolving Prompt-to-Intervention Models for Cellular Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02766v1)
- **Authors**: Nam H. Le, Patrick Erikson, Yanbo Zhang, Michael Levin, Josh Bongard
- **Abstract**: Guiding biological systems toward desired states, such as morphogenetic outcomes, remains a fundamental challenge with far-reaching implications for medicine and synthetic biology. While large language models (LLMs) have enabled natural language as an interface for interpretable control in AI systems, their use as mediators for steering biological or cellular dynamics remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present a functional pipeline that translates natural language prompts into spatial vector fields capable of directing simulated cellular collectives. Our approach combines a large language model with an evolvable neural controller (Prompt-to-Intervention, or P2I), optimized via evolutionary strategies to generate behaviors such as clustering or scattering in a simulated 2D environment. We demonstrate that even with constrained vocabulary and simplified cell models, evolved P2I networks can successfully align cellular dynamics with user-defined goals expressed in plain language. This work offers a complete loop from language input to simulated bioelectric-like intervention to behavioral output, providing a foundation for future systems capable of natural language-driven cellular control.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GIVING SIMULATED CELLS A VOICE: EVOLVING PROMPT-TO-INTERVENTION MODELS FOR CELLULAR CONTROL."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework, termed ZapGPT, for controlling simulated cellular dynamics using natural language. It leverages large language models (LLMs) to translate natural language prompts (e.g., "Cluster!") into spatial vector fields, which act as interventions on a 2D simulated cellular environment. The resulting cellular dynamics are then interpreted back into natural language using a vision-language model (VLM), forming a Dynamics-to-Response (D2R) model. The system is optimized using evolutionary algorithms (EAs) to refine the Prompt-to-Intervention (P2I) mapping, aiming to align cellular behavior with user-defined goals. The proof-of-concept experiments focus on simple collective behaviors like clustering and scattering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs as an interface to control cellular dynamics is novel. The integration of LLMs (for P2I), VLMs (for D2R), and evolutionary algorithms (for optimization) within a single framework is a unique approach. While individual components have been used in other contexts, their combination for cell behavior control is a fresh perspective. However, the paper explicitly frames itself as preliminary, and the simulated environment is highly simplified. Therefore, the novelty is primarily in the conceptual integration rather than groundbreaking individual components.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this research lies in its ability to bridge the gap between high-level human intent and complex biological systems. If successfully extended to real-world biological systems, this framework could have far-reaching implications for medicine, synthetic biology, and regenerative medicine. Natural language interfaces could significantly democratize cellular control, making it accessible to a wider range of researchers and clinicians.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear conceptual framework: The ZapGPT pipeline is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   Integration of multiple AI techniques: Combines LLMs, VLMs, and EAs in a functional manner.
    *   Proof-of-concept demonstration: Shows that the framework can align cellular dynamics with user-defined goals in a simulated environment.
    *   Clear articulation of limitations and future directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Simplified simulation environment: The 2D simulation is a far cry from the complexity of real biological systems, including cell differentiation and multiple chemical signals.
    *   Limited vocabulary and behaviors: Experiments focus on clustering and scattering, which limits the scope of the demonstration.
    *   Dependency on curated datasets: The visual evaluation component (D2R) requires a curated dataset of behaviors, limiting generalization to previously unseen behaviors.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a compelling and innovative concept with a clear pipeline architecture. The proof-of-concept is promising but very preliminary. The main weakness is the simplicity of the simulated environment. Despite this, the potential impact of this work, if successfully scaled up, is significant, and the approach is sufficiently novel to warrant attention. The paper lays a clear groundwork for future investigation and explicitly defines constraints that need to be tackled in the future.
The integration of existing methods to solve a complex problem warrants a respectable score.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### HSplitLoRA: A Heterogeneous Split Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning Framework for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02795v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Lin, Yuxin Zhang, Zhe Chen, Zihan Fang, Xianhao Chen, Praneeth Vepakomma, Wei Ni, Jun Luo, Yue Gao
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable breakthroughs, revolutionizing the natural language processing domain and beyond. Due to immense parameter sizes, fine-tuning these models with private data for diverse downstream tasks has become mainstream. Though federated learning (FL) offers a promising solution for fine-tuning LLMs without sharing raw data, substantial computing costs hinder its democratization. Moreover, in real-world scenarios, private client devices often possess heterogeneous computing resources, further complicating LLM fine-tuning. To combat these challenges, we propose HSplitLoRA, a heterogeneous parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) framework built on split learning (SL) and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning, for efficiently fine-tuning LLMs on heterogeneous client devices. HSplitLoRA first identifies important weights based on their contributions to LLM training. It then dynamically configures the decomposition ranks of LoRA adapters for selected weights and determines the model split point according to varying computing budgets of client devices. Finally, a noise-free adapter aggregation mechanism is devised to support heterogeneous adapter aggregation without introducing noise. Extensive experiments demonstrate that HSplitLoRA outperforms state-of-the-art benchmarks in training accuracy and convergence speed.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HSplitLoRA, a novel framework for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of large language models (LLMs) in heterogeneous distributed environments using split learning (SL) and low-rank adaptation (LoRA). HSplitLoRA addresses the challenges of fine-tuning LLMs on resource-constrained and heterogeneous client devices by incorporating three key components: (1) an important weight identification scheme to prioritize weights for fine-tuning; (2) an adaptive rank and model splitting configuration to dynamically adjust LoRA adapter ranks and the model split point based on client device computing budgets; and (3) a noise-free adapter aggregation mechanism to consolidate heterogeneous LoRA adapters without introducing noise. Experiments demonstrate that HSplitLoRA outperforms existing methods in terms of training accuracy and convergence speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a relevant and important problem: efficiently fine-tuning LLMs in resource-constrained and heterogeneous environments. The integration of SL and PEFT (LoRA) is a logical step, but the paper goes beyond a simple combination by introducing adaptive mechanisms tailored to heterogeneous clients. The three core components (weight identification, adaptive configuration, and noise-free aggregation) form a coherent and well-motivated approach.

*   **Novelty:** The proposed framework has several novel aspects. The weight identification scheme, which balances current and historical importance while accounting for computing costs, is a valuable contribution. The adaptive rank and model splitting based on device capabilities is also a novel and significant contribution to the SL+LoRA approach. The most novel is the 'noise-free adapter aggregation' approach. The traditional approach of averaging low rank matrices, with differing dimensions is problematic and the presented approach solves this well, adding strong value.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant bottleneck in democratizing LLM fine-tuning. By enabling efficient fine-tuning on heterogeneous client devices, it broadens the applicability of LLMs to real-world scenarios where data privacy and resource constraints are paramount. A well performing open source implementation would be of great value.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Novel integration of SL, LoRA, and adaptive mechanisms.
    *   Strong experimental results demonstrating improved accuracy and convergence.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   Good balance between theoretical analysis and empirical validation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental setup, while comprehensive, could benefit from incorporating more diverse datasets and model architectures, perhaps including other recent LLMs to broaden the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The paper could explore the sensitivity of the framework to variations in network bandwidth and latency, as these factors can significantly impact performance in distributed training settings.
    *   The algorithm is complex and its real-world deployment requires careful engineering and tuning of several parameters, which may limit its accessibility to non-experts.

The work builds upon prior research in FL, SL, and PEFT, but it introduces significant innovations that advance the state-of-the-art. The paper is well-written, the experiments are thorough, and the results are convincing. The proposed approach has the potential to be highly impactful in enabling efficient and privacy-preserving fine-tuning of LLMs in real-world distributed environments. The main limitation is the complexity, it isn't a method that can be easily picked up.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** While the paper addresses a critical problem and presents several novel contributions, some limitations need to be considered. The scope is primarily limited to LoRA, the experimental setup needs some diversification, and the algorithm’s complexity might pose a barrier to entry for some. The combination of the proposed techniques and clear problem focus justifies a relatively high score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Generating HomeAssistant Automations Using an LLM-based Chatbot
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02802v1)
- **Authors**: Mathyas Giudici, Alessandro Sironi, Ismaele Villa, Samuele Scherini, Franca Garzotto
- **Abstract**: To combat climate change, individuals are encouraged to adopt sustainable habits, in particular, with their household, optimizing their electrical consumption. Conversational agents, such as Smart Home Assistants, hold promise as effective tools for promoting sustainable practices within households. Our research investigated the application of Large Language Models (LLM) in enhancing smart home automation and promoting sustainable household practices, specifically using the HomeAssistant framework. In particular, it highlights the potential of GPT models in generating accurate automation routines. While the LLMs showed proficiency in understanding complex commands and creating valid JSON outputs, challenges such as syntax errors and message malformations were noted, indicating areas for further improvement. Still, despite minimal quantitative differences between "green" and "no green" prompts, qualitative feedback highlighted a positive shift towards sustainability in the routines generated with environmentally focused prompts. Then, an empirical evaluation (N=56) demonstrated that the system was well-received and found engaging by users compared to its traditional rule-based counterpart. Our findings highlight the role of LLMs in advancing smart home technologies and suggest further research to refine these models for broader, real-world applications to support sustainable living.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Generating HomeAssistant Automations Using an LLM-based Chatbot":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate HomeAssistant automations, aiming to make smart homes more sustainable and user-friendly.  The authors evaluate different LLMs (GPT3.5, GPT4, LLAMA2, MISTRAL, codeLLAMA) in generating valid JSON configurations for HomeAssistant based on natural language user commands. They also investigate whether a prompt focused on sustainability ("green" prompt) leads to different or improved outcomes compared to a generic prompt. The research includes a comparative evaluation of LLM performance and a user study (N=56) comparing a GPT3.5-powered chatbot (EcoMate) to a traditional rule-based chatbot.  The results indicate that GPT models, particularly GPT4, excel in generating valid HomeAssistant routines and that the LLM-based chatbot is perceived as more engaging and likeable than its rule-based counterpart.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's contribution lies in specifically focusing on using LLMs *within the context of HomeAssistant* to promote *domestic sustainability* through conversational agents. While prior work has explored LLMs for home automation (King et al. being explicitly mentioned), this paper is significant because it specifically focuses on the "green" context, attempts to measure the impact of sustainable prompts, and provides a user evaluation compared to the "non-LLM-based" system. The evaluation methodology using specific metrics such as JSON validity in a defined HomeAssistant environment is also reasonably novel.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem: making smart homes more accessible and sustainable for non-expert users.  LLMs, if properly harnessed, have the potential to bridge the gap between user intent and the technical complexity of configuring home automation systems. The findings regarding the performance of different LLMs in generating valid HomeAssistant configurations are valuable for researchers and developers working in this area.  The positive user evaluation of the EcoMate chatbot suggests that LLMs can enhance user engagement and encourage adoption of sustainable practices within the home.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined research questions:** The research questions are clear and address specific aspects of LLM capabilities and user perceptions.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation methodology:** The paper employs a mixed-methods approach, combining quantitative evaluation of LLM performance (JSON validity, latency, relevance score) with qualitative analysis and user studies.
*   **Focus on sustainability:** The incorporation of a "green" prompt and the analysis of its impact (even if limited) is a valuable contribution.
*   **Emphasis on repeatability:** Public code and data make the study reproducible.
*   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited impact of "green" prompt:**  The paper admits that the quantitative analysis revealed no *significant* distinctions between "green" and "no green" prompts. The reported qualitative findings offer only a vague suggestion about more green attributes being generated. This weakens the overall claim about promoting sustainable behaviors.
*   **Artificial laboratory setting:**  The user study was conducted in a controlled laboratory environment with hypothetical scenarios. The study is limited by not measuring actual energy consumption or long-term behavioral changes.  The appliances' consumption data was artificially constructed.
*   **SUS scale limited:** The SUS scale showed no statistically significant difference between the two conditions and can lead to some questions about the usability of both systems.
*   **GPT4 unavailability limits direct comparability:** The ethical considerations that prevented the usage of GPT4 for the user studies limited the real performance that can be achieved in the real-life usage scenarios. The user-experience and acceptance can greatly improve with that model in particular.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable initial exploration of LLMs for generating HomeAssistant automations to promote sustainable living.  The study's focus on the "green" aspect, although yielding limited quantitative results, points towards a promising direction. The paper has solid findings on the better interaction performance achieved with LLMs but is limited by the artificial settings and lack of demonstrable environmental impact.

Score: 6

The novelty is moderate because it expands on existing work by adding the dimension of sustainability and HomeAssistant. The significance is noteworthy, but the limited impact of the "green" prompt and the controlled lab environment restrict its potential influence. The code and data's availability are commendable, facilitating further research. But there could have been larger findings by conducting the research directly to people´s homes and measuring energy reduction.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Quantifying the Hessian Structure of Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02809v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaorui Dong, Yushun Zhang, Zhi-Quan Luo, Jianfeng Yao, Ruoyu Sun
- **Abstract**: Empirical studies reported that the Hessian matrix of neural networks (NNs) exhibits a near-block-diagonal structure, yet its theoretical foundation remains unclear. In this work, we reveal two forces that shape the Hessian structure: a ``static force'' rooted in the architecture design, and a ``dynamic force'' arisen from training. We then provide a rigorous theoretical analysis of ``static force'' at random initialization. We study linear models and 1-hidden-layer networks with the mean-square (MSE) loss and the Cross-Entropy (CE) loss for classification tasks. By leveraging random matrix theory, we compare the limit distributions of the diagonal and off-diagonal Hessian blocks and find that the block-diagonal structure arises as $C \rightarrow \infty$, where $C$ denotes the number of classes. Our findings reveal that $C$ is a primary driver of the near-block-diagonal structure. These results may shed new light on the Hessian structure of large language models (LLMs), which typically operate with a large $C$ exceeding $10^4$ or $10^5$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the phenomenon of the near-block-diagonal structure observed in the Hessian matrix of neural networks (NNs). It proposes that this structure arises from two primary forces: a "static force" linked to the network's architecture and a "dynamic force" resulting from training. The paper then focuses on a rigorous theoretical analysis of the "static force" at random initialization, considering linear models and one-hidden-layer networks trained with mean-squared error (MSE) and cross-entropy (CE) loss for classification. Using random matrix theory, the authors compare the limit distributions of diagonal and off-diagonal blocks of the Hessian. The analysis reveals that the number of classes (C) is a primary driver of the near-block-diagonal structure, particularly as C tends to infinity, pushing off-diagonal blocks towards zero. This challenges earlier claims that CE loss is the sole determining factor. The paper offers both numerical experiments and theoretical proofs to support its claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in providing a theoretical foundation for an empirically observed phenomenon. While previous works documented the near-block-diagonal structure of the Hessian, they lacked a rigorous explanation. The paper's use of random matrix theory to characterize the distribution of Hessian blocks is a novel contribution. The identification of the number of classes 'C' as a key driver is also a new and valuable insight. Challenging the conventional wisdom attributed to CE loss from Collobert (2004) demonstrates a significant reevaluation of existing understanding.

*   **Significance:** The findings could have significant implications for understanding and improving NN training, especially in large language models (LLMs) where the number of classes is substantial. The results suggest that algorithms exploiting the block-diagonal structure can be more efficient. The theoretical analysis inspires a specialized study towards optimization community, as well as inspiring further research into algorithm design, training dynamics, and optimization for NNs, especially LLMs. The paper offers a potential direction for novel optimization techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous theoretical analysis:** The paper provides mathematically sound proofs using random matrix theory.
    *   **Clear separation of concerns:** Distinguishing between "static" and "dynamic" forces simplifies the problem.
    *   **Challenging established beliefs:** Questioning the role of CE loss demonstrates intellectual rigor.
    *   **Numerical validation:** The experiments support the theoretical findings, enhancing confidence in the results.
    *   **High potential for impact:**  Provides a solid foundation for further exploration.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Model limitations:** The analysis is primarily restricted to linear and one-hidden-layer networks. Generalizing the theory to deeper architectures is a challenge, but it is important for practical LLMs.
    *   **Initialization focus:** The primary theoretical analysis is limited to random initialization. Understanding the dynamic evolution of the Hessian during training (the "dynamic force") is a future direction requiring substantially different tools.
    *   **Complexity:** The use of random matrix theory makes the paper technically demanding, potentially limiting its accessibility to a broader audience. It is a double-edged sword, though, since it is this very complexity that makes it a significant mathematical contribution.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is a strong theoretical building block that shifts existing understanding. It has potential influence for those researching optimization and training of NNs, especially for LLMs. Its ideas are more fundamental and more insightful.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a significant contribution by providing a rigorous theoretical foundation for an empirically observed phenomenon. Its analysis challenges a longstanding belief in the field and opens up new avenues for research and optimization techniques. The primary weakness is the limited scope of the models considered. While this provides tractability for the random matrix theory analysis, extending these results to deeper, more complex architectures is essential. The techniques and tools might become very well known, although the result itself might be viewed as incremental. Taking all factors into account:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Database-Agnostic Gait Enrollment using SetTransformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02815v1)
- **Authors**: Nicoleta Basoc, Adrian Cosma, Andy Cǎtrunǎ, Emilian Rǎdoi
- **Abstract**: Gait recognition has emerged as a powerful tool for unobtrusive and long-range identity analysis, with growing relevance in surveillance and monitoring applications. Although recent advances in deep learning and large-scale datasets have enabled highly accurate recognition under closed-set conditions, real-world deployment demands open-set gait enrollment, which means determining whether a new gait sample corresponds to a known identity or represents a previously unseen individual. In this work, we introduce a transformer-based framework for open-set gait enrollment that is both dataset-agnostic and recognition-architecture-agnostic. Our method leverages a SetTransformer to make enrollment decisions based on the embedding of a probe sample and a context set drawn from the gallery, without requiring task-specific thresholds or retraining for new environments. By decoupling enrollment from the main recognition pipeline, our model is generalized across different datasets, gallery sizes, and identity distributions. We propose an evaluation protocol that uses existing datasets in different ratios of identities and walks per identity. We instantiate our method using skeleton-based gait representations and evaluate it on two benchmark datasets (CASIA-B and PsyMo), using embeddings from three state-of-the-art recognition models (GaitGraph, GaitFormer, and GaitPT). We show that our method is flexible, is able to accurately perform enrollment in different scenarios, and scales better with data compared to traditional approaches. We will make the code and dataset scenarios publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Database-Agnostic Gait Enrollment using SetTransformers":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of open-set gait enrollment, which is determining whether a new gait sample belongs to a known person (already in the gallery) or a new identity.  The authors propose a SetTransformer-based framework that makes enrollment decisions based on the embedding of a probe sample and a context set from the gallery.  Crucially, the method aims to be dataset-agnostic and recognition-architecture-agnostic.  This is achieved by decoupling the enrollment process from the main gait recognition pipeline and training the SetTransformer on various gallery-probe configurations.  The method is evaluated using skeleton-based gait representations and benchmark datasets (CASIA-B and PsyMo) with embeddings from state-of-the-art recognition models (GaitGraph, GaitFormer, GaitPT).  The results demonstrate the method's flexibility and accuracy across different scenarios and its ability to scale better compared to traditional threshold-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a moderately novel approach to open-set gait enrollment. The key novelty lies in its architecture-agnostic and dataset-agnostic training. Leveraging SetTransformers for enrollment is a reasonable application of an existing architecture, but the design choices for the inputs (k-nearest neighbors and identity embeddings) and how they're combined are fairly standard. The core contribution is the *training strategy* that emphasizes diversity in gallery/probe configurations to improve generalization. While other works have looked at open-set gait recognition, this work provides a valuable *systematic way* to train the enrollment.
* **Significance:** The problem addressed (open-set gait enrollment) is highly relevant to real-world deployment of gait recognition systems, a crucial point the authors effectively highlight. Most existing research focuses on closed-set scenarios, making this a useful step forward. The claimed ability to generalize across datasets and recognition models would be a substantial benefit to practical applications, reducing the need for task-specific retraining. However, the improvements demonstrated against traditional methods, while appreciable, are not revolutionary. The cross-dataset evaluation shows that there is much room for further improvement in the generalizability of the approach.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the open-set gait enrollment problem and its practical importance.
    * **Dataset-Agnostic Design:** The emphasis on dataset-agnostic training is a strong design choice, enhancing the potential for real-world applicability.
    * **Architecture Agnostic Implementation:** Decoupling from any particular architecture or gait modality means the model can be easily adopted into various other existing approaches.
    * **Thorough Evaluation:**  The evaluation protocol, varying gallery sizes and identity distributions, is commendable and simulates realistic scenarios better than many existing studies. The baseline comparison is useful.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:**  The SetTransformer architecture itself isn't new, and its adaptation to this specific task, while well-executed, isn't groundbreaking. The architecture choice is mostly justified through the problem.
    * **Limited Cross-Dataset Generalization:** The cross-dataset evaluation results (Table 4) indicate a significant drop in performance when transferring between CASIA-B and PsyMo, suggesting that the dataset-agnostic goal isn't fully achieved in practice, which undercuts one of the central claims.
    * **Baselines:** While the baselines are reasonable (Logistic Regression on simple features, etc.), they are not particularly sophisticated. Competing against more recent and advanced open-set recognition techniques would further strengthen the paper.
    * **No comparisons against the VAE reconstruction error detector, or the GAN approach** The authors discuss these methods from [17] and [28] but don't compare against them which is important for assessing the contribution.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging a greater focus on open-set gait recognition and by demonstrating the benefits of dataset-agnostic training strategies. The evaluation protocol is also a useful contribution that other researchers can adopt.
* **Justification for the Score:** The paper makes a solid contribution to a relevant problem, providing a practical architecture with a useful training paradigm. However, the limitations in cross-dataset generalization and incremental architectural novelty temper the impact. Therefore, a score reflecting these trade-offs is appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### ReplaceMe: Network Simplification via Layer Pruning and Linear Transformations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02819v1)
- **Authors**: Dmitriy Shopkhoev, Ammar Ali, Magauiya Zhussip, Valentin Malykh, Stamatios Lefkimmiatis, Nikos Komodakis, Sergey Zagoruyko
- **Abstract**: We introduce ReplaceMe, a generalized training-free depth pruning method that effectively replaces transformer blocks with a linear operation, while maintaining high performance for low compression ratios. In contrast to conventional pruning approaches that require additional training or fine-tuning, our approach requires only a small calibration dataset that is used to estimate a linear transformation to approximate the pruned blocks. This estimated linear mapping can be seamlessly merged with the remaining transformer blocks, eliminating the need for any additional network parameters. Our experiments show that ReplaceMe consistently outperforms other training-free approaches and remains highly competitive with state-of-the-art pruning methods that involve extensive retraining/fine-tuning and architectural modifications. Applied to several large language models (LLMs), ReplaceMe achieves up to 25% pruning while retaining approximately 90% of the original model's performance on open benchmarks - without any training or healing steps, resulting in minimal computational overhead (see Fig.1). We provide an open-source library implementing ReplaceMe alongside several state-of-the-art depth pruning techniques, available at this repository.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "ReplaceMe," a novel training-free depth pruning method for large language models (LLMs) and other transformer architectures. The key idea is to replace a contiguous sequence of transformer blocks with a single, estimated linear transformation (LT). This LT is learned using a small calibration dataset and then fused with the weight matrix of a preceding layer, avoiding any increase in the number of parameters. The authors demonstrate that ReplaceMe can achieve significant pruning (up to 25%) while maintaining a high percentage (around 90%) of the original model's performance on various benchmarks, without requiring any retraining or fine-tuning. They compare their method with existing pruning techniques, including those that use "healing" mechanisms, and show that ReplaceMe is competitive and often outperforms training-free methods. The paper includes detailed ablation studies examining the impact of calibration data characteristics, distance functions, regularization techniques, and different model architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The most significant novelty of ReplaceMe lies in its truly training-free nature. Many pruning methods, even those advertised as "training-free," still require a "healing" phase involving fine-tuning, which can be computationally expensive. By contrast, ReplaceMe eliminates this need, relying solely on a small calibration dataset for estimating the linear transformation.
*   **Weaknesses:** The idea of replacing multiple layers with a linear transformation isn't entirely new. Prior work, like LLM-Streamline, has explored similar concepts, but ReplaceMe distinguishes itself by offering a generalized training-free method and the effective incorporation of a linear transform.  UIDL also explores depth pruning, but ReplaceMe presents a method more effective to what layers to cut out, by measuring the cosine distances between outputs from layers, with an effective compensation of the errors introduced by such cuts with a matrix multiplication incorporated as a layer. Also, ReplaceMe claims a way to cut layers with lower computation overhead as well as higher accuracy than UIDL. So the methods presented are better but similar to current art.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The significance of ReplaceMe stems from its practical implications for deploying and using large language models, especially in resource-constrained environments. By reducing model size without sacrificing performance, ReplaceMe could make LLMs more accessible to a wider range of users. The detailed ablation studies provide valuable insights into the factors that influence pruning performance, which could guide future research in this area.
*   **Weaknesses:** The paper acknowledges that at high compression ratios, some form of "healing" may become necessary, suggesting a limitation in ReplaceMe's applicability. The performance retention drops to 90%, and it will not be applicable to those who prefer high performance (near 100%)
The paper also presents results that ReplaceMe is better than other models, but it does so with small margins.
The results of integrating a LT on the Multi-Layer Perceptron component shows only marginal gains.

**Overall Assessment:**

ReplaceMe is a significant contribution to the field of LLM pruning. Its training-free nature is a notable advantage, and the comprehensive experimental results support its effectiveness. While ReplaceMe may not be a perfect solution for all scenarios, it represents a valuable tool for simplifying LLMs and enabling their wider adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the combination of the training-free pruning of the transformer layers coupled with the compensation matrix multiplication, the rigorous experimentation and the results showcasing that the proposed method is better than state of the art, I would assign a score of 7.5.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Dataset Copyright Evasion Attack against Personalized Text-to-Image Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02824v1)
- **Authors**: Kuofeng Gao, Yufei Zhu, Yiming Li, Jiawang Bai, Yong Yang, Zhifeng Li, Shu-Tao Xia
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models have rapidly advanced, enabling high-quality image generation conditioned on textual prompts. However, the growing trend of fine-tuning pre-trained models for personalization raises serious concerns about unauthorized dataset usage. To combat this, dataset ownership verification (DOV) has emerged as a solution, embedding watermarks into the fine-tuning datasets using backdoor techniques. These watermarks remain inactive under benign samples but produce owner-specified outputs when triggered. Despite the promise of DOV for T2I diffusion models, its robustness against copyright evasion attacks (CEA) remains unexplored. In this paper, we explore how attackers can bypass these mechanisms through CEA, allowing models to circumvent watermarks even when trained on watermarked datasets. We propose the first copyright evasion attack (i.e., CEAT2I) specifically designed to undermine DOV in T2I diffusion models. Concretely, our CEAT2I comprises three stages: watermarked sample detection, trigger identification, and efficient watermark mitigation. A key insight driving our approach is that T2I models exhibit faster convergence on watermarked samples during the fine-tuning, evident through intermediate feature deviation. Leveraging this, CEAT2I can reliably detect the watermarked samples. Then, we iteratively ablate tokens from the prompts of detected watermarked samples and monitor shifts in intermediate features to pinpoint the exact trigger tokens. Finally, we adopt a closed-form concept erasure method to remove the injected watermark. Extensive experiments show that our CEAT2I effectively evades DOV mechanisms while preserving model performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Dataset Copyright Evasion Attack against Personalized Text-to-Image Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the emerging problem of unauthorized dataset usage in the context of personalized text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models.  It focuses on copyright evasion attacks (CEA) against dataset ownership verification (DOV) mechanisms that embed watermarks in datasets to detect unauthorized use.  The authors identify limitations in existing backdoor removal techniques (TPD and T2IShield) when applied to DOV in T2I models. To overcome these limitations, the authors propose a novel CEA called CEAT2I, which comprises three stages: watermarked sample detection (using feature convergence analysis), trigger identification (using token ablation), and efficient watermark mitigation (using closed-form concept erasure). Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of CEAT2I in evading DOV while maintaining model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and addressing the vulnerabilities of DOV mechanisms in T2I diffusion models *specifically* regarding copyright evasion. While backdoor removal techniques exist, their direct applicability and effectiveness against DOV in T2I models were not well-studied.  The core idea of using *feature convergence* to identify watermarked samples is novel and empirically supported. The combination of this detection method with trigger identification and closed-form mitigation forms a new attack pipeline, CEAT2I.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant practical implications.  As personalized T2I models become more prevalent, the need to protect datasets from unauthorized use is critical.  By demonstrating the feasibility of evading DOV, the paper highlights a serious security concern. This motivates the development of more robust DOV mechanisms and a deeper understanding of the vulnerabilities in current approaches.  The insights gained from this research can also inform the design of better defense strategies against model stealing and copyright infringement in generative AI. The well-structured experiments and publicly available code also enhance the paper's potential impact, allowing others to reproduce and extend the findings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Relevance:** The problem of dataset copyright protection in the age of generative AI is highly relevant and timely.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the threat model, attack goals, and limitations of existing defenses.
    *   **Effective Methodology:** The proposed CEAT2I method is well-designed, incorporating feature convergence analysis, token ablation, and closed-form mitigation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on multiple datasets and DOV methods, demonstrating the effectiveness of CEAT2I. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Open Source Code:** Releasing the code allows other researchers to reproduce, build upon, and validate the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation focuses primarily on Stable Diffusion and related DOV methods. It's unclear how well CEAT2I would generalize to other T2I architectures or different types of watermarking schemes.
    *   **Defenses:** While the paper analyzes existing *backdoor removal* defenses, it could be strengthened by including an assessment of countermeasures against CEAT2I itself. Adaptive defenses are briefly considered, but a deeper exploration of robust defensive strategies would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Computation Overhead:**  As the authors acknowledge, a notable limitation lies in the extra computational overhead involved in both watermarked samples detection and mitigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is highly likely to stimulate further research in robust DOV mechanisms for T2I models.  It will also contribute to a better understanding of the security risks associated with dataset reuse and model personalization.  It serves as a valuable case study for adversarial machine learning in generative AI.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant attack against an important emerging security challenge in generative AI. The approach is well-motivated, thoroughly evaluated, and publicly available. Although the scope is somewhat limited, and further research on defense strategies against CEAT2I is desirable, the paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and addressing a key vulnerability in current DOV techniques. The work will stimulate further research in robust DOV mechanisms for T2I models.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### No Other Representation Component Is Needed: Diffusion Transformers Can Provide Representation Guidance by Themselves
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02831v1)
- **Authors**: Dengyang Jiang, Mengmeng Wang, Liuzhuozheng Li, Lei Zhang, Haoyu Wang, Wei Wei, Guang Dai, Yanning Zhang, Jingdong Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have demonstrated that learning a meaningful internal representation can both accelerate generative training and enhance generation quality of the diffusion transformers. However, existing approaches necessitate to either introduce an additional and complex representation training framework or rely on a large-scale, pre-trained representation foundation model to provide representation guidance during the original generative training process. In this study, we posit that the unique discriminative process inherent to diffusion transformers enables them to offer such guidance without requiring external representation components. We therefore propose Self-Representation A}lignment (SRA), a simple yet straightforward method that obtain representation guidance through a self-distillation manner. Specifically, SRA aligns the output latent representation of the diffusion transformer in earlier layer with higher noise to that in later layer with lower noise to progressively enhance the overall representation learning during only generative training process. Experimental results indicate that applying SRA to DiTs and SiTs yields consistent performance improvements. Moreover, SRA not only significantly outperforms approaches relying on auxiliary, complex representation training frameworks but also achieves performance comparable to methods that heavily dependent on powerful external representation priors.
